BANANA
BANANA PRODUCTION NOTES
From Land Preparation and Planting to Maintenance and Harvesting
1. BANANA PRODUCTION OVERVIEW
* Banana (Musa spp.) is a major tropical fruit and food crop.
* Cardava, locally known as Saba, is a cooking-type banana important for food and rural livelihoods in the Philippines.
* Saba banana can reach approximately 4 meters in height.
* A bunch may have approximately 8–16 hands, with around 12–20 fingers per hand.
* Saba fruit can be used for different food products, including:
   * banana flour;
   * banana flakes;
   * banana ketchup;
   * banana wine;
   * banana vinegar; and
   * banana chips.
* The banana inflorescence may also be used as a vegetable.
* Banana leaves can be used as wrapping or decorative materials.
* The pseudostem can be used as livestock or poultry feed.
* Banana production requires appropriate:
   * soil condition;
   * moisture and water availability;
   * climate;
   * land preparation;
   * healthy planting materials;
   * nutrient management;
   * weed management;
   * pest and disease management; and
   * harvesting practices.
* The Cardava study identifies macropropagation as a low-cost approach for producing planting materials using locally available farm-waste substrates.
2. SITE AND CLIMATE REQUIREMENTS
2.1 Climate
Banana is suited to warm and moist growing conditions.
For Saba production, favorable conditions include approximately:
* 27–30°C temperature
* At least 60 inches of annual rainfall
* Adequate moisture throughout crop development
Important climate considerations include:
* Banana requires adequate moisture for growth.
* Rainfall should be sufficient and reasonably distributed.
* Extended dry conditions can affect plant growth and production.
* Long dry seasons may still be suitable when irrigation is available and economically feasible.
* Excessive water or prolonged flooding should be avoided because banana requires good drainage.
* Banana plants can be damaged by strong winds.
* Areas frequently exposed to strong winds and typhoons should be avoided when selecting a production site.
* Wind speeds of approximately 48–56 km/h can cause crown distortion, while stronger winds can cause serious plant blowdown.
* Areas exposed to strong winds should have appropriate protection such as windbreaks.
* Water availability should be considered before establishing the plantation because banana has a relatively high water requirement.
* Irrigation may be needed when rainfall is insufficient.
For game implementation
The climate system can therefore treat the following as important banana conditions:
Suitable conditions
* Temperature: 27–30°C
* Adequate rainfall
* Adequate moisture
* Good water availability
Risk conditions
* Excessive dryness
* Insufficient water
* Strong winds
* Typhoons
* Excessive water or flooding
3. SOIL REQUIREMENTS
Banana production requires soil capable of supporting strong root and plant development.
Preferred soil conditions
* Deep soil
* Fertile soil
* Moist soil
* Friable loam
* Well-drained soil
* Good soil aeration
Soil pH
The suitable soil pH range is approximately:
pH 4.5–7.5
Soil limitations
Avoid:
* extremely sandy soils;
* very rocky soils; and
* areas with poor drainage.
Good drainage is particularly important.
The production area should therefore avoid prolonged waterlogging because excessive standing water can negatively affect the growing environment and root development.
4. SITE SELECTION
Before establishing banana plants, select an area with:
* suitable soil;
* sufficient water;
* good drainage;
* adequate sunlight;
* suitable terrain; and
* protection from excessive wind.
Terrain
Commercial Saba plantations are ideally established on flat terrain.
Hilly or rolling areas may also be used when appropriate contour-based farming practices are applied.
Areas frequently exposed to:
* strong winds;
* typhoons; and
* severe wind damage
should preferably be avoided.
5. LAND PREPARATION
Before planting banana, the farm should be properly prepared.
Step 1 — Clear the area
* Clear unnecessary vegetation.
* Remove existing weeds.
* Remove materials that may interfere with planting.
* Prepare the area for cultivation.
Step 2 — Plow the soil
Where appropriate, the land should be plowed approximately 2–3 times.
Repeated plowing is particularly useful where another crop was previously grown.
Step 3 — Harrow the field
After plowing:
* Harrow the soil.
* Break soil clods.
* Improve soil tilth.
* Prepare a suitable planting surface.
Step 4 — Establish drainage
Drainage should be established before planting, particularly in areas susceptible to excessive water.
The objective is to:
* prevent prolonged waterlogging;
* maintain suitable soil moisture;
* protect the roots;
* reduce conditions favorable to some diseases; and
* allow plants to develop properly.
Step 5 — Prepare planting holes
For Saba banana:
* Hole diameter: approximately 50–60 cm
* Hole depth: approximately 60–80 cm
The exact size may depend on the size of the planting material.
Step 6 — Consider planting direction
The DA production guide recommends an east–west orientation for planting rows so that plants receive more evenly distributed sunlight.
6. PLANTING MATERIALS
Healthy planting material is important for successful banana production.
The production guide recommends selecting healthy and vigorous planting materials.
Traditional propagation
Traditional Cardava propagation may use suckers obtained from healthy mother plants.
The Cardava study used sword suckers approximately 20 cm in height.
The Saba guide recommends planting suckers approximately 3–4 feet tall.
Suitable suckers should:
* come from healthy and vigorous clumps;
* have many healthy roots;
* be free from visible disease;
* show no signs of nematode damage; and
* show no signs of borer damage.
Planting materials should be inspected before planting to avoid introducing diseases and pests into the production area.
7. MACROPROPAGATION OF CARDAVA
The Cardava study provides a propagation approach that can be incorporated into production.
Macropropagation
Macropropagation uses:
* whole banana corms; or
* large sections of corms.
The purpose is to stimulate latent buds and produce additional planting materials.
Basic procedure reported in the study
1. Select suitable corm material.
2. Use sterilized knives.
3. Perform decortication and decapitation of the corm to expose latent buds.
4. Disinfect the corm.
5. Plant the prepared corm material into the propagation medium.
6. Maintain adequate moisture through regular irrigation.
7. Monitor shoot emergence and early growth.
8. Allow healthy shoots to develop before transplanting.
The study used alcohol and fungicide as part of the corm disinfection procedure.
8. PROPAGATION MEDIA
The Cardava study tested five propagation media:
1. Soil only — control
2. Soil + coconut husk
3. Soil + compost
4. Soil + mung bean pod hull
5. Soil + rice hull
Best-performing medium
Soil + mung bean pod hull produced the fastest shoot emergence and greatest early plant growth.
Compost and coconut husk also performed well.
Soil + rice hull showed the poorest early performance among the tested media.
Shoot emergence
The Cardava study reported approximately:
* 10 days for shoot emergence using soil + mung bean pod hull under macropropagation.
This makes soil + mung bean pod hull a useful propagation option for the Cardava production mechanic.
9. PLANTING
After preparing the land and planting materials:
1. Prepare the planting hole.
2. Ensure that the soil is suitable.
3. Check drainage.
4. Place one healthy sucker vertically in the hole.
5. Fill the hole with appropriate surface soil.
6. Press the soil around the sucker to remove air spaces.
7. Maintain adequate moisture.
8. Monitor the newly planted banana regularly.
Planting distance
The Saba production guide recommends a general spacing of approximately:
4–7 meters
A commonly used arrangement is:
4 m × 4 m
This can require approximately:
625 suckers per hectare
Spacing can depend on the intended production and marketing system.
Closer spacing may be desirable when fruit is sold by weight, while wider spacing may produce larger bunches when fruit is sold according to finger count.
Planting time
Bananas can generally be planted throughout the year.
However, in areas with distinct wet and dry seasons, planting should preferably begin at the start of the rainy season.
10. WATER AND IRRIGATION
Water must be maintained throughout banana production.
Important practices
* Newly planted banana requires adequate moisture.
* Irrigation should be provided when rainfall is insufficient.
* Avoid allowing the soil to become excessively dry.
* At the same time, prevent prolonged waterlogging.
* Newly established plants require particular attention to moisture.
* Drainage should be maintained in areas susceptible to excessive water.
* Long dry periods may be managed through irrigation when economically feasible.
In the Cardava propagation experiment, regular irrigation was applied to maintain adequate moisture during propagation.
Game condition
Adequate water → Healthy growth
Insufficient water → Reduced growth / crop stress
Excessive water → Poor growing conditions / increased production risk
11. FERTILIZATION AND NUTRIENT MANAGEMENT
Banana requires sufficient nutrients for continuous growth and production.
The DA manual recommends that fertilizer management preferably be based on:
* soil analysis, and/or
* leaf tissue analysis.
When these analyses are unavailable, the general fertilizer recommendation is:
90-30-120 NPK
Specific Saba fertilizer program
For the first year, the Saba guide provides a fertilizer program of approximately:
* 3.5 bags complete fertilizer (14-14-14) per hectare
* 3 bags urea (45-0-0) per hectare
* 10 bags muriate of potash (0-0-60) per hectare
The guide indicates an application rate of approximately:
440 g per hill every 4 months
The fertilizer rate is stated to be doubled in succeeding years.
Important application considerations
* Apply fertilizer when adequate soil moisture is available.
* Provide nutrients during plant growth.
* Maintain appropriate soil fertility.
* Integrate fertilization with water and soil management.
12. WEED MANAGEMENT
Weeds compete with banana plants for:
* water;
* nutrients; and
* growing space.
Therefore, weed control should be performed regularly, especially during early plant establishment.
Weed management practices
Methods include:
* manual weeding;
* ring weeding around plants;
* line weeding;
* slashing; and
* appropriate weed-control measures.
When working close to banana plants, care should be taken to avoid damaging the roots or plant.
Root protection
Banana has a relatively shallow root system, with much of the root system occurring within the top six inches of soil.
Therefore:
* avoid deep cultivation near banana plants;
* cultivation should not exceed approximately six inches; and
* care should be taken not to damage the roots.
Mulching
Mulching can be used for:
* conserving soil moisture;
* suppressing weeds; and
* maintaining better soil conditions.
13. DESUCKERING / SUCKER MANAGEMENT
Banana plants naturally produce suckers.
However, excessive suckers can compete with the mother plant for:
* water;
* nutrients; and
* growing space.
Management
* Regularly inspect sucker growth.
* Remove unnecessary suckers.
* Maintain appropriate suckers for continued production.
* Avoid excessive competition around the mother plant.
For Saba production, the guide recommends maintaining approximately two suckers per hill/clump.
The guide also recommends retaining suckers that are deeply embedded in the soil.
Proper sucker management helps maintain productive plants.
14. LEAF AND PLANT MAINTENANCE
Regular sanitation and plant maintenance are important.
Remove:
* old leaves;
* dried leaves;
* diseased leaves;
* nonfunctional leaves;
* unnecessary leaf sheaths; and
* old bracts when appropriate.
The DA guide recommends maintaining approximately 8–15 functional leaves.
Purpose
Proper leaf management helps:
* maintain productive foliage;
* improve plant sanitation;
* remove disease-affected material; and
* maintain healthy crop development.
15. WIND PROTECTION
Banana plants are vulnerable to strong winds.
Strong winds can:
* damage leaves;
* distort the crown;
* bend or break plants;
* damage the pseudostem; and
* cause plant blowdown.
Wind speeds of approximately 48–56 km/h can cause crown distortion, while stronger winds can result in serious blowdown.
Management
In locations frequently affected by strong winds:
Establish windbreaks.
However, areas frequently exposed to severe winds and typhoons should preferably be avoided during site selection.
16. PEST MANAGEMENT
The banana production guide identifies several important banana pests.
Major pests include:
* Banana aphids
* Thrips
* Nematodes
* Mealybugs
* Corm weevil
* Fruit peel scarring beetle
* Floral thrips
General management
Pest management should include:
1. Regular crop inspection.
2. Early identification of pest problems.
3. Removal of affected plant material when appropriate.
4. Maintaining farm sanitation.
5. Using appropriate pest-control measures.
6. Continued monitoring after treatment.
Corm weevil management
After harvesting:
* Chop the pseudostem.
* Scatter the pieces so they dry and rot quickly.
* Cut the pseudostem close to the ground.
* Cover the cut corm base with soil.
Freshly cut pseudostem pieces can also be used as traps.
The pieces may be inspected after approximately 2–3 days, and trapped beetles can be collected.
Fruit peel scarring beetle
This pest damages the surface of banana fruit.
Management includes:
* proper bunch protection;
* sanitation; and
* appropriate pest-control practices.
Floral thrips
Floral thrips can affect banana inflorescences and developing fruit.
The guide provides management practices involving appropriate treatments and protection of the inflorescence.
Game mechanic
No pest detected → Continue maintenance
Pest detected → Apply appropriate control
Uncontrolled pest → Reduced plant health / production
17. DISEASE MANAGEMENT
The DA guide identifies several diseases affecting banana.
Important examples include:
* Banana bunchy top virus
* Banana bract mosaic
* Banana mosaic
* Banana streak virus
* Sigatoka
* Black leaf streak
* Fusarium wilt/Panama disease
* Moko/bacterial wilt
* Bugtok/Tibagnol
* Cigar-end rot
* Anthracnose
* Crown rot
* Dry rot
General disease prevention
Disease management should focus on:
* healthy planting materials;
* farm sanitation;
* early detection;
* removal of infected plants when necessary;
* appropriate drainage;
* quarantine measures; and
* appropriate disease-control practices.
The Cardava study also emphasizes sanitation during propagation. In its macropropagation procedure, corms were disinfected with alcohol and fungicide before planting.
18. SIGATOKA AND BLACK LEAF STREAK
Sigatoka and black leaf streak can affect banana leaves and reduce plant productivity.
Management combines:
* chemical control;
* sanitation;
* cultural practices;
* appropriate plant population/density;
* good drainage;
* removal of sources of infection; and
* proper plant nourishment.
Regular inspection is important so that symptoms can be identified early.
19. FUSARIUM WILT / PANAMA DISEASE
Fusarium wilt is an important banana disease.
Important preventive and management measures include:
* using healthy planting materials;
* maintaining farm sanitation;
* removing infected plants appropriately;
* maintaining proper drainage;
* using disease-free planting materials; and
* applying quarantine measures.
For a simulation game, Fusarium wilt can be represented as a serious crop-health event requiring early detection and management.
20. BUGTOK / TIBAGNOL
Bugtok/Tibagnol is an important disease for Saba production.
Management should involve:
* sanitation;
* regular monitoring;
* removal of affected materials;
* use of healthy planting materials;
* removal of the inflorescence when false fingers appear; and
* fruit wrapping when appropriate.
For Cardava/Saba production, Bugtok should be included as one of the possible crop-health problems.
21. MOSAIC AND BUNCHY TOP
Viral diseases such as mosaic and bunchy top require early detection and control.
Management includes:
* immediate removal of infected plants;
* destruction of infected plant materials;
* control of aphids and other vectors where applicable;
* use of virus-free planting materials; and
* maintaining farm sanitation.
22. GROWTH MONITORING
Banana plants should be monitored throughout production.
Important indicators include:
* plant height;
* leaf condition;
* number of functional leaves;
* sucker development;
* soil moisture;
* pest presence;
* disease symptoms; and
* bunch development.
The Cardava study measured plant height at:
* 6 months
* 12 months
* 18 months
after transplanting during its field evaluation.
At 18 months:
* Macropropagated plants reached approximately 210 cm average height.
* Traditionally propagated plants reached approximately 190 cm average height.
The study also reported approximately:
* 25 kg/plant yield for macropropagated plants.
* 18 kg/plant yield for traditionally propagated plants.
* A reported yield advantage of approximately 7.10 kg/plant for macropropagation.
23. FLOWERING AND BUNCH DEVELOPMENT
As the banana plant develops, the production cycle progresses toward bunch formation.
During this stage, the farmer should continue:
* adequate water management;
* nutrient management;
* weed control;
* sucker management;
* leaf sanitation;
* pest monitoring;
* disease monitoring; and
* protection from environmental damage.
The plant should be kept healthy until the bunch reaches the appropriate maturity stage.
24. HARVEST MATURITY
Harvesting should be based on the maturity of the banana.
The DA guide provides different maturity periods depending on the variety.
Variety
	Approximate maturity after shooting
	Amas
	6–8 weeks
	Señorita
	6–8 weeks
	Latundan
	9–11 weeks
	Lakatan
	12–14 weeks
	Bungulan
	12–14 weeks
	Cavendish
	13–15 weeks
	Saba/Cardava
	20–24 weeks
	For Cardava/Saba, the maturity period is therefore longer than that of several other varieties listed in the guide.
Additional maturity indicators for Saba
Maturity can also be assessed through:
* the first leaf turning yellow; and
* the shape and angle of the fingers.
More rounded fingers indicate greater maturity.
Saba may be harvested 5–10 days before ripening when the bananas are intended for immediate shipment while still green.
25. CARDAVA HARVESTING
For Cardava intended for appropriate processing or marketing purposes, harvesting should consider the required maturity and fruit characteristics.
The fruit should not be harvested too early because immature fruit has not developed adequately.
For green-market purposes, Saba can be harvested several days before ripening.
The harvest stage should therefore be selected according to the intended:
* market;
* transport;
* processing; or
* consumption purpose.
26. HARVESTING PROCEDURE
The DA manual describes manual harvesting as a process requiring two persons.
Step 1 — Prepare for harvesting
* Inspect the bunch.
* Confirm that it has reached the appropriate maturity.
* Prepare the harvesting area and transport equipment.
* Work carefully to prevent bunch damage.
Step 2 — Prepare the plant
Leaves may need to be removed carefully to provide access to the bunch.
Avoid unnecessary damage to neighboring plants.
Step 3 — Support the bunch
The bunch should be supported before it is completely separated from the plant.
The helper/backer supports the bunch while the cutter performs the cutting.
Step 4 — Cut the pseudostem
The cutter makes a controlled cut or nick in the pseudostem so that the bunch lowers gently.
Step 5 — Remove the bunch
Once the bunch has been properly supported:
* cut the peduncle/bunch stalk;
* carefully separate the bunch; and
* avoid dropping the fruit.
Step 6 — Transport
Carry the bunches carefully to the designated collection, processing, or packing area.
Do not throw or drop the bunches.
27. POST-HARVEST HANDLING
After harvest:
* Handle bunches carefully.
* Avoid dropping or throwing bunches.
* Avoid bruising the fruit.
* Avoid placing bunches directly on surfaces where they may be damaged.
* Move harvested bunches to a suitable holding or packing area.
* Keep harvested bananas protected from excessive heat and direct sunlight.
* Continue appropriate sanitation and handling practices.
* Prepare bananas for their intended market or processing use.

	31. KEY POINTS FOR AGRIDABAW-3D
For the banana crop simulation, the player should be able to:
* Select a suitable farm location.
* Check temperature suitability.
* Check rainfall and water availability.
* Check soil suitability.
* Check soil pH.
* Check drainage.
* Prepare the land.
* Plow and harrow the field.
* Prepare planting holes.
* Select healthy planting materials.
* Select a propagation method.
* Perform Cardava macropropagation.
* Select a propagation medium.
* Plant banana at an appropriate distance.
* Irrigate the crop.
* Apply fertilizer.
* Control weeds.
* Protect shallow roots.
* Manage suckers.
* Maintain functional leaves.
* Maintain farm sanitation.
* Monitor pests.
* Detect pest infestations.
* Manage corm weevil.
* Detect diseases.
* Manage Sigatoka/black leaf streak.
* Detect and manage Fusarium wilt.
* Detect and manage Bugtok.
* Detect viral diseases such as mosaic and bunchy top.
* Protect plants from strong winds.
* Monitor crop growth.
* Monitor bunch development.
* Determine harvest maturity.
* Harvest the bunch using proper handling.
* Transport harvested bananas carefully.
* Process or sell the harvested banana.
Banana production requires appropriate planting materials, suitable farm conditions, proper propagation, field establishment, and continuous crop management to maintain plant growth and productivity (Department of Agriculture–High Value Crops Development Program, n.d.). Proper propagation is particularly important because the method used to produce planting materials can influence crop establishment and subsequent production. Recent research on Cardava banana found that macropropagated plants had faster shoot emergence and greater early growth than traditionally propagated plants, while plants produced through macropropagation also showed significantly higher field yields. The study further found that farm-waste-based substrates, particularly mung bean pod hulls, compost, and coconut husk, can support macropropagation, demonstrating the potential of locally available materials for sustainable banana production. These findings support the inclusion of quality planting materials, propagation method, substrate or growing medium, establishment, crop management, and yield as important considerations in banana production documentation and in the AgriDabaw-3D simulation (Paderes & Batoon, 2026).

CACAO
CACAO PRODUCTION NOTES
From Site Selection, Nursery Establishment, Planting, Maintenance, Pest and Disease Management, Harvesting, Fermentation, Drying, and Marketing
1. CACAO PRODUCTION OVERVIEW
* Cacao is scientifically known as Theobroma cacao.
* It is an economically important crop and the primary agricultural raw material used in chocolate production.
* Cacao is a tropical crop that requires appropriate temperature, rainfall, soil, shade, and farm management.
* Cacao flowers are produced directly on the trunk and older branches.
* The flowers are pollinated by tiny flies, particularly Forcipomyia midges.
* The fruit is called a cacao pod.
* A mature pod is approximately 15–30 cm long, 8–10 cm wide, and may weigh around 500 g.
* A pod may contain approximately 20–60 seeds, commonly called cacao beans, embedded in white pulp.
* Cacao production in the Philippines has particular importance in Mindanao, especially the Davao Region.
* The Philippine Cacao Industry Roadmap identifies the need to improve productivity, quality planting materials, production practices, postharvest handling, market access, and value addition.
* In the 2026 Aleosan, Cotabato study, cacao farmers identified lack of access to experts, price fluctuation, disease outbreaks, and lack of market outlets as major challenges.
2. IMPORTANCE OF CACAO IN THE PHILIPPINES
The Philippine Cacao Industry Roadmap reports that cacao has been cultivated in the Philippines for centuries, with commercial farms established in the 1950s.
The industry experienced production declines because of:
* typhoons;
* pests;
* diseases;
* aging trees;
* possible genetic deterioration of commonly used varieties;
* declining world market prices; and
* competition from other plantation crops such as banana and palm oil.
The Roadmap reports that the Philippines had approximately 31,285 hectares planted with cacao and approximately 9,341 metric tons of production in 2020. It also identifies insufficient quality planting materials and limited farmer training in good agricultural practices as factors contributing to low productivity.
For Davao specifically, the Roadmap reports that Davao City produced 2,508.98 metric tons in 2020, representing approximately 27% of the Davao Region's cacao production in that year.
3. SITE SELECTION
Before establishing a cacao farm, the production area should be assessed for:
* climate;
* temperature;
* rainfall;
* soil;
* drainage;
* elevation;
* available water;
* existing shade;
* accessibility; and
* suitability for long-term cacao production.
The Roadmap emphasizes the importance of expanding cacao production in areas identified as suitable for cacao cultivation.
Suitable site characteristics
A suitable cacao site should have:
* adequate rainfall;
* suitable temperature;
* fertile and sufficiently deep soil;
* adequate drainage;
* suitable shade conditions;
* manageable terrain; and
* access to farm and market support.
4. SOIL REQUIREMENTS
The Bureau of Plant Industry guide identifies cacao as favoring deep soil with suitable texture and fertility.
Soil composition
The production guide describes suitable soil as approximately:
* 50% loamy soil/sand
* 30–40% clay
* 10–20% silt
The guide identifies deep soil as favorable for cacao growth.
Soil pH
Recommended soil pH:
5.0–6.5
Good soil conditions support:
* root development;
* vegetative growth;
* flowering;
* pod development; and
* nutrient availability.
5. CLIMATE REQUIREMENTS
Cacao requires a warm tropical environment.
The production guide identifies:
* Mean maximum temperature: 30–32°C
* Mean minimum temperature: approximately 18°C
* Ideal annual rainfall: 1,250–3,000 mm
* Preferred rainfall: approximately 1,500–2,000 mm
* Dry season: preferably not more than 3 months
* Suitable altitude: approximately 300–1,200 meters above sea level
* Suitable temperature is generally found at elevations up to approximately 700 m.
* Cacao thrives particularly well under Type IV climate, characterized by relatively evenly distributed rainfall throughout the year.
For game implementation
Suitable conditions
* Warm temperature
* Adequate rainfall
* Short or manageable dry season
* Adequate soil moisture
* Suitable elevation
Risk conditions
* Prolonged drought
* Excessive rainfall
* Poor drainage
* Strong weather events
* Unsuitable temperature
6. SHADE REQUIREMENTS
Shade management is one of the most important parts of young cacao establishment.
The production guide indicates:
First year
Newly planted cacao trees require approximately:
75% shade
This corresponds to approximately:
25% direct sunlight
Second year
Shade can be reduced to approximately:
50% shade
Mature pod-bearing trees
After establishment, shade can be reduced to approximately:
25% shade
The objective is to gradually acclimatize cacao plants while avoiding excessive exposure during early development.
Suitable shade crops
The guide identifies permanent shade/intercrop options such as:
* coconut;
* cashew;
* longan;
* durian;
* mango; and
* mangosteen.
Cacao and shade trees may be planted at approximately:
6 m × 3 m
Temporary shade structures may also be made using locally available materials such as:
* palm fronds; and
* sugarcane leaves.
Important management principle
Too much shade can increase humidity and create favorable conditions for some pests and diseases.
Therefore, shade must be managed gradually rather than simply maximized.
7. PLANTING MATERIALS
High-quality planting materials are essential for establishing productive cacao farms.
The production guide recommends planting materials that are:
* healthy;
* vigorous;
* uniform;
* free from pests;
* free from diseases; and
* sourced from recommended clones or cultivars.
The Roadmap specifically identifies the availability of quality planting materials as an important industry concern.
8. CACAO VARIETIES AND CLONES
The Bureau of Plant Industry guide identifies several National Seed Industry Council-approved cacao varieties/clones, including:
* BR 25 (CC-99-05)
* K 1
* K 2
* UIT 1 (CC-99-02)
* ICS 40
* UF 18
* S 5
* K 4
The guide provides differences among clones in:
* flowering age;
* fruiting age;
* pod characteristics;
* pod color;
* pod size;
* bean characteristics;
* pod index; and
* pest and disease resistance.
Example
BR 25
* First flowering: approximately 16.12 months
* Fruiting follows at approximately 17.70 months
* Young pods are reddish/red-green and turn yellow at maturity.
K 1
* Flowers at approximately 23.20 months
* Fruits at approximately 25.10 months
* Moderately resistant to known insect pests and diseases.
K 2
* Flowers at approximately 21.10 months
* Fruiting approximately 24.12 months
* Young pods are red and turn yellow/orange when mature.
* Approximately 34 beans per pod.
ICS 40
* Flowers at approximately 17.63 months
* Fruiting approximately 19.63 months
* Approximately 44 beans per pod.
These differences can be useful in a simulation because different cacao varieties can have different:
* growth periods;
* flowering periods;
* fruiting periods;
* pod characteristics; and
* resistance characteristics.
9. PROPAGATION BY SEED
Seed propagation involves producing planting materials from seeds obtained from healthy cacao pods.
Seed selection
Select seeds from:
* ripe pods;
* healthy pods;
* preferably a seed garden;
* uniform-sized seeds; and
* larger seeds where possible.
Larger seeds are considered more likely to produce vigorous and fast-growing seedlings.
Seed germination
The production guide recommends:
1. Remove the mucilage surrounding the seeds.
2. Rub seeds with sawdust or sand to loosen the mucilage.
3. Wash the seeds.
4. Drain the water.
5. Keep seeds in a moist and well-ventilated location for pre-germination.
Pre-germinated seeds
Seeds showing signs of germination should be collected approximately two days after preparation.
Plant the pre-germinated seed:
* approximately 1 cm deep;
* in prepared nursery polybags;
* as soon as the germ is approximately 1 cm long.
Delaying planting may damage the developing root or shoot.
10. VEGETATIVE PROPAGATION
Vegetative propagation can be used when maintaining desirable characteristics of selected cacao plants.
Methods identified in the guide include:
* patch budding;
* community nodal grafting;
* conventional grafting; and
* side grafting.
11. NURSERY MANAGEMENT
The nursery should produce strong and healthy seedlings before field establishment.
Important nursery practices include:
* selecting healthy seeds;
* proper germination;
* maintaining moisture;
* providing appropriate shade;
* preventing pest and disease entry;
* selecting vigorous seedlings; and
* removing weak or abnormal seedlings.
Culling
Poor-growing seedlings should be removed.
Examples include:
* bags containing seeds that failed to germinate;
* very small seedlings; and
* crinkled or poorly developed seedlings.
12. NURSERY FERTILIZATION
Fertilization should preferably be based on soil analysis.
When analysis is unavailable, the production guide recommends approximately:
15–35 g ammonium phosphate (18-48-0) per polybag
depending on polybag size.
Fertilizer should be applied:
* after the first leaf hardens; and
* when leaves are dry to prevent leaf scorching.
13. TRANSPLANTING
Transplanting should be performed carefully to reduce seedling shock.
Before transplanting
The guide recommends rotating the polybag slightly approximately one week before field planting, particularly when roots have penetrated into the ground.
Timing
Field planting should begin at the:
onset of the rainy season
Unless irrigation is available, planting during the dry season is not advisable.
14. FIELD PLANTING
Planting time
The best season for field planting is the beginning/onset of the rainy season.
The guide recommends planting during:
* early morning; or
* late afternoon.
Avoid planting seedlings with young, soft flush leaves because they are more susceptible to:
* sunburn;
* planting shock; and
* stress.
Planting hole
A normal planting hole is approximately:
30 cm × 30 cm × 30 cm
The hole should be large enough to accommodate the soil ball around the seedling.
During holing:
* separate surface soil from subsoil;
* avoid unnecessary mixing; and
* return the appropriate soil around the seedling.
15. CACAO SPACING
The production guide identifies several planting densities.
High-density planting
Approximately:
1.5–2.0 m × 6.0 m
or about:
2,300 trees/ha
Low-density planting
Approximately:
3 m × 2 m
or about:
1,666 plants/ha
Another low-density arrangement:
2.5 m × 2.5 m
or approximately:
1,600 plants/ha
Depending on shade, existing tree crops, and soil fertility, cacao density may range from approximately:
400–1,100 plants/ha
For cacao intercropped with coconut or cashew, average density is approximately:
600 plants/ha.
16. STAKING AND FIELD LAYOUT
Before planting:
1. Mark planting points.
2. Use stakes to maintain planting alignment.
3. Use a cable, wire, or guide for straight-line planting.
4. Maintain the selected spacing.
5. Consider existing shade trees and other crops.
Correct spacing helps manage:
* sunlight;
* shade;
* airflow;
* tree growth;
* farm maintenance; and
* harvesting access.
17. WEED MANAGEMENT
Weeds compete with cacao plants for:
* water;
* nutrients;
* sunlight; and
* growing space.
The guide recommends manual weed management.
Ring weeding
Remove weeds around the cacao tree using approximately:
1-meter radius from the stem
A sickle or similar manual tool may be used.
Between blocks
Weeds between planting blocks can be controlled by cutting them with a scythe.
The production guide states that herbicide use is not recommended in this context.
Mulching with available materials such as rice hull may also be used.
18. FERTILIZATION AND NUTRIENT MANAGEMENT
Cacao requires 16 essential elements to complete its life cycle.
The guide identifies important nutrients including:
* Nitrogen (N)
* Phosphorus (P)
* Potassium (K)
* Calcium (Ca)
* Magnesium (Mg)
Deficiency of essential nutrients can reduce:
* plant growth; and
* production.
Nutrient functions
Nutrient
	Main function / important period
	Nitrogen (N)
	Vegetative growth; important at onset of rainy season and approximately three months later
	Phosphorus (P)
	Supports flowering; important at onset of rainy season
	Potassium (K)
	Supports flower set and fruit development; important before flowering and approximately three months later
	Calcium (Ca)
	Helps lengthen leaf life
	Magnesium (Mg)
	Helps lengthen leaf life
	Organic fertilizer should be applied regularly around the base of the plant, particularly during the wet season, to support vegetative and reproductive growth.
Basal fertilizer
Basal fertilizer is particularly important during the establishment stage of young cacao trees.
19. FERTILIZER APPLICATION SCHEDULE
Where soil analysis is unavailable, the production guide provides the following example rates:
Months after field planting
	N (g/plant)
	P (g/plant)
	K (g/plant)
	1 month
	6.4
	6.4
	6.4
	4 months
	8.5
	8.5
	8.5
	8 months
	8.5
	8.5
	8.5
	12 months
	12.8
	12.8
	12.8
	18 months
	17.0
	17.0
	17.0
	24 months
	27.0
	27.3
	38.5
	Total
	80.5
	80.5
	91.7
	These values are presented in the guide as recommended rates in the absence of soil analysis.
20. SHADE AND NUTRIENT MANAGEMENT
Shade affects cacao's nutrient requirements.
The production guide states that appropriate shade can reduce nutrient requirements because reduced light intensity lowers plant metabolism.
However, shade must be managed carefully because excessive shade can increase humidity and contribute to pest and disease problems.
Therefore, the farm should balance:
Shade → Moisture protection and reduced stress
with
Controlled sunlight → Better airflow, lower humidity, and improved flowering/management
21. PRUNING
Pruning is an important cacao management practice.
Main objectives
Pruning helps to:
1. Increase cacao pod production.
2. Reduce pest and disease infestation.
3. Control tree shape.
4. Control tree height.
5. Make harvesting easier.
6. Improve farm maintenance.
7. Improve sunlight penetration.
8. Reduce excessive humidity.
22. PRUNING YOUNG CACAO TREES
For trees younger than approximately 3 years:
* Remove weak fan branches.
* Retain approximately 3–4 equally spaced fan branches after formation of the jorquette.
* Remove chupons or side shoots growing from the base of the stem.
23. MAINTENANCE PRUNING
Regularly remove:
* low-hanging branches;
* downward-growing branches;
* crossing branches;
* curved branches;
* irregularly growing branches;
* dead branches;
* diseased branches;
* badly damaged branches;
* unnecessary shoots; and
* chupons.
Shoots and additional branches within approximately 60 cm of the jorquette should also be removed.
Top pruning
Highest branches can be pruned to approximately 4 meters to keep the tree manageable.
Center opening
The center of the tree can be opened through a champagne-glass-shaped pruning structure.
This helps:
* reduce humidity;
* increase sunlight;
* improve accessibility; and
* support flowering.
The guide indicates that heavy pruning is best performed after the high-production cycle, approximately one month before the rainy season. Fertilizer application is recommended after pruning.
24. REHABILITATION OF OLD OR UNPRODUCTIVE CACAO TREES
Cacao farms may require rehabilitation when:
* trees are old;
* trees are unproductive;
* trees produce few pods despite flowering;
* trees produce limited flowers;
* existing varieties are low-yielding; or
* bean quality is poor.
Side grafting
Side grafting can be used to renew less productive cacao trees.
The scion or budwood should come from selected trees with:
* high productivity; and
* tolerance/resistance to major pests and diseases.
A tree can have up to three side grafts, with approximately one month between grafting operations.
25. SIDE GRAFTING PROCEDURE
The guide describes the following basic procedure:
1. Identify an old or less productive but healthy tree.
2. Gather bud sticks from a budwood garden.
3. Prepare the bud sticks.
4. Make a horizontal deep cut on the main trunk.
5. Shave the bark downward.
6. Ensure the cut reaches the white wood.
7. Make two downward cuts.
8. Create a window by peeling the bark.
9. Expose the cambium.
10. Insert the bud stick.
11. Tie the window closed.
12. Secure the graft.
13. Cover the graft with a plastic bag.
14. Tie the plastic securely using raffia.
15. Remove the plastic cover after approximately one month.
16. Maintain the grafted branch until it becomes established.
26. CACAO POD BORER
One of the major cacao pests is the Cacao Pod Borer (CPB).
The guide identifies the scientific name as Conopomorpha species.
Management
Important practices include:
* frequent harvesting;
* sanitation;
* burying empty pod husks;
* removing diseased pods;
* removing black pods;
* removing pods damaged by animals;
* pruning;
* increasing sunlight;
* bagging or sleeving young pods;
* maintaining tree nutrition.
Harvesting frequency
The guide specifically recommends regular/weekly harvesting of ripe pods as a way of breaking the pest's life cycle.
27. VASCULAR STREAK DIEBACK
Vascular Streak Dieback (VSD) is caused by Oncobasidium theobromae.
Management
* Conduct sanitation pruning.
* Cut infected branches approximately 30 cm below the infected area.
* Burn infected cuttings.
* Use polyethylene roofing in nurseries to reduce spore deposition on seedlings.
* Reduce excessive shade to lower humidity.
* Plant VSD-tolerant varieties where appropriate.
28. BLACK POD ROT AND CANKER
The guide identifies Black Pod Rot and Canker, associated with Phytophthora palmivora.
Management
* Harvest frequently.
* Remove infected pods.
* Remove dead pods.
* Remove mummified pods.
* Destroy or bury affected pods.
* Prune cacao trees.
* Prune shade trees when necessary.
* Reduce excessive humidity.
* Maintain good drainage.
* Remove and destroy trees that have died from canker.
* Treat affected bark according to the guide.
29. HELOPELTIS
Helopeltis is a sap-sucking pest.
Management includes:
* farm sanitation;
* regular harvesting;
* careful pruning;
* appropriate shade management;
* monitoring affected plants.
Excessive pruning should be avoided because new chupons may develop and become feeding sites for Helopeltis.
30. STEM BORER
Stem borer is associated with Zeuzera.
Management
* Cut infested branches approximately 40 cm below the lowest larval hole.
* Destroy infested branches.
* Burn large branches with stem-borer holes when appropriate.
* Plug holes with mud or wood.
* Larvae may be physically removed using a wire.
* The guide also describes the use of soap solution in the exit hole to encourage larvae to emerge for removal.
31. OTHER PEST PROBLEMS
The production guide also discusses:
Leaf-eating insects
Examples include:
* caterpillars;
* cocoa loopers;
* grasshoppers;
* locusts;
* leaf-cutting ants; and
* leaf beetles.
Management includes:
* appropriate control measures;
* shade management; and
* monitoring.
Sap-sucking insects
Examples include:
* thrips;
* aphids;
* leafhoppers; and
* psyllids.
Affected plants should be monitored and treated appropriately.
32. GENERAL PEST AND DISEASE MANAGEMENT STRATEGY
The production guide emphasizes an integrated management approach involving:
* pruning;
* frequent harvesting;
* sanitation;
* tree nourishment;
* appropriate shade;
* drainage;
* removal of infected materials; and
* use of suitable planting materials.
The purpose is to:
* reduce pest populations;
* reduce disease spread;
* maintain tree health;
* improve pod production; and
* increase farm productivity.
33. HARVESTING
Cacao pods develop, mature, and ripen in approximately:
160–180 days
Maturity indicators
Pod color changes depending on the variety.
Examples include:
* green → yellow
* dark red/purple → yellow/orange
The farmer should observe the pod's color and maturity characteristics before harvesting.
34. PROPER CACAO HARVESTING
During harvest:
* Remove mature pods carefully.
* Avoid damaging the pods unnecessarily.
* Most importantly, avoid damaging the flower cushions on the trunk and branches.
Flower cushions must remain intact because they can produce flowers and pods for several years.
Harvesting objective
The goal is to obtain mature pods while maintaining the productivity of the tree for future harvest cycles.
35. POD BREAKING AND BEAN REMOVAL
After harvesting:
1. Store/hold the harvested pods appropriately before opening.
2. Open pods using a wooden baton or suitable pod splitter.
3. Avoid cutting or injuring the beans.
4. Remove the beans from the pod.
5. Separate damaged beans.
6. Prepare the wet beans for fermentation.
Cutting the beans can create opportunities for:
* mold;
* insect damage; and
* quality deterioration.
36. FERMENTATION
Fermentation is one of the most important postharvest processes.
Proper fermentation is necessary for developing the compounds that later contribute to chocolate flavor during roasting.
During fermentation:
* sugars in the pulp are converted into alcohol;
* alcohol is converted into acetic acid;
* biochemical reactions occur inside the beans;
* flavor precursors develop; and
* undesirable acidity and bitterness are reduced through subsequent processing.
Factors affecting fermentation
Fermentation is affected by:
* pod ripeness;
* quantity of beans;
* cacao type;
* fermentation duration; and
* management of the fermentation mass.
37. FERMENTATION DURATION
The production guide recommends approximately:
5 days of fermentation
with:
* first turning after 48 hours;
* second turning after another 48 hours.
Fermentation progress can be evaluated through:
* odor;
* internal bean color; and
* external bean color.
The other production guide describes complete fermentation as approximately 5–6 days, with turning after 48 hours and 96 hours.
38. FERMENTATION METHODS
Fermentation boxes
Example dimensions:
85 cm × 85 cm × 45 cm deep
Capacity:
approximately 300 kg wet beans
Other box sizes between:
* 75 × 75 × 45 cm; and
* 1 × 1 × 0.45 m
can accommodate approximately 200–400 kg depending on design and quantity.
Fermentation heaps
For small quantities:
* Place wet beans on banana leaves or bamboo mats.
* Ensure drainage and aeration.
* Raise the heap from the ground.
* Cover the heap.
* Protect it from rainwater.
* Prevent contamination and mold.
Basket fermentation
* Line the basket with banana leaves or perforated plastic.
* Keep the basket slightly raised.
* A batch of approximately 50 kg or more may ferment in approximately 5–6 days.
* Turn after approximately 48 hours.
* Keep the basket under a roof.
* Cover with jute bags to help retain heat.
39. DRYING OF CACAO BEANS
After fermentation, cacao beans must be dried.
The drying process reduces moisture from approximately:
45% → 7%
Drying also contributes to:
* continued flavor development;
* oxidation of polyphenols;
* reduction of bitterness and astringency; and
* reduction of excessive acidity.
40. SUN DRYING
Sun drying is appropriate when conditions permit.
Under Philippine conditions, a practical approach is:
1–2 days sun drying + artificial drying
This can reduce drying costs while supporting quality dried fermented beans.
Beans may be dried using:
* drying pavements;
* plastic sheets;
* bamboo mats;
* bamboo trays; or
* plastic screens.
The beans should be protected from:
* dust;
* foreign materials;
* rain; and
* contamination.
41. ARTIFICIAL DRYING
When using artificial dryers:
Temperature should not exceed 60°C.
Rapid drying can cause excessive acetic acid retention, resulting in overly acidic beans.
The guide describes an artificial dryer approximately:
6 m × 12 m
as capable of accommodating approximately:
5,000 kg fermented beans
with a bean layer of approximately 21 cm.
42. BEAN STORAGE
After drying:
* Store beans in bags.
* Keep bags on wooden pallets.
* Use a dry and ventilated storage area.
* Do not place bags directly on the floor.
* Do not place hot beans into plastic bags.
* Avoid moisture accumulation.
* Prevent mold development.
The production guide also recommends maintaining records of:
* harvested pod weight;
* fermented wet-bean weight;
* dried-bean weight;
* harvest dates;
* fermentation dates; and
* drying dates.
43. CACAO BEAN QUALITY PARAMETERS
The production guide presents quality parameters including:
Parameter
	Guide value
	Fully fermented beans
	80–90%
	Mouldy beans
	3.0% maximum
	Insect damaged/infested/germinated
	2.5% maximum
	Waste/flat beans/fragments/shells/nibs
	1.0% maximum
	Foreign matter
	Free from foreign matter
	Moisture
	7.0% maximum
	pH
	5.0–5.7
	Uniformity
	Not more than 12% outside ±1/3 of average weight
	Odor/flavor
	No undesirable off-flavor
	These parameters demonstrate that cacao production does not end with harvesting; fermentation, drying, sorting, grading, and storage directly influence final bean quality.
44. CURRENT FARM-LEVEL PRODUCTION CONDITIONS
The 2026 study of cacao production in Aleosan, Cotabato provides current field-level evidence about smallholder production.
The study surveyed 58 cacao farmers who were members of the Aleosan Cacao Farmers Association.
The study found that:
* many farmers operate at a relatively small production scale;
* production remains underdeveloped;
* many trees are still young or not yet fully productive;
* pest and disease pressures reduce yields;
* farmers face difficulty accessing technical experts;
* price fluctuations affect production decisions;
* market outlets remain a concern; and
* farmers see potential for expanding their cacao areas.
Monthly production reported in the study
The majority of farmers reported harvesting:
0–9 kg dried beans per month — 67.2%
Other reported groups included:
* 10–30 kg: 18.9%
* 31–60 kg: 5.2%
* 61–90 kg: 1.7%
* 91–120 kg: 5.2%
* 121 kg and above: 1.7%
The study indicates that low production is associated with factors such as:
* immature trees;
* newly planted trees;
* trees recovering from pests and diseases; and
* pest and disease infestation.
45. INTERCROPPING
Intercropping is relevant to cacao production in the Philippines.
The Aleosan study found that many farmers practice intercropping, particularly with coconut, which provides shade to young cacao trees while providing another source of income.
The Roadmap also identifies coconut-cacao intercropping as an important production approach and provides production targets associated with this system.
Other crops identified in the production guide as possible shade/intercrop species include:
* coconut;
* cashew;
* longan;
* durian;
* mango; and
* mangosteen.
46. CACAO PRODUCTION AND MARKET CHALLENGES
The Aleosan study identified the major challenges as:
1. Lack of access to experts
2. Price fluctuation
3. Disease outbreaks
4. Lack of market outlets
5. Lack of tools
6. Lack of training
7. Changing weather
Farmers suggested solutions including:
* stronger market linkages;
* price support;
* training;
* technical assistance; and
* expert guidance.
47. INDUSTRY-LEVEL CHALLENGES
The Philippine Cacao Industry Roadmap identifies broader industry concerns including:
* insufficient quality planting materials;
* inadequate training on good agricultural practices;
* low productivity;
* aging trees;
* pest and disease problems;
* climate and natural calamity risks;
* insufficient postharvest facilities;
* market concerns; and
* the need to improve the cacao value chain.
The Roadmap reports that although high-yielding varieties are available, the targeted 2 kg/tree/year performance has not been achieved in most farms. It reports observed production of approximately 0.5–1.0 kg/tree/year and declining yield per hectare during 2010–2020.
48. VALUE-ADDING AND MARKETING
Cacao can enter different parts of the value chain, including:
Cacao pods
 ↓
Wet beans
 ↓
Fermented beans
 ↓
Dried beans
 ↓
Processing
 ↓
Cacao-based products
The Roadmap emphasizes strengthening:
* production;
* fermentation;
* quality;
* processing;
* market access;
* branding;
* value-added products; and
* linkages with domestic and international markets.
The Aleosan study also identifies value-adding and market linkage as important prospects for farmers.

	Approximately 1,600 plants/ha
	Intercropped cacao density
	Approximately 600 plants/ha
	Nursery ammonium phosphate
	15–35 g/polybag
	Ring-weeding radius
	Approximately 1 m
	Branches near jorquette
	Remove shoots within 60 cm
	Top pruning
	Up to approximately 4 m
	VSD pruning
	Cut 30 cm below infected area
	Stem-borer pruning
	Cut 40 cm below lowest larval hole
	Pod development/maturity
	Approximately 160–180 days
	Fermentation
	Approximately 5 days
	Fermentation turning
	48 and 96 hours
	Basket fermentation
	Approximately 5–6 days
	Drying moisture target
	Approximately 7%
	Artificial drying maximum
	60°C
	Fully fermented beans
	80–90%
	Maximum mouldy beans
	3%
	Maximum insect damaged/infested/germinated
	2.5%
	Maximum waste/flat beans/etc.
	1%
	Bean moisture
	7% maximum
	Bean pH
	5.0–5.7
	Roadmap target yield
	2 kg/tree/year
	Reported common production
	Approximately 0.5–1.0 kg/tree/year
	Aleosan farmers reporting 0–9 kg/month
	67.2%
	52. KEY POINTS FOR AGRIDABAW-3D
For the cacao crop simulation, the player should be able to:
* Select a suitable farm location.
* Check the temperature.
* Check rainfall.
* Check elevation.
* Check soil type.
* Check soil pH.
* Check drainage.
* Select an appropriate cacao variety.
* Select quality planting materials.
* Establish a nursery.
* Germinate cacao seeds.
* Perform vegetative propagation.
* Maintain seedlings.
* Cull weak seedlings.
* Establish shade crops.
* Adjust shade according to tree age.
* Prepare planting holes.
* Transplant seedlings.
* Apply fertilizer.
* Control weeds.
* Prune cacao trees.
* Remove chupons.
* Control tree height.
* Manage shade.
* Monitor cacao pod borer.
* Monitor VSD.
* Monitor black pod and canker.
* Monitor Helopeltis.
* Monitor stem borers.
* Maintain farm sanitation.
* Monitor flowering.
* Monitor pod development.
* Harvest mature cacao pods.
* Avoid damaging flower cushions.
* Break pods carefully.
* Remove beans.
* Ferment beans.
* Turn the fermentation mass.
* Dry fermented beans.
* Monitor moisture.
* Sort and grade beans.
* Store beans properly.
* Respond to market prices.
* Sell dried/fermented beans.
* Explore value-added processing.


Cacao production requires appropriate environmental conditions, quality planting materials, proper farm establishment, and continuous crop management, including fertilization, pruning, shade management, and pest and disease control (Bureau of Plant Industry, 2025). At the industry level, improving productivity also requires quality planting materials, farmer training, improved postharvest practices, and stronger market linkages (Department of Agriculture, 2022). These requirements are supported by recent evidence from Aleosan, Cotabato, where farmers identified technical assistance, disease management, price stability, and market access as important concerns affecting cacao production (Blabagno & Demillo, 2026).

MAIS / CORN
MAIS (CORN) PRODUCTION 
1. CORN OVERVIEW
* Scientific name: Zea mays.
* Corn is one of the important agricultural products in the Philippines.
* It is used as human food and is also an important ingredient for animal feeds.
* Corn is widely planted by Filipino farmers, particularly in southern Mindanao.
* Successful corn production requires:
   * suitable and high-quality seed;
   * appropriate land preparation;
   * adequate water;
   * proper fertilization;
   * weed management;
   * pest management;
   * timely harvesting;
   * immediate drying; and
   * proper storage.
* Before planting, farmers are advised to make sure that there is already a potential buyer or market for the harvested corn.
2. MAIN REQUIREMENTS FOR HIGH YIELD AND INCOME
The ATI/DA guide identifies four major principles:
1. Make sure there is a buyer or market before planting.
2. Use a high-yielding and good-quality variety or hybrid.
3. Provide proper crop care, including appropriate fertilizer, irrigation, and pest control.
4. Harvest at the proper maturity and dry the harvested corn immediately.
The Studocu corn technology guide similarly emphasizes selecting hybrids according to their growth, resistance, stress tolerance, and yield characteristics.
3. VARIETY OR HYBRID SELECTION
The variety or hybrid should be selected according to:
* suitability to the climate and environmental conditions of the planting area;
* yield potential;
* resistance or tolerance to pests and diseases;
* market demand;
* intended use; and
* seed quality.
The ATI/DA guide specifically emphasizes that variety selection should consider both production characteristics and market requirements.
Hybrid selection characteristics
The accessible Studocu guide identifies the following characteristics when selecting hybrids:
* rapid early growth and vigor;
* standability;
* pest and disease resistance;
* stress tolerance; and
* yield.
It notes that early- to medium-maturing hybrids of approximately 102–114 days relative maturity tend to have better early growth than late hybrids of more than 115 days.
Important interpretation for the simulation
A corn variety can therefore have different:
* growth rate;
* maturity period;
* yield potential;
* resistance;
* stress tolerance; and
* market suitability.
4. SEED QUALITY
Use high-quality seed to prevent:
* poor emergence;
* gaps in the planting rows;
* unnecessary replanting;
* additional production expenses; and
* uneven crop establishment.
The ATI/DA guide also notes that properly treated seed can reduce damage from insects such as ants and other seed-damaging pests.
5. LAND PREPARATION
Proper land preparation is intended to produce a suitable seedbed.
Benefits include:
* better seed emergence;
* more uniform plant establishment;
* faster root development;
* weed suppression;
* suitable soil moisture retention; and
* better crop establishment.
Corn planted continuously
In areas continuously planted to corn:
* Plow the field once while the soil still has sufficient moisture.
* This helps crop residues and weeds decompose.
* It also helps establish a more uniform crop stand.
* It can reduce weed growth.
Corn after irrigated rice
When corn follows irrigated rice:
1. Keep the soil sufficiently moist before plowing.
2. Plow the field.
3. Irrigate immediately after plowing.
4. Harrow the soil.
5. Prepare the furrows.
6. Aim for soil conditions appropriate for upland corn production.
6. FURROW PREPARATION
Furrows should have:
* maximum spacing of approximately 75 cm;
* depth of approximately 8 cm.
Furrowing is performed on the day of planting and again during the hilling-up operation.
7. CONSERVATION TILLAGE
The ATI/DA guide identifies conservation-tillage approaches for suitable corn-growing areas, including:
* zero tillage;
* reduced tillage; and
* minimum tillage.
The guide also discusses glyphosate use in relation to these approaches.
Sloping areas
Where corn is planted on sloping land:
* establish appropriate contour crops or vegetation;
* use plants that can help protect the soil;
* reduce erosion risks.
The guide warns that soil can easily erode or collapse with the crop when heavy rainfall occurs without vegetation or other erosion-control measures.
The guide does not recommend herbicide-tolerant GMO corn on very steep areas without soil-erosion control measures.
8. SOIL REQUIREMENTS
The two accessible references do not provide a specific numerical soil-pH requirement for corn.
However, the references emphasize:
* proper seedbed preparation;
* adequate soil moisture;
* soil fertility;
* soil testing;
* appropriate fertilizer application; and
* erosion management.
The ATI/DA guide recommends determining fertilizer requirements through soil analysis rather than assuming that all fields require the same fertilizer rate.
Soil testing
Soil can be tested through:
* the nearest Bureau of Soils and Water Management (BSWM) office; or
* a rapid soil test kit with assistance from Department of Agriculture technicians.
9. WATER MANAGEMENT / IRRIGATION
Water is important for successful corn establishment.
Immediately after planting
In irrigated areas:
* Irrigate after planting.
* The purpose is to provide sufficient moisture for seed germination.
The traditional method described in the guide is irrigation through the furrows.
During dry periods
The ATI/DA guide recommends:
* irrigating newly planted seeds during dry conditions to promote rapid and uniform emergence;
* continuing irrigation at intervals of approximately 5–7 days after emergence;
* continuing until approximately two weeks after emergence;
* thereafter irrigating approximately every two weeks until 21 days before harvest;
* during rainy periods, irrigating only when necessary.
Irrigation from the Studocu source
The accessible Studocu guide notes that corn uses relatively little water during the seedling stage.
If early-season precipitation and stored soil moisture are sufficient for emergence and early development, immediate irrigation after planting may not be necessary because exposed soil can lose water through evaporation.
It also identifies the vegetative stages as relatively less sensitive to yield loss from water stress compared with more sensitive stages.
Water-use efficiency
The Studocu guide defines water-use efficiency as the amount of grain produced per unit of water used.
It identifies practices that can improve water-use efficiency, including:
* crop-residue management;
* mulching;
* row spacing;
* genetic characteristics;
* irrigation-system efficiency.
10. FERTILIZER MANAGEMENT
The ATI/DA guide recommends using the DA Balanced Fertilization Recommendations for local fertilizer decisions.
If soil has not been tested:
Hybrid corn
Use approximately:
6–8 bags of complete fertilizer (14-14-14) per hectare
OPV corn
Use approximately:
4–6 bags of complete fertilizer (14-14-14) per hectare.
If soil has been tested:
* use the recommended amount;
* apply half of the nitrogen requirement initially;
* apply the remaining half later;
* apply all recommended phosphorus and potassium according to the soil-test recommendation.
11. ORGANIC + INORGANIC FERTILIZER
The guide provides an example combination of:
* 6 bags organic fertilizer
* 4 bags 16-20-0
* per hectare.
Application:
1. Spread the organic fertilizer evenly along the furrows before planting.
2. Apply the inorganic fertilizer similarly.
3. Cover the fertilizer with approximately 2 cm of soil before planting.
4. Apply the remaining nitrogen according to soil type and timing recommendations.
12. NITROGEN APPLICATION TIMING
For clay soils:
* Apply the remaining nitrogen approximately 25–30 days after planting, or during hilling-up.
For sandy/silty soils:
* Apply half of the remaining nitrogen during hilling-up.
* Apply the other half approximately 45 days after planting.
13. ACIDIC SOIL MANAGEMENT
For acidic soil:
* Apply lime according to the amount recommended by BSWM.
* Incorporate the lime into the soil 14–21 days before planting.
* The guide gives approximately 3 tons per hectare as a common amount, but the actual amount varies according to soil acidity.
14. SPECIAL FERTILIZER RECOMMENDATIONS
For yellow hybrids:
* 375 kg/ha of 16-20-0
* plus 50 kg/ha of 46-0-0
For white hybrids and OPVs:
* 300 kg/ha of 16-20-0.
The guide also notes:
* inorganic fertilizer prices tend to increase during the rainy season;
* plant nitrogen demand increases during the dry season;
* potassium demand increases during the rainy season;
* soil and plant analysis can improve fertilizer recommendations according to the desired yield.
15. PLANTING
Planting should be performed using good-quality seed and an appropriate planting arrangement.
Hybrid corn
* 1 seed per hill
* approximately 20 cm between hills
OPV corn
* 1–2 seeds per hill
* approximately 25 cm between hills
Baby corn
* closer spacing of approximately 15–18 cm
A corn planter or mechanical planter may be used to obtain more uniform:
* planting depth; and
* seed emergence.
16. PLANTING DEPTH
The accessible Studocu guide recommends:
* approximately 1 inch deep;
* approximately 4–6 inches between seeds;
* approximately 30–36 inches between rows.
The ATI/DA guide additionally notes that single-cross hybrid seeds are smaller and therefore require shallower planting.
17. WEED MANAGEMENT
Weeds compete with corn for:
* water;
* nutrients;
* growing space; and
* other resources.
The ATI/DA guide recommends early weed control.
Around 14–21 days after planting
* Carefully remove weeds when they are abundant.
* Avoid damaging corn plants during weeding.
The guide also recommends shallow cultivation/replowing at appropriate stages to suppress weeds.
18. CROP THINNING / STAND MANAGEMENT
During approximately 7–20 days after planting:
* monitor plant establishment;
* check plant population;
* remove unwanted plants where necessary;
* cultivate shallowly;
* manage weeds;
* monitor pest populations.
The guide integrates thinning, cultivation, and pest management during this stage.
19. HILLING-UP
Hilling-up is performed around the plant during the later early-growth period.
Important functions include:
* covering fertilizer;
* supporting the plants;
* suppressing weeds; and
* improving field management.
The guide places major hilling-up/fertilizer operations approximately 20–40 days after planting.
20. SIDE-DRESSING
Apply the remaining recommended nitrogen fertilizer:
* along the furrow;
* approximately 6 cm away from the plant.
After fertilizer application:
1. Cover the fertilizer immediately.
2. Perform hilling-up.
3. Irrigate immediately when irrigation is available so the fertilizer can dissolve.
4. Continue weekly pest monitoring.
21. CORN PEST MANAGEMENT
The ATI/DA guide emphasizes Integrated Pest Management (IPM) rather than relying only on chemical control.
Important components include:
* resistant varieties;
* appropriate crop management;
* monitoring;
* natural enemies;
* biological control;
* sanitation;
* appropriate pest-control measures.
22. PEST MONITORING
Beginning approximately 3 days after crop emergence:
* inspect the field weekly;
* monitor pest populations;
* monitor natural enemies;
* watch for pest damage;
* continue monitoring throughout the crop cycle.
23. CORN BORER
Corn borer is one of the major pests addressed by the guide.
The crop should be monitored particularly for:
* pest population;
* egg-laying;
* damage symptoms.
Around 20–25 days after planting, monitor corn plants closely for egg masses.
Trichogramma control
If approximately:
3–4 corn-borer egg clusters per 100 corn plants
are observed:
* release approximately 50–70 Trichogramma cards per hectare.
Tricho cards should be attached to leaves at approximately:
15-meter intervals.
24. TRICHOGRAMMA APPLICATION
Trichogramma should be released:
* in the morning when conditions are not excessively dewy; or
* in the afternoon.
Avoid using Trichogramma directly from the refrigerator because refrigeration can slow their ability to search for pest eggs.
The guide recommends repeated placement of tricho cards weekly for three consecutive weeks.
Important note
The guide specifically promotes IPM against:
* corn borer; and
* earworm.
It gives Trichogramma and earwigs as examples of biological control approaches.
25. DISEASE AND PLANT HEALTH MONITORING
From seedling establishment until before harvest:
* continuously inspect the plants;
* immediately remove plants showing serious symptoms;
* burn affected plants according to the guide;
* report widespread symptoms to an agricultural technician.
Symptoms specifically mentioned include:
* chlorotic streaks;
* spots;
* rotting; and
* other abnormal symptoms.
26. SYNCHRONOUS PLANTING
In irrigated corn-growing areas, the guide recommends synchronous planting.
This means planting crops at approximately the same period.
The purpose is to:
* reduce pest problems;
* reduce disease problems; and
* coordinate crop management.
27. BABY CORN PRODUCTION
Baby corn is a distinct production/use category included in the guide.
Harvest timing
Baby corn is generally harvested approximately:
50 days after planting
Another maturity indicator is when the corn silk reaches approximately:
1 inch long.
Harvest handling
After harvesting:
1. Bring baby corn immediately to the preparation area.
2. If the packaging/preparation area is not ready, trim the husk to approximately the length of the ear.
3. Remove the remaining husk when the preparation area is ready.
4. Arrange the baby corn in its container.
5. Cover immediately to preserve freshness.
The guide identifies Styrofoam containers with plastic wrap as a commonly used packaging approach.
Storage
* Baby corn may be stored for approximately one day in a cool area.
* For storage of up to approximately five days, refrigeration is recommended.
28. SWEET AND GLUTINOUS CORN
The guide identifies two types of corn for boiling/eating:
* glutinous corn
* sweet corn
The guide states that appropriate varieties are available for these uses.
29. FRESH / BOILING CORN
For corn harvested while the kernels are still soft:
* harvest at approximately 65–70 days after planting, depending on variety;
* harvest before noon to maintain quality;
* do not remove the husk immediately;
* sell or wholesale immediately when possible;
* if immediate sale is not possible, move the harvested corn to a shaded location.
30. GRAIN CORN HARVEST
For grain corn:
Typical maturity: 90–120 days after planting, depending on variety.
Harvest indicators
Corn is ready for harvest when:
* a black layer develops at the kernel attachment point;
* approximately 90–120 days have passed, depending on variety;
* kernels are shiny/hard;
* leaves and husks are dry.
These indicators should be considered together with variety maturity.
31. HARVESTING PROCEDURE
After harvest:
1. Remove the husk when appropriate.
2. Remove the ear from the plant.
3. Place the harvested corn directly into sacks or baskets.
4. Do not pile harvested corn directly on the ground.
5. Bring harvested corn immediately to the drying area.
The reason for avoiding direct contact with the soil is that dirt can reduce:
* grain quality; and
* selling price.
32. INITIAL DRYING
Immediately after harvest:
* sun-dry for approximately 2–3 days; or
* use a mechanical dryer for approximately 6–8 hours.
The first drying stage should reduce moisture to approximately:
17–18% grain moisture.
This helps harden the kernels so they are less likely to break during shelling.
33. SHELLING
After the initial drying:
* shell the corn;
* remove the kernels from the cobs.
Equipment mentioned includes:
* AMDP manual sheller;
* engine-powered sheller;
* traditional corn shelling tools.
34. SECONDARY DRYING
After shelling:
* sun-dry the kernels for another 1–2 days;
* use a clean drying surface such as:
   * tarpaulin;
   * sacks; or
   * metal sheets;
* dry thoroughly to prevent pest infestation and mold.
Mechanical drying may also be used.
35. TARGET MOISTURE FOR STORAGE
For longer storage:
* dry the grain again after approximately one week if necessary;
* target approximately 12% grain moisture content.
The guide explains that sufficiently low moisture helps prevent:
* storage pests;
* mold;
* deterioration; and
* conditions associated with aflatoxin development.
36. POSTHARVEST HANDLING
If harvested corn cannot be dried immediately:
* avoid removing the husk too early when this would expose wet kernels;
* keep the harvested corn protected;
* dry as soon as possible.
The guide specifically warns that fungus can spread easily in dehusked corn that is not dried promptly.
37. GRAIN QUALITY FOR MARKETING
The ATI/DA guide identifies characteristics associated with higher-quality commercial grain:
* approximately 12–14% moisture;
* approximately 97% purity;
* approximately 3% immature grains;
* no more than approximately 5% damaged grain.
These quality characteristics can influence market price.
38. CORN STORAGE
Storage is performed after harvest to preserve grain quality for short- or long-term periods.
Its purposes are to:
* prevent rapid deterioration;
* prevent rotting;
* protect grain from pests;
* protect grain from diseases;
* protect grain from changing weather conditions;
* maintain food/feed/seed quality.
39. TYPES OF STORAGE
The guide identifies several storage systems.
Farm storage
Used for relatively smaller quantities of grain.
Kamalig
A storage structure made from concrete materials that can hold a moderate amount of bagged grain.
Warehouse
A concrete storage structure designed to hold larger quantities of bagged grain.
Flat bed / flat storage
Grain is stored on the floor of the storage structure.
Silo
A storage structure made from:
* steel;
* galvanized iron; or
* cement,
used for bulk grain storage.
Containers
Small-scale storage may use:
* boxes;
* cans;
* drums;
* baskets; and
* similar containers.
40. OUTDOOR STORAGE
The guide also identifies outdoor storage technologies for locations where large warehouses are not practical, especially for small cooperatives or buyers.
Examples include:
* Volcanic Cube
* Local Plastic Closure (LPC)
According to the guide:
* these technologies can protect stored corn/rice from insects, rats, and rain for approximately three months;
* Volcanic Cube may be usable for up to approximately 10 years;
* LPC may be usable for approximately 5 years when properly cleaned and stored.
41. STORAGE ROOM PREPARATION
Before storing grain:
* clean the walls;
* clean the ceiling;
* clean the floor;
* keep the storage facility clean;
* keep it dry;
* ensure good ventilation;
* ensure the grain itself is clean and dry.
The guide recommends first-in, first-out handling:
Grain stored first should be the first grain removed.
42. STORAGE PEST MANAGEMENT
Stored grain must be protected from:
* insects;
* rats;
* birds;
* moisture;
* mold.
The guide recommends:
* maintaining approximately 1 meter of distance between grain stacks and walls;
* maintaining approximately 1 meter between stacks;
* placing grain on raised platforms;
* separating pest-damaged grain;
* destroying or burying affected grain away from the storage facility;
* maintaining ventilation.
43. RAT AND BIRD CONTROL
Signs of rats or birds include:
* damaged or gnawed sacks;
* rat footprints;
* bird droppings.
To reduce these pests:
* close possible entry points;
* use live traps;
* use snap traps;
* use other appropriate trapping devices;
* rodenticides may also be used according to the guide.
44. STORED-GRAIN INSECT CONTROL
Where insects multiply in storage, the guide identifies methods such as:
* spraying;
* fogging; and
* fumigation.
For corn weevil problems during long-term storage:
* use the recommended treatment solution;
* dry the grain after treatment before putting it into sacks.
45. STORAGE VENTILATION
Adequate airflow is important because it prevents stored grain from:
* heating;
* retaining excessive moisture; and
* developing quality problems.
Grain should therefore be kept in a clean, dry, and well-ventilated storage environment.
