	49. KEY VARIABLES FOR AGRIDABAW-3D
For the AgriDabaw-3D corn simulation, the source-supported variables can be represented as:
Game variable
	Player decision/action
	Market availability
	Check whether a buyer exists before planting
	Corn variety
	Select hybrid, OPV, baby corn, etc.
	Seed quality
	Select high-quality seed
	Yield potential
	Choose variety according to potential yield
	Pest resistance
	Select resistant/tolerant varieties
	Disease resistance
	Select suitable resistant varieties
	Stress tolerance
	Choose suitable hybrid
	Soil condition
	Assess field and soil
	Soil fertility
	Conduct soil testing
	Soil acidity
	Apply lime when required
	Land preparation
	Plow, harrow, furrow
	Erosion risk
	Apply contour/erosion-control measures
	Plant spacing
	Select appropriate spacing
	Water availability
	Manage irrigation
	Fertilizer
	Select appropriate fertilizer
	Nitrogen timing
	Apply split nitrogen
	Weed population
	Cultivate/remove weeds
	Crop stand
	Thin/manage plants
	Hilling-up
	Support plants and cover fertilizer
	Corn borer
	Monitor and apply IPM
	Trichogramma
	Release biological-control cards
	Disease symptoms
	Remove affected plants
	Maturity
	Determine correct harvest time
	Drying
	Dry harvested corn
	Grain moisture
	Reach appropriate storage moisture
	Shelling
	Remove kernels from cobs
	Grain quality
	Maintain purity and minimize damage
	Storage
	Maintain clean, dry, ventilated facility
	Storage pests
	Control rats, birds, insects
	Market price
	Sell according to market opportunity
	50. SYNTHESIS FOR DOCUMENTATION / CAPSTONE PAPER
Corn production is a sequential agricultural process that begins with market planning, variety selection, and quality seed selection, followed by land preparation, planting, water and nutrient management, weed and pest control, harvesting, drying, and storage. The Department of Agriculture's Agricultural Training Institute guide emphasizes that high corn yield and income depend not only on selecting high-yielding varieties but also on ensuring a market, providing appropriate crop care, managing water and fertilizer, controlling pests, and conducting timely harvesting and drying.
Proper variety selection should consider the climate and conditions of the production area, yield potential, resistance to pests and diseases, stress tolerance, and market requirements. The additional corn production technology guide also identifies rapid early growth, standability, pest and disease resistance, stress tolerance, and yield as important characteristics when selecting hybrids. Early- to medium-maturing hybrids are identified in that source as having approximately 102–114 days relative maturity.
Land preparation is important for establishing an appropriate seedbed. The ATI/DA guide recommends appropriate plowing, harrowing, and furrow preparation, with furrows no more than 75 cm apart and approximately 8 cm deep. Conservation-tillage approaches may also be used in suitable areas, while sloping fields require erosion-control measures. Soil fertility should preferably be determined through soil testing, with fertilizer recommendations adjusted according to soil conditions. Acidic soils may require lime based on BSWM recommendations.
After planting, the crop requires continuous management. Irrigation should provide sufficient moisture for establishment and should be adjusted according to rainfall and crop conditions. Fertilizer should be applied according to soil conditions, crop type, and growth stage. Weed management is particularly important during early crop development, while pest monitoring should begin shortly after emergence and continue throughout the production cycle. The guide emphasizes integrated pest management, including the use of resistant varieties and biological-control methods such as Trichogramma against corn borer.
Harvesting depends on the intended use of the corn. Baby corn may be harvested at approximately 50 days, fresh or boiling corn at approximately 65–70 days, and grain corn generally at approximately 90–120 days depending on variety. Grain corn should be harvested when the kernels have reached appropriate maturity and the leaves and husks have dried. After harvest, the grain should be dried, shelled, dried again, and brought to an appropriate moisture level before storage. The guide identifies approximately 12% moisture as a target for longer-term storage and approximately 12–14% moisture as a commercial quality range.
Proper storage is the final stage of the production cycle. Corn should be stored in a clean, dry, and well-ventilated facility and protected from insects, rats, birds, moisture, and mold. Grain stacks should be elevated and separated from walls and other stacks. Pest-damaged grain should be isolated and appropriately disposed of. These practices are important because postharvest handling directly affects grain quality, storage safety, and market value.
Overall, the two references support representing corn production in AgriDabaw-3D as a complete production cycle rather than simply a planting-and-harvesting activity. The simulation can include decisions involving market availability, seed quality, variety selection, land preparation, soil fertility, irrigation, fertilizer application, weed management, pest monitoring, IPM, maturity assessment, harvesting, drying, grain moisture, storage, and selling. This structure reflects the production practices documented in the provided Philippine corn-production references.
Corn production involves a series of interconnected activities beginning with market planning and variety selection and continuing through land preparation, planting, irrigation, fertilization, weed management, pest control, harvesting, drying, and storage (Agricultural Training Institute, n.d.). Hybrid selection may additionally consider early growth, standability, pest and disease resistance, stress tolerance, and yield (Aquino, 2021/2022). These practices demonstrate that corn production requires continuous monitoring and decision-making throughout the crop cycle rather than a single planting and harvesting activity.

STRAWBERRY
STRAWBERRY PRODUCTION 
1. STRAWBERRY OVERVIEW
* Scientific name: Fragaria × ananassa.
* Cultivated strawberry originated from the hybridization of Fragaria chiloensis and Fragaria virginiana.
* The cultivated strawberry types discussed in the guide include:
   * June-bearing
   * Everbearing
   * Day-neutral
* June-bearing strawberries are harvested mainly in early summer.
* Everbearing types can produce fruit during different periods, including July and late August through September.
* Day-neutral varieties can flower and fruit during summer, although high temperatures can favor vegetative growth over flowering.
2. SITE SELECTION
A suitable strawberry production site should:
* be appropriate for agricultural food production;
* have adequate air drainage;
* have adequate water drainage;
* have suitable soil;
* have suitable environmental conditions;
* be assessed for possible hazards before establishing a new production site.
For a new site, potential hazards should be identified and, when necessary, further evaluated through analysis and characterization of contaminants.
3. SOIL REQUIREMENTS
Strawberries grow best in:
* well-drained clay-loam soils;
* loamy soils;
* soils with a pH of approximately 5.5–6.5;
* soils with adequate moisture-holding capacity.
Avoid:
* waterlogged soils;
* sandy soils;
* sites with conditions unsuitable for adequate drainage.
Previous crops
Avoid planting strawberries in sites previously planted with:
* tomato;
* potato;
* pepper;
* eggplant;
unless appropriate measures are taken, because these Solanaceous crops may leave Verticillium wilt organisms in the soil. The guide suggests soil fumigation or the use of Verticillium-resistant varieties when such sites must be used.
4. CLIMATIC REQUIREMENTS
Strawberries grow and yield best at approximately:
14–23°C (57–73°F).
Temperatures above approximately 23°C may reduce flower development in many strawberry cultivars.
For many cultivars:
* day length greater than approximately 10–11 hours;
* combined with relatively low temperature;
can favor:
* flowering;
* fruit set;
* fruit development.
Longer days combined with warm temperatures generally favor vegetative growth rather than flowering.
5. VARIETY / CULTIVAR SELECTION
Selection should consider:
* fruit yield;
* fruit quality;
* market requirements;
* grower preference;
* adaptability to the locality;
* soil type;
* nutrient levels;
* water availability;
* prevailing temperature;
* humidity;
* insect pest populations;
* presence of major pathogens.
The guide encourages obtaining planting materials from accredited plant nursery operators.
6. PLANTING MATERIALS
Strawberries can be propagated using:
* suckers
* runners
The production guide identifies runners as preferred because they can provide higher yield.
The ATI Region VII handout provides a practical runner-propagation procedure:
1. Fill pots with a mixture of:
   * one part garden soil;
   * one part vermicast.
2. Place the pots near the mother plant.
3. Lay the runner on top of the potting medium.
4. Water thoroughly.
5. Allow approximately 4–6 weeks for sufficient root growth.
6. Cut the rooted runner away from the mother plant.
7. Approximately one week after cutting, transplant the runner into a larger pot.
7. PLANTING SEASON
Planting timing depends on the growing location.
For:
* rain-fed hillsides: planting is generally done from June to July;
* valley floors prone to flooding: planting may be done from approximately late August to September.
8. PLANTING SYSTEMS
Two major systems are described:
Matted-row system
* Mother plants are planted.
* Runners are allowed to develop and spread.
* The runners eventually form a matted row of plants.
Spaced-matted-row system
* Runners are spaced to achieve more controlled plant density.
* Plants are maintained in rows.
* Cultivation is performed between rows.
9. PLANT SPACING
For both matted-row and spaced-matted-row systems:
* Mother plants: 18–36 inches apart
* Rows: 36–48 inches apart
The guide indicates an optimum plant density of approximately:
6–7 plants per square foot
for most varieties.
10. RAISED BEDS
Plants grown on heavy soils may benefit from raised beds approximately:
3–5 inches high
Raised beds can improve root development.
However, raised-bed production requires greater water availability because the beds can have higher water requirements.
11. PROPER PLANTING DEPTH
The strawberry crown must not be planted:
* too high; or
* too deep.
Too high
Exposes the roots to wind and may cause:
* plant death;
* poor plant performance.
Too deep
Can cause:
* crown rot.
Therefore, the crown should be positioned at the proper planting depth, neither too high nor too low.
12. IRRIGATION
Adequate irrigation is important because it helps maintain sufficient moisture for:
* plant growth;
* fruit production;
* yield.
Possible irrigation systems mentioned include:
* sprinkler/overhead irrigation;
* drip irrigation;
* flooding;
* overhead bucket systems;
* rubber-hose sprinkling.
Generally, a strawberry plant should receive at least approximately:
1 inch of water per week
during the growing season, either through rainfall or irrigation.
13. IRRIGATION WATER QUALITY
The irrigation water source should be assessed for:
* chemical contamination;
* biological contamination;
* proximity to possible contamination sources.
Examples of possible contamination sources include:
* dumping sites;
* septic tanks.
Water should be suitable for its intended agricultural use.
14. SUBSURFACE IRRIGATION
Where water is abundant:
* subsurface irrigation approximately twice a month or at two-week intervals during the peak dry season can support longer fruiting and better fruit quality.
The guide also indicates that subsurface irrigation can help control some soil-borne insects, including:
* grubs;
* ants;
* cutworms;
* mole crickets.
15. FERTILIZER MANAGEMENT
Fertilizer application should preferably be based on:
soil nutrient analysis.
The purpose is to:
* optimize nutrient use;
* minimize nutrient losses;
* avoid unnecessary fertilization;
* protect soil structure;
* reduce contamination risks.
Only fertilizers properly registered with the Fertilizer and Pesticide Authority (FPA) should be used.
16. EFFECT OF EXCESSIVE FERTILIZER
Excessive or unnecessary fertilization can:
* harm plants;
* alter soil structure;
* increase contamination risks.
Excessive nitrogen is particularly undesirable because it can:
* delay flower development;
* delay fruit maturity;
* soften the berries.
17. RECOMMENDED FERTILIZATION SCHEME
The guide provides the following production scheme:
Inorganic fertilizer
Triple 14
* 20 bags/ha
* side-dressing;
* split application:
   * first application: 2 months after transplanting
   * second application: 4 months after transplanting
Muriate of Potash
* 3 bags/ha
* approximately 90 kg
* side-dressing;
* apply 2 months after transplanting
Urea
* 4 bags/ha
* approximately 120 kg
* side-dressing;
* apply 1 month after transplanting.
18. ORGANIC FERTILIZER
The guide provides three possible organic fertilizer choices:
Cow/carabaо manure
* 18 tons/ha
* basal application;
* approximately 2 weeks before transplanting
Chicken dung
* 6 tons/ha
* basal application;
* approximately 1–2 weeks before transplanting
Compost from farm wastes
* 6 tons/ha
* basal application;
* approximately 1 week before transplanting
The guide indicates that any one of the three organic fertilizers may be used in the recommended scheme.
19. ORGANIC FERTILIZER SAFETY
Composting areas should be:
* separated from crop-production areas;
* separated from drinking-water sources;
* separated from farm-water sources.
Undecomposed or untreated organic material should not be applied because it may contain contaminants.
Organic fertilizer materials should be properly treated before application.
20. LIMING
Lime may be applied when necessary and should be:
* thoroughly mixed into the soil;
* applied at least 2–3 weeks before planting.
Soil samples can be submitted to soil laboratories to determine soil pH.
The guide provides limestone requirements according to soil type and pH. For soils with average organic matter and a target pH of 6.0, the listed rates increase as soil acidity increases.
21. MULCHING
Mulching provides several benefits:
* reduces water evaporation;
* reduces loss of newly applied fertilizer and soil nutrients;
* suppresses weeds;
* keeps berries clean;
* prevents soil from splashing onto plants and fruit during heavy rainfall;
* regulates soil temperature.
22. MULCHING MATERIALS
The guide identifies locally available materials such as:
* rice straw;
* cogon;
* dried pine needles.
Black polyethylene may also be used.
Black polyethylene mulch
Advantages include:
* earlier ripening;
* longer harvest season.
It is recommended for areas with available water for flood irrigation.
Grass-type mulch thickness
For:
* cogon;
* rice straw;
* dried pine needles;
the recommended mulch thickness is approximately:
2 cm.
Thicker mulch can shield the root zone from natural precipitation and irrigation water.
23. WEED MANAGEMENT
Strawberries are relatively shallow-rooted.
Weeds compete with strawberry plants for:
* light;
* water;
* nutrients.
Heavy weed competition can reduce:
* plant establishment;
* plant density;
* fruit size;
* flower-bud initiation;
* harvesting efficiency.
24. MECHANICAL WEED CONTROL
Hand weeding should be performed:
twice a month / approximately every 15 days
during the first two months.
After mulching:
* weeding may be done occasionally;
* frequency depends on weed growth and density.
25. CULTURAL WEED CONTROL
Mulching is an important cultural weed-control method.
Depending on the material:
* black polyethylene is placed at planting;
* cogon grass and rice straw are placed just after planting.
The guide identifies mulching as an economical and efficient weed-control method.
26. CHEMICAL WEED CONTROL
Herbicides can potentially damage strawberry plants if improperly used.
Therefore:
* read the product label carefully;
* use the appropriate product;
* calibrate the sprayer;
* apply the proper amount.
27. PRUNING
Two pruning activities are identified:
Leaf pruning
Performed when leaves become overdeveloped and create excessive shading.
Purposes include:
* improving flowering;
* removing old leaves;
* removing leaves that no longer contribute to fruit development;
* reducing possible suppression of plant growth and flower development caused by accumulated inhibitors in old leaves.
Flower-truss pruning
Remove fruit trusses that have already produced and been harvested.
Purpose:
* provide room for new trusses;
* prevent crowding of newly developing fruits.
28. RENOVATION
Strawberry plants can become overcrowded because of:
* perennial growth;
* prolific runner production.
Overcrowding reduces:
* fruit size;
* fruit quality;
* yield.
Dense plantings can also increase disease problems because:
* foliage dries more slowly after rain;
* fruit dries more slowly;
* spraying becomes more difficult.
The recommended density is approximately:
6–7 plants per square foot.
Renovation should begin approximately:
7–14 days after the last picking
to allow plants to recover and establish new runners for the following crop.
29. PEST MANAGEMENT
Crop protection should focus on appropriate control measures while minimizing unnecessary pesticide use.
The guide recommends:
* using appropriate registered products;
* obtaining products from licensed suppliers;
* following approved label instructions;
* applying Integrated Pest Management (IPM);
* rotating chemical and other control measures to reduce resistance development.
30. WHITE GRUBS
Scientific group identified:
Phyllophaga
White grubs:
* range approximately ½–1 inch;
* live/burrow in soil;
* eventually develop into May or June beetles;
* can damage strawberry roots.
31. STRAWBERRY LEAFROLLERS
Scientific name:
Ancylis comptana fragariae
The adult moths:
* emerge around April or May;
* lay eggs, usually on the underside of leaflets.
Larvae:
* feed on the leaf epidermis;
* produce silk;
* use silk to tie leaflets together.
32. STRAWBERRY MITES
The guide identifies:
* two-spotted spider mite — Tetranychus urticae
* cyclamen mite — Steneotarsonemus pallidus
Spider mites damage leaf surfaces while feeding on plant sap.
Cyclamen mites feed on:
* new leaves;
* unfolding leaves;
* blossoms.
Damage can result in:
* leaf death;
* leaf drop;
* distorted fruits.
33. SLUGS
Slugs identified include Agriolimax and Arion species.
They can:
* create deep holes in strawberries;
* leave slime trails;
* cause damaged fruit to decay rapidly.
34. GRAY MOLD
Strawberry gray mold is caused by:
Botrytis cinerea
It becomes particularly problematic during:
* rainy periods;
* cloudy periods;
* periods immediately before or during harvest.
Fruit is especially susceptible when touching:
* soil;
* infected fruit;
* rotting fruit;
* dead leaves in dense foliage.
Symptoms include:
* soft, rapidly growing light-brown spots;
* fruit drying;
* darkening;
* powdery fungal spores producing a gray appearance.
35. VERTICILLIUM WILT
The guide identifies the causal soil fungus as:
Verticillium alboatrum
Symptoms can occur:
* in new plantings as runners develop;
* in older plantings shortly before harvest.
Symptoms can vary according to cultivar and may resemble other root diseases, so affected plants require testing for definitive diagnosis.
Once established, the fungus may survive in soil for 25 years or more.
36. LEAF SPOT
Strawberry plant leaf spot is associated with:
Mycosphaerella fragariae
Symptoms include:
* dark purple to reddish-purple spots;
* approximately 1/8–1/4 inch spots;
* spots mainly visible on leaves.
Other potentially affected plant parts include:
* petioles;
* stolons;
* fruit stalks/pedicels;
* calyxes;
* ripe strawberries.
The centers of the spots may eventually become:
* nearly white;
* tan;
* gray.
37. LEAF BLIGHT
Strawberry plant leaf blight is associated with:
Dendrophoma obscurans
It commonly causes damage after harvest.
Symptoms include:
* one to six enlarging elliptical or angular blemishes;
* reddish-purple spots initially;
* dark-brown centers as spots enlarge;
* lighter-brown areas;
* purplish borders.
The disease mainly attacks weaker, slow-growing plants and can also cause pink, soft rot near the stem end of fruit.
38. CROP PROTECTION SAFETY
Workers applying crop-protection products should:
* be properly trained;
* understand correct handling;
* understand correct application procedures.
A first-aid kit should be available for workers who experience injuries or chemical contamination. Workers who directly handle and apply chemicals should undergo periodic/annual medical examinations.
39. PESTICIDE STORAGE
Crop-protection products should be stored:
* in their original containers;
* with legible labels;
* in a secure structure;
* in a well-lighted facility;
* in a sound facility;
* with access restricted to authorized personnel;
* with appropriate segregation of products.
Storage shelves should be made of non-absorbent materials such as:
* metal;
* rigid plastic.
40. PESTICIDE DISPOSAL
Empty chemical containers:
* must not be reused for food or drinks;
* should be securely kept before disposal;
* should be rinsed three times before disposal.
Tank washings must be disposed of appropriately to minimize:
* produce contamination;
* environmental harm.
41. FARM SANITATION
The production site should be maintained free from potential sources of contamination.
Cleaning and sanitation procedures should be:
* prepared;
* implemented;
* regularly followed.
Cleaning and sanitation chemicals should be selected carefully to minimize contamination risks.
42. ANIMAL, PEST, AND DISEASE CONTROL
Domestic and farm animals should be excluded from:
* production areas;
* harvesting areas;
* packing areas;
* storage areas.
Measures should be implemented to prevent pests and diseases from entering:
* cultivation areas;
* handling areas;
* packing areas;
* storage areas.
Baits and traps should be strategically positioned so they do not contaminate:
* fruit;
* packaging;
* handling materials.
43. WORKER HYGIENE
Workers should observe:
* personal cleanliness;
* appropriate clothing;
* hand washing;
* proper fingernail hygiene;
* appropriate personal behavior.
Workers should not:
* smoke;
* spit;
* eat;
* chew while performing activities where these behaviors could create contamination risks.
Toilets and handwashing facilities should:
* be accessible;
* be properly maintained;
* be hygienic;
* be located appropriately.
44. EQUIPMENT AND HARVESTING CONTAINERS
Harvesting and handling containers must not be used for:
* agricultural chemicals;
* lubricants;
* oil;
* cleaning chemicals;
* plant debris;
* tools.
Reusable harvesting containers and tools that contact fresh fruit should be:
* made from non-toxic materials;
* easy to clean;
* easy to disinfect.
Equipment and farm vehicles should be regularly maintained.
Equipment, containers, and materials should be stored separately from:
* chemicals;
* fertilizers;
* soil additives.
45. DOCUMENTATION AND RECORD KEEPING
Records should be maintained for:
Site history
For new sites:
* record identified hazards;
* document remedial actions;
* record results of remedial measures;
* identify each production area by name or code.
Planting materials
If planting material is produced on the farm or obtained from non-accredited sources:
* document chemical treatment;
* document purpose of treatment.
If obtained from an accredited nursery:
* record cultivar;
* supplier;
* procurement date.
Fertilizer and crop protection
Record:
* source;
* product name;
* date obtained;
* quantity obtained.
After application, record:
* date;
* product/material used;
* treatment location;
* application rate;
* application method;
* operator name.
Sprayer maintenance and calibration should also be recorded.
46. HARVEST MATURITY
Strawberries can be harvested at different maturity levels.
Full ripe
* Fruit surface is red throughout.
* Suitable for:
   * intermediate fresh consumption;
   * processing.
Three-fourths ripe
Suitable for:
* nearby markets;
* long-distance markets.
47. HARVESTING TIME
Harvesting should:
* follow the requirements of the intended market;
* avoid rainy conditions;
* separate fruit unfit for human consumption;
* properly dispose of fruit that cannot be made safe through further processing.
The guide states that strawberries are best harvested during the later part of the day, around 3:00 PM. Harvesting at this time may extend storage life by approximately ½–1 day according to the guide.
48. HARVESTING TECHNIQUE
To harvest:
1. Hold the fruit by its peduncle.
2. Cut using the thumbnail.
3. Leave approximately 0.5–1.0 cm of peduncle attached to the fruit.
Harvest frequency:
* approximately every 2 days for fresh-market fruit;
* approximately every 2–3 days when intended for processing.
49. GRADING
The guide provides the following fruit-size classification:
Grade
	Diameter
	Average fruit weight
	XL
	>20 mm
	32 g and above
	L
	15–20 mm
	26–31 g
	M
	8–14 mm
	20–25 g
	S
	6–7 mm
	14–19 g
	Reject/Cull
	—
	13 g and below
	Produce should be graded and packed according to market requirements.
50. PACKAGING
Harvested berries may be placed in:
* woven bamboo trays;
* baskets;
* other appropriate containers.
Harvesting containers generally range from:
1–3 kg
while bulk transport containers may range from:
8–18 kg.
Containers exceeding approximately 18 kg can increase the risk of fruit damage caused by compression during transportation.
51. TRANSPORT
Field-packed produce should be:
* covered during transportation;
* protected from contamination;
* transported separately from materials that can cause chemical contamination;
* protected from biological and physical hazards.
Incompatible products should not be mixed during transportation.
If a farm vehicle is also used for other purposes, it should be cleaned before transporting harvested strawberries.
The guide describes berries being transported in:
* plastic trays;
* woven bamboo trays;
* approximately 300-g capacity packages lined with thin cellophane;
* cartons separated by cardboard sections.
Baskets used for transportation should generally be limited to 18 kg or less and may be lined with newspaper to reduce compression damage.
52. STRAWBERRY PERISHABILITY
Strawberries are highly perishable because of their high moisture content.
When left at room temperature for more than approximately:
24 hours
they may over-ripen and become unsuitable for fresh consumption.
Fruit that cannot be sold or processed immediately should be:
* stored at low temperature; or
* partially processed to prevent deterioration.
53. PROCESSING AND UTILIZATION
Fresh strawberries can be used for:
* salads;
* shakes;
* juices.
Traditional processed products include:
* jams;
* preserves;
* wines;
* candies.
The guide discourages using excessively overripe berries for processing because this may produce lower-quality processed products.
54. ATI REGION VII PROPAGATION AND GROWTH INFORMATION
The shorter ATI Region VII handout provides additional practical information for home/small-scale propagation:
* use a 1:1 mixture of garden soil and vermicast;
* place the pot close to the mother plant;
* place the runner on the growing medium;
* water thoroughly;
* allow approximately 4–6 weeks for adequate root development;
* cut the rooted runner from the mother plant;
* transplant it into a larger pot approximately one week later;
* water plants 2–3 times per week during the dry season;
* strawberries may begin flowering after approximately 3–4 months;
* harvest when fruits turn red.

	59. SYNTHESIS FOR DOCUMENTATION / CAPSTONE PAPER
Strawberry production requires a suitable production site, well-drained soil, appropriate climatic conditions, quality planting materials, and continuous crop management. The production guide identifies well-drained clay-loam and loamy soils with a pH of approximately 5.5–6.5 and temperatures of about 14–23°C as favorable conditions for strawberry production. Proper cultivar selection should also consider yield, fruit quality, market requirements, locality, water availability, temperature, humidity, pests, and disease risks (Agricultural Training Institute–Cordillera Administrative Region [ATI-CAR], n.d.).
Proper establishment begins with quality runners, suitable planting systems, correct plant spacing, appropriate planting depth, irrigation, fertilization, and mulching. The guide identifies runners as preferred planting materials and describes matted-row and spaced-matted-row systems, with mother plants spaced 18–36 inches apart and rows 36–48 inches apart. Soil analysis should guide fertilizer management, while mulching can reduce water loss, suppress weeds, keep fruit clean, and regulate soil temperature. The ATI Region VII handout additionally provides a practical runner-propagation procedure using a one-to-one mixture of garden soil and vermicast, with rooted runners generally ready for separation after approximately four to six weeks (ATI-CAR, n.d.; Agricultural Training Institute–Region VII [ATI-7], n.d.).
Continuous maintenance is necessary to sustain plant growth and fruit quality. Weed competition can reduce plant establishment, fruit size, flower-bud initiation, and yield, making regular weeding and mulching important. Pruning of excessive or old leaves can support flowering, while removal of harvested flower trusses provides space for new fruiting structures. Plant density must also be managed because overcrowding can reduce fruit size, quality, and yield while increasing disease problems. Renovation is recommended within approximately 7–14 days after the final picking to allow plants to recover and establish new runners (ATI-CAR, n.d.).
Strawberry production also requires continuous pest and disease management. The guide identifies white grubs, leafrollers, mites, slugs, gray mold, Verticillium wilt, leaf spot, and leaf blight among the important pest and disease concerns. Integrated Pest Management is encouraged to minimize unnecessary pesticide use, while crop-protection products should be properly registered, applied according to label instructions, and handled by trained personnel. Proper sanitation, worker hygiene, equipment cleaning, and safe chemical storage further reduce contamination and production risks (ATI-CAR, n.d.).
Harvesting should be based on the intended market and fruit maturity. Full-ripe strawberries have a red surface throughout and are suitable for intermediate fresh consumption or processing, while three-fourths-ripe fruits are suitable for nearby and long-distance markets. The guide recommends avoiding harvest during rain and describes careful picking that leaves approximately 0.5–1.0 cm of peduncle attached. Because strawberries are highly perishable, harvested fruit should be graded, packed, transported carefully, and sold, stored under low temperature, or processed promptly. Proper handling is particularly important because fruit can deteriorate rapidly when kept at room temperature for extended periods (ATI-CAR, n.d.).
Overall, these references support representing strawberry production in AgriDabaw-3D as a complete crop-management cycle, beginning with site and climate assessment and continuing through propagation, planting, irrigation, fertilization, mulching, weed control, pruning, pest and disease management, harvesting, grading, packaging, transport, and postharvest handling. The production variables documented in the references can therefore serve as source-based gameplay parameters for simulating strawberry cultivation and the consequences of farm-management decisions.


Combined paragraph
Strawberry production requires suitable soil and climatic conditions, quality planting materials, proper crop establishment, and continuous management, including irrigation, fertilization, mulching, weed control, pruning, and pest and disease management (Agricultural Training Institute–Cordillera Administrative Region [ATI-CAR], n.d.). Proper harvesting, grading, packaging, transportation, and postharvest handling are also necessary because strawberries are highly perishable and susceptible to physical and biological deterioration. The ATI Region VII propagation guide further provides a practical approach to producing strawberry planting materials through rooted runners and emphasizes regular watering, flowering development, and harvesting at the red-fruit stage (Agricultural Training Institute–Region VII [ATI-7], n.d.).
61. APA 7TH EDITION REFERENCES
Agricultural Training Institute–Cordillera Administrative Region. (n.d.). Good agricultural practice: Strawberry production [PDF]. Department of Agriculture. ATI-CAR Strawberry Production Guide
Agricultural Training Institute–Region VII. (n.d.). Strawberry [PDF]. Department of Agriculture. ATI-7 Strawberry Handout


DURIAN
DURIAN PRODUCTION GUIDE 
1. DURIAN OVERVIEW
* Scientific name: Durio zibethinus Murr.
* Durian belongs to the genus Durio.
* The guide places it in the family Bombacaceae.
* It is described as an important native fruit of Southeast Asia and is commonly known as the “king of fruits.”
* The fruit is characterized by:
   * large size;
   * distinctive odor;
   * thick, thorn-covered husk;
   * edible creamy to yellow, pinkish, or orange flesh;
   * seeds that can also be eaten when cooked.
* Durian fruits are generally ovoid, ovoid-oblong, or nearly round.
* The guide describes fruits approximately 15–30 cm long, 12.5–15 cm wide, and weighing up to 8 kg.
* The rind is thick, tough, semi-woody, and covered with sharp spines.
* The fruit contains five compartments, each potentially containing several seeds.
* Mature fruits naturally fall from the tree.
2. PRODUCTION IMPORTANCE IN THE PHILIPPINES
The guide reports that in 2016:
* approximately 16,618.86 hectares were planted to durian in the Philippines;
* approximately 1,265,890 bearing trees were recorded;
* the Davao Region accounted for 8,344 hectares, or 50.21% of the national planted area;
* Davao had approximately 818,270 bearing trees;
* national production was approximately 71,444.19 metric tons;
* Davao contributed approximately 53,805.71 metric tons, equivalent to 75.31% of national production.
The source identifies Davao as a particularly important durian-producing region.
3. USES OF DURIAN
The guide describes several uses of the fruit and other plant parts.
Fruit
Durian flesh may be:
* eaten fresh;
* chilled and eaten directly;
* boiled with sugar;
* cooked in coconut water;
* used as an ice-cream flavoring;
* canned in syrup;
* dried;
* processed into durian paste.
Unripe durian may be boiled and eaten as a vegetable.
Seeds
Seeds may be:
* boiled;
* dried;
* fried;
* roasted;
* cooked with sugar;
* prepared with coconut oil and spices.
Rind
The dried or partially dried rind may be:
* burned as fuel;
* used to produce smoke for fish;
* burned into ash for certain traditional uses.
Other traditional uses
The guide also records traditional medicinal uses involving:
* leaves;
* roots;
* fruits;
* leaf juice;
* burned rind ash.
These are presented in the source as traditional uses rather than as validated medical recommendations.
4. SOIL REQUIREMENTS
Durian is adapted to:
* sandy loam soil;
* clay loam soil;
* soils with high organic matter;
* reasonably deep soils;
* slightly acidic soil.
The guide identifies approximately pH 5.6 as the soil condition associated with adaptation.
Important soil considerations
The production system therefore requires:
* adequate soil depth;
* appropriate drainage;
* sufficient organic matter;
* suitable acidity;
* good soil preparation before planting.
Heavy soils require deeper and wider planting holes.
5. CLIMATE REQUIREMENTS
Durian is adapted to:
* lowland tropical conditions;
* elevations up to approximately 800 meters above sea level;
* temperatures of approximately 25–35°C;
* approximately 80% relative humidity.
These values provide the principal climate parameters described by the guide.
6. VARIETY SELECTION
The guide lists NSIC-approved and registered durian varieties, including:
* Chanee
* Monthong
* Atabrine
* Mamer
* GD 69
* Alcon Fancy
* Lacson Uno
* Arancillo
* Puyat
* Oboza
* Duyaya
* SMIARC #2
The varieties differ in:
* fruit shape;
* fruit size;
* maturity period;
* peel color;
* edible portion;
* aroma;
* flesh color;
* total soluble solids (TSS).
Examples:

	7. PROPAGATION
Durian can be propagated through seed and grafting-related techniques.
The guide reports that:
* seedling trees may take many years before bearing;
* grafted trees generally bear earlier;
* selected cultivars are propagated using budding or grafting techniques;
* grafted trees generally remain shorter than seedling trees.
The guide specifically discusses:
* inarching;
* patch budding;
* hypocotyl grafting;
* epicotyl side grafting;
* cleft grafting.
8. PREPARATION OF PLANTING MATERIALS
Step 1 — Select seeds
Use:
* fully developed seeds;
* seeds from mature or ripe fruits;
* recommended varieties.
Step 2 — Germination
Germinate seeds in a:
* seed box; or
* seed bed.
Possible germination media include:
* coco peat;
* fine river sand;
* sawdust.
Step 3 — Transfer seedlings
At approximately 9–12 days after germination, prick seedlings into polyethylene bags.
Recommended bag sizes include:
* 6 × 10 inches; or
* 7 × 11 inches.
The growing medium should contain:
* 1 part garden soil;
* 1 part fine river sand;
* 1 part compost.
Step 4 — Nursery management
Place seedlings in a nursery with a net cover providing approximately 70% sunlight penetration.
Water:
* immediately after planting into the polyethylene bags;
* every 2–3 days thereafter, or according to need.
Step 5 — Grafting age
The guide gives different ages for different techniques:
* Hypocotyl grafting: 21–30 days old rootstock
* Epicotyl side grafting: 2–4 months old
* Cleft grafting: approximately 5 months old
* Patch budding: 8 months and older
Step 6 — Hardening
Reduce nursery shade gradually from approximately 30% shade to full sunlight during the two months before field planting.
9. LAND PREPARATION
Before planting:
1. Clear the area.
2. Remove stumps.
3. Remove grasses.
4. Plow the field twice.
5. Harrow the field three times.
The purpose is to loosen and prepare the soil for planting.
10. PLANTING DISTANCE
Two planting arrangements are provided:
8 × 8 meters
* approximately 156 plants/ha
10 × 10 meters
* approximately 100 plants/ha
The choice affects:
* tree density;
* canopy space;
* orchard management;
* movement within the orchard.
11. PREPARATION OF PLANTING HOLES
Standard hole:
* 30 cm wide
* 30 cm deep
For heavy soil:
* deeper holes;
* wider holes;
are recommended.
12. PLANTING PROCEDURE
1. Remove the seedling from the polyethylene bag.
2. Avoid breaking the soil ball.
3. Prune spiraled roots to encourage branching.
4. Plant young seedlings during their dormant stage.
5. Place the seedling in the prepared hole.
6. Fill the hole using a soil-manure mixture.
7. Gently press the soil around the plant.
8. Plant during the rainy season.
13. TEMPORARY SHADE AFTER PLANTING
Newly planted seedlings require temporary shade.
Shade may be provided for:
* approximately two weeks;
* or several months depending on weather conditions.
The purpose is to:
* reduce transplanting shock;
* protect seedlings from strong sunlight;
* allow the plant to recover after field establishment.
14. FERTILIZATION
Basal fertilizer
Apply:
50 grams or approximately 5 tablespoons of complete fertilizer (14-14-14)
as basal fertilizer.
The fertilizer should be:
* applied around the plant;
* covered with a thin layer of soil.
The guide also emphasizes that fertilizer application should be based on:
* soil analysis; or
* plant tissue analysis.
15. WEED MANAGEMENT
Ring weeding should be performed:
* regularly;
* before fertilizer application.
For the cost-management schedule in the guide:
* trees 1–5 years old: ring weed approximately 1 meter from the trunk;
* trees 6–10 years old: weed up to the canopy drip area;
* ring weeding is performed at approximately two-month intervals.
Spaces between rows should be cleared of weeds approximately four times per year.
16. INTERCROPPING
Seasonal crops may be planted:
* between durian rows;
* while young durian trees are not yet productive.
This allows the area between trees to be used during the non-bearing period.
17. MULCHING
Mulching is recommended particularly during the dry season.
Possible mulch materials include:
* rice straw;
* dried leaves;
* cut grasses;
* coconut husk.
Cut banana pseudostems may also be used as mulch, particularly where water is difficult to obtain, because they contain considerable moisture.
18. IRRIGATION
Water the trees during the:
dry season.
Irrigation is particularly important for:
* newly established trees;
* maintaining plant vigor;
* preventing water stress.
The establishment cost schedule includes watering for the first three months in the field.
19. PRUNING
Pruning is important because it:
* improves air circulation;
* decreases relative humidity within the canopy;
* reduces conditions favorable to pests and diseases;
* prevents low branches from being splashed by rain;
* removes possible pathogen contact points;
* shapes the canopy;
* improves fruit production.
Remove:
* dead branches;
* broken branches;
* diseased branches;
* low-lying branches;
* water shoots.
20. TRAINING
Training is performed early in orchard development, particularly during the:
* first year;
* second year.
The purpose is to establish the desired tree structure and canopy form.
21. POLLINATION
Durian has a high rate of self-incompatibility.
Therefore:
hand pollination is recommended
to achieve a much higher percentage of fruit set.
For a simulation, pollination can therefore be represented as an important event between flowering and fruit development.
22. FRUIT THINNING
Fruit thinning is normally performed:
4–6 weeks after flower opening.
For durian cv. Puyat, the guide cites research showing that thinning at approximately 6 weeks after fruit set, while leaving 2–3 fruits per cluster, may increase yield.
Small and misshapen fruits should be removed, leaving approximately:
1–2 fruitlets per cluster.
23. RECOMMENDED FRUIT LOAD PER TREE
The guide provides the following recommended number of fruits:
Tree age
	Recommended fruits/tree
	4 years
	5
	5 years
	15
	6 years
	25
	7 years
	40
	8 years
	55
	9 years
	70
	10 years
	85
	24. PROPPING
As fruit load increases, branches may require support.
Recommended materials:
* bamboo poles;
* wooden poles.
Branches can be tied:
* upward to the main trunk; or
* to an external support such as a wire network.
25. MAJOR INSECT PESTS
25.1 Fruit borer
Scientific names identified:
* Conogethes punctiferalis
* Tonica sp.
The pest:
* attacks flowers and fruits;
* has larvae that bore into fruit;
* damages the pulp;
* lays eggs singly on fruit spines;
* may enter through the peduncle, base, or side of the fruit.
Management includes:
* monitoring during early fruit development;
* fruit thinning;
* collecting and burning or burying fallen fruits;
* light trapping;
* insecticide spraying beginning approximately 4 weeks after fruit set.
25.2 Shot-hole borer
Scientific name:
Xyleborous sp.
Characteristics:
* small black or brown beetle;
* associated with fungal pathogens;
* associated with physiologically stressed trees;
* attacks the main trunk or main branches.
Management:
* maintain tree vigor;
* fertilize appropriately;
* irrigate;
* prune regularly;
* maintain wider planting distances;
* use resistant varieties where available;
* scrape infested portions;
* apply insecticide to affected areas.
25.3 Durian psyllids
Scientific name:
Allocarsidara incognita
Characteristics:
* adults are greenish-brown to brownish;
* suck sap from young leaves;
* particularly prevalent during flushing.
The guide identifies predators as natural enemies and also describes insecticide management, including products such as:
* malathion;
* deltamethrin;
* lambdacyhalothrin;
* chlorpyrifos;
* dimethoate.
The source describes applications 4–5 times from June to November.
25.4 Bao-bao
Scientific name:
Haplaphalara sp.
It:
* attacks shoots;
* attacks flowers;
* attacks fruitlets;
* sucks sap from young leaves and flowers;
* contributes to fruitlet dropping;
* may cause severe defoliation;
* can eventually cause tree death.
The guide recommends the same control measures used for durian psyllids.
25.5 Twig borer / carpenter moth
Larvae:
* bore holes in young plants;
* attack small branches;
* attack twigs of older trees.
Management:
* prune damaged branches;
* cut affected material;
* burn the removed material.
25.6 Mealybugs
Management:
* insecticide application when infestation becomes heavy.
26. MAJOR DISEASES
26.1 Phytophthora disease
The guide describes Phytophthora as a serious disease of durian.
It is:
* soil-borne;
* capable of infecting all parts of the tree;
* capable of affecting trees at different stages of growth;
* spread through wind, rainwater, insects, and snails.
Three major forms are described.
Patch canker
Symptoms:
* wet lesions on bark;
* commonly near the crotch;
* bark necrosis;
* discoloration;
* reddish-brown gummy/resinous exudation.
Management:
* provide wider spacing;
* remove weeds;
* prune low-hanging branches;
* apply chemical treatment at the first sign of infection;
* scrape and remove diseased bark;
* paint infected wood with fungicide;
* apply Trichoderma harzianum as described by the guide.
Seedling dieback and foliar blight
Symptoms:
* small off-colored spots;
* water-soaked spots;
* spots become dark;
* spots may merge into large necrotic or dead areas.
Nursery management emphasizes:
* sanitation;
* aeration;
* drainage.
Where necessary, the guide describes periodic systemic fungicide applications, including products based on:
* metalaxyl;
* fosetyl aluminum;
* combinations such as metalaxyl-mancozeb.
Phytophthora fruit rot
Initial symptoms:
* tiny water-soaked lesions.
As disease progresses:
* lesions enlarge;
* become brown;
* become dark;
* eventually result in peel cracks as fruit approaches maturity.
Management should integrate:
* phytosanitary measures;
* cultural practices;
* chemical treatment during the pre-harvest period.
27. PHYSIOLOGICAL DISORDERS
Uneven fruit ripening
Characterized by:
* hardened, leathery aril in a fruit that appears ripe;
* affected aril remains unripe;
* whitish;
* odorless;
* tasteless.
Wet or watercore
Characterized by:
* excessive moisture;
* very soft core;
* very soft flesh.
Tip burn
Symptoms:
* end portion of the flesh becomes dark yellow.
The guide indicates that it is probably associated with:
* insufficient water during flesh/aril development.
28. FLOWERING TO FRUIT DEVELOPMENT
The overall production sequence is:
Flowering → Pollination → Fruit Set → Fruit Thinning → Fruit Development → Propping → Maturity → Harvest
Because durian has a high level of self-incompatibility, hand pollination is particularly important to increase fruit set. Fruit thinning is subsequently used to manage fruit load and remove small or misshapen fruitlets.
29. FRUIT MATURITY
A well-maintained durian tree may begin bearing as early as approximately:
5 years old.
The fruit generally falls from the tree when mature and ripe, often at night.
Maturity indicators
The guide identifies the following:
1. Fruit age
   * Local cultivars: approximately 106–108 days from flower opening
   * Chanee: approximately 112–113 days
   * Monthong: approximately 125 days
2. Color change
   * pericarp becomes yellowish-green or brown.
3. Sound
   * dull, hollow sound when tapped.
4. Aroma
   * strong characteristic aroma.
5. Spines
   * become very pliable.
6. Suture
   * becomes very distinct.
7. Flavor
   * sweet with characteristic durian flavor.
30. HARVESTING
Durian may be harvested by:
* collecting naturally fallen fruit;
* hand-picking fruit at the appropriate maturity stage.
The guide warns that falling durian fruit can cause serious injury, so people should exercise caution when working beneath trees during the ripening period.
31. HAND-PICKED VS. FALLEN FRUIT
According to the guide:
* hand-picked fruit may have a shelf life of approximately 5–7 days;
* fallen fruit may have a shelf life of approximately 2–3 days.
Fallen fruit may have excellent flavor but deteriorates more rapidly.
32. YIELD
Durian fruit generally matures approximately:
3½–4½ months after fruit set.
The source reports that:
* seedling trees in India may bear approximately 40–50 fruits annually;
* well-grown, high-yielding cultivars may produce approximately 6,720 kg/ha.
These figures are presented in the guide's production discussion.
33. POSTHARVEST HANDLING
Durian is highly perishable.
The guide states:
* naturally dropped fruits should be consumed within approximately 2–3 days;
* properly harvested fruit can be kept for approximately one week under ambient conditions;
* optimum maturity at harvest is important;
* fruit should be sorted according to:
   * cultivar;
   * degree of ripeness.
Harvesting too early can extend storage life but may result in incomplete flavor development. Allowing fruit to fall naturally may produce better flavor but reduces shelf life.
34. REFRIGERATION
Refrigeration is identified as the most effective method for extending shelf life.
The guide provides examples:
* durian stored at approximately 10°C in Malaysia for up to two weeks without observed chilling injury;
* mature-green Thai cultivars such as Monthong, Chanee, and Kanyao stored at approximately 15°C;
* the source notes that storage requirements for locally grown introduced cultivars still need to be established.
These are source-reported storage examples rather than a universal storage recommendation.
35. MINIMALLY PROCESSED DURIAN
The guide describes minimally processed durian as:
* extracted pulp;
* seeds retained;
* packed in a Styrofoam tray;
* covered with transparent cling film.
The pulp may be frozen at approximately:
−18°C to −10°C
for as long as:
six months, according to the guide.
36. PACKAGING
Local or nearby markets
The guide describes:
50-kg bamboo baskets
as a common container.
Advantages:
* inexpensive;
* good ventilation.
Disadvantage:
* not rigid enough for stacking.
Therefore, bamboo baskets should not simply be piled on top of each other during transport.
Distant markets
For Metro Manila:
* approximately 25-kg wooden crates;
* approximately 10–15 fruits per crate.
For inter-island transport:
* banana cartons;
* approximately 4–6 fruits per carton.
37. PROTECTION DURING PACKAGING
To reduce mechanical damage:
* separate individual fruits using banana leaf sheaths;
* use newspaper layers in wooden crates;
* use cardboard between layers if fruits must be stacked.
Wooden crates offer stronger protection and can be stacked more safely than bamboo baskets.
38. TRANSPORT AND HANDLING
After collection, fruit should be transported promptly to the intended market.
Vehicles may include:
* jeeps;
* trucks;
* ships;
* aircraft for inter-island trade.
Vehicles should be:
* covered with tarpaulin or canvas;
* protected against direct sunlight.
The guide recommends transporting durian at night when possible because:
* direct sunlight can cause cracking;
* heat can accumulate inside containers.
39. ESTABLISHMENT REQUIREMENTS FOR ONE HECTARE

Equipment and tools

₱4,210.00.

The production guide's economic section tracks the following major activities:
* training/pruning;
* weeding/underbrushing;
* fertilization;
* irrigation;
* propping;
* pest and disease management;
* harvesting.
The guide provides a year-by-year cost schedule from Year 1 to Year 10.

* complete fertilizer 14-14-14;
* ammonium fertilizer 16-20-0;
* muriate of potash 0-0-60;
* foliar fertilizer;
* chicken dung.

* 0-0-60: ₱1,600/bag (50 kg)
* foliar fertilizer: ₱220/100-g pack
* chicken dung: ₱60/30-kg bag
These are the prices used in the guide's cost analysis and should not be treated as current 2026 market prices.
42. IRRIGATION COST SCHEDULE
The guide's cost analysis tracks irrigation expenses annually.
It notes that:
* irrigation for the first three months in the field is already included in establishment costs;
* subsequent irrigation costs are separately calculated.
The reported annual costs vary as the orchard develops.
43. PROPPING COST SCHEDULE
The cost analysis begins significant propping expenses around:
Year 5
as the trees become more productive.
The guide uses:
* bamboo poles;
* tying materials;
* labor.
The reported example begins with 468 bamboo poles in Year 5 and increases support requirements in later years.

The guide's economic analysis uses example prices of:
* insecticide: ₱950/L
* fungicide: ₱650/kg
The calculated annual pest and disease management costs increase as the orchard matures.
Again, these are historical/example cost assumptions in the production guide, not current pesticide prices.

Year 4
and increases as production increases.

* labor;
* kaing/baskets for hauling.

₱200 per kaing
for the cost calculation.

	51. SYNTHESIS FOR DOCUMENTATION / CAPSTONE PAPER
Durian production requires suitable tropical environmental conditions, appropriate soil, quality planting materials, proper orchard establishment, and continuous crop management. The production guide identifies sandy-loam and clay-loam soils with high organic matter and slightly acidic conditions, approximately pH 5.6, as suitable for durian. The crop is adapted to lowland tropical environments up to approximately 800 meters above sea level, with an optimum temperature range of 25–35°C and approximately 80% relative humidity. Proper land preparation, planting distance, nursery management, temporary shading, irrigation, fertilization, weed control, mulching, pruning, and training are important components of orchard establishment and maintenance.
Durian production continues through flowering, pollination, fruit setting, fruit thinning, fruit support, and pest and disease management. Because durian has a high rate of self-incompatibility, hand pollination is recommended to improve fruit setting. Fruit thinning is generally performed four to six weeks after flower opening, with small and misshapen fruits removed to manage fruit load. Regular pruning improves air circulation and reduces conditions favorable to pests and diseases, while irrigation and mulching help manage water availability during dry periods. Major production risks identified in the guide include fruit borers, shot-hole borers, psyllids, bao-bao, twig borers, mealybugs, and Phytophthora diseases.
Harvesting should be based on appropriate maturity indicators, including fruit age, peel color, sound when tapped, aroma, spine pliability, and development of the fruit suture. Local cultivars are identified as reaching maturity at approximately 106–108 days from flower opening, while Chanee and Monthong require approximately 112–113 and 125 days, respectively. Durian is highly perishable; therefore, proper harvesting, sorting, packaging, storage, and transportation are important for maintaining fruit quality. Hand-picked fruits have a longer reported shelf life than naturally fallen fruits, while refrigeration and freezing can extend the usable period of appropriately handled fruit.
Overall, the production guide supports representing durian cultivation in AgriDabaw-3D as a long-term orchard management cycle beginning with soil and climate assessment and continuing through propagation, land preparation, planting, maintenance, flowering, pollination, fruit thinning, pest and disease management, harvesting, and postharvest handling. The documented production parameters can serve as source-based gameplay variables for simulating how environmental conditions and farmer decisions influence durian growth, fruit production, quality, and farm management.
52. SAMPLE PROPER IN-TEXT CITATIONS
General durian production
Durian production requires suitable tropical environmental conditions, appropriate soil, quality planting materials, proper orchard establishment, and continuous crop management (Bureau of Plant Industry [BPI], n.d.).
Soil and climate
Durian is adapted to sandy-loam and clay-loam soils with high organic matter and slightly acidic conditions, and it grows under lowland tropical conditions with an optimum temperature of approximately 25–35°C (BPI, n.d.).
Planting and orchard establishment
Proper durian establishment includes nursery propagation, land preparation, appropriate planting distance, planting-hole preparation, temporary shading, irrigation, fertilization, and weed management (BPI, n.d.).
Pollination and fruit management
Because durian has a high rate of self-incompatibility, hand pollination is recommended to increase fruit setting, while fruit thinning is used to remove small and misshapen fruitlets and manage fruit load (BPI, n.d.).
Pest and disease management
Durian production requires continuous monitoring and management of insect pests and diseases, including fruit borers, psyllids, shot-hole borers, Phytophthora diseases, and other production problems (BPI, n.d.).
Harvest and postharvest
Durian should be harvested according to appropriate maturity indicators and handled carefully because the fruit is highly perishable and susceptible to quality deterioration after harvest (BPI, n.d.).
Combined paragraph
Durian production requires suitable tropical conditions, appropriate soil, quality planting materials, proper orchard establishment, and continuous management, including fertilization, irrigation, weed control, pruning, pollination, fruit thinning, and pest and disease management (Bureau of Plant Industry [BPI], n.d.). Proper harvesting and postharvest handling are also important because durian is highly perishable and may have a substantially shorter shelf life when allowed to fall naturally compared with properly hand-picked fruit. The production guide further identifies fruit age, peel color, aroma, sound, spine pliability, and suture development as important indicators for determining harvest maturity (BPI, n.d.).
