EGGPLANT
EGGPLANT PRODUCTION GUIDE  
1. EGGPLANT OVERVIEW
* Scientific name: Solanum melongena L.
* Eggplant is also known as:
   * Aubergine
   * Brinjal
   * Talong in Tagalog
   * Tarong in Ilocano
   * Bringhinas in Bisaya
* It belongs to the nightshade family (Solanaceae).
* It is described as a tender perennial plant.
* The plant is generally erect and bushy, and its stem may have a few spines.
* Leaves are large, ovate, and slightly lobed.
* Fruit shape and color vary according to variety.
* Eggplant is extensively cultivated throughout Asia and is one of the important vegetables produced in the Philippines.
2. USES AND FOOD VALUE
Eggplant is mainly cultivated for its immature fruits.
The young fruits may be:
* roasted;
* fried;
* stuffed;
* cooked;
* pickled;
* processed;
* incorporated into vegetable dishes such as pinakbet and dinengdeng;
* eaten raw when young.
The reference also identifies eggplant as a source of:
* potassium;
* iron;
* protein;
* vitamins A and B.
The skin contains nasunin, described in the source as an antioxidant and free-radical scavenger.
3. PRODUCTION IMPORTANCE IN THE PHILIPPINES
The provided Philippine production guide reports that:
* eggplant is among the country's leading vegetables in terms of production value;
* approximately 18,000 hectares were devoted to eggplant production in the referenced production data;
* major producing areas included:
   * Ilocos – 29%
   * Central Luzon – 14%
   * Southern Tagalog – 13%;
* another version of the guide reports more than 20,000 hectares and an average Philippine yield of 9.95 tons/ha.
The figures are source-specific production statistics and should therefore be cited with the particular guide rather than treated as current 2026 statistics.
4. SOIL REQUIREMENTS
Eggplant grows well in:
* sandy loam soil;
* clay loam soil;
* clayey soils can also be used according to the production guide.
The reported suitable soil pH is approximately:
5.5–6.8
Another production guide specifically identifies clay loam to clayey soils with pH around 5.5–6.0.
Soil preparation considerations
Good soil preparation is important because a well-pulverized soil:
* improves soil aeration;
* promotes root formation;
* facilitates establishment of transplanted seedlings.
Eggplant is also moderately sensitive to flooding and is particularly moisture-sensitive during:
* flowering;
* fruit enlargement.
5. CLIMATE REQUIREMENTS
Eggplant can be grown from:
* low elevations;
* to mid elevations;
* throughout the year.
Production is described as particularly favorable during the dry, cool months.
The reported temperature requirements include:
Growth stage
	Temperature
	Seed germination
	20–30°C
	Soil temperature
	24–32°C
	Plant growth
	21–30°C
	The guide also reports a water requirement of approximately:
35–40 mm per week.
6. VARIETY SELECTION
Variety selection should consider:
* high yield;
* resistance to insect pests;
* resistance to diseases;
* suitability to local climatic conditions;
* non-seasonal production;
* consumer acceptance.
The references list several varieties, including:
* Batangas Long
* Bulakena
* Casino
* Claveria KS
* Dumaguete
* Jackpot
* Long Purple
* Tanauan KS
The listed varieties are predominantly long and purple, although other forms and colors are also accepted in some regions.
Example variety maturity
The Studocu production guide lists approximate maturity periods:
Variety
	Fruit form/color
	Maturity
	Batangas Long
	Long, purple
	70 days
	Bulakena
	Long, purple
	70 days
	Casino
	Long/blunt, purple
	60 days
	Claveria KS
	Long, purple
	90 days
	Dumaguete
	Medium/long, purple
	70 days
	Jackpot
	Long, purple
	60 days
	Long Purple
	Long, purple
	70 days
	Tanauan KS
	Long, purple
	90 days
	The same table identifies some varieties as OP (open-pollinated) and others as F1 hybrids.
7. SEED REQUIREMENT
For one hectare, the production guide indicates approximately:
100 g of seed
while another guide specifies approximately:
200–250 g of seed/ha
The difference comes from the respective production-guide recommendations and should not be merged into one fixed requirement. For documentation, cite the particular production system or guide being followed.
8. SEEDLING PRODUCTION
Eggplant is preferably grown through transplanting rather than direct field sowing.
Two seedling-production methods are described:
1. Seedbed method
2. Seedling tray/box/potlet method
Seedbed method
For the ATI production guide:
* prepare approximately a 1 × 5 m seedbed;
* mix:
   * 1 part sand;
   * 1 part compost;
   * 1 part garden soil;
* place seeds in furrows;
* water gently after sowing;
* germination occurs in approximately 7–10 days;
* when seedlings develop 2–3 leaves, prick and transfer them;
* approximately 4–6 weeks after pricking, seedlings are ready for field planting.
The longer production guide provides another seedbed arrangement:
* 1 × 10 m seedbed
* approximately 15 cm high;
* fully exposed to sunlight;
* accessible to water;
* well-drained;
* protected with windbreaks.
The soil should be pulverized, leveled, and cleared of weeds and crop residues.
9. SEEDBED STERILIZATION
Before sowing, seedbeds may be sterilized to reduce:
* weed seeds;
* soil pathogens.
The procedure described is:
1. Water the prepared seedbed.
2. Spread approximately 3–5 cm of rice straw or rice hull over the surface.
3. Burn the material slowly.
4. Allow the soil to cool.
5. Remove unburned materials.
6. Remove excess ash.
7. Do not immediately sow after sterilization because burned materials may produce toxic effects.
8. Water the seedbed before sowing.
This provides a cleaner environment for seedling establishment.
10. SEED SOWING
For seedbed production:
* prepare furrows approximately 5 cm apart;
* soak the required seeds in clean water the night before sowing;
* sow immediately after soaking or air-dry the seeds before sowing;
* drill seeds evenly in the furrows;
* lightly cover with fine soil.
For seedling trays:
* use approximately 100- or 104-hole trays;
* use a medium consisting of:
   * 1 part compost;
   * 1 part carbonized rice hull;
   * 1 part garden soil;
* sterilize garden soil by roasting or drenching it with boiling water;
* sow one seed per hole;
* sow approximately 0.5 cm deep;
* cover with fine soil;
* sprinkle with water.
11. ADVANTAGES OF SEEDLING TRAYS OR POTLETS
The tray/potlet method:
* uses less seed;
* promotes uniform seedling growth;
* minimizes transplanting shock;
* reduces seedling mortality;
* reduces labor for thinning;
* reduces weeding labor;
* reduces watering labor;
* reduces pest-management labor.
12. SEEDLING CARE
Young seedlings should be protected from:
* excessive sunlight;
* excessive rain.
Temporary shade may be constructed using:
* plastic;
* cogon;
* talahib;
* coconut leaves.
The guide describes a shade structure approximately:
* 120 cm high on the east side
* 60 cm high on the west side.
13. SEEDLING WATERING
Water seedlings preferably:
in the morning
to help minimize damping-off.
Water should be sufficient to:
* maintain soil moisture;
* avoid excessive saturation.
Overwatering may:
* encourage damping-off;
* produce weak seedlings.
The ATI organic guide similarly emphasizes watering after sowing and transplanting.
14. STARTER FERTILIZATION OF SEEDLINGS
Approximately 10 days after emergence, the guide recommends a starter solution consisting of either:
* 1 tablespoon urea (45-0-0) in 4 L water; or
* 1 tablespoon ammonium sulfate (21-0-0) in 4 L water.
After applying the fertilizer solution:
* water the seedlings immediately;
* this helps prevent leaf injury.
15. HARDENING OF SEEDLINGS
Approximately one week before transplanting, seedlings should be hardened.
The process involves:
1. Gradually exposing seedlings to sunlight.
2. Beginning exposure up to approximately 10:00 a.m.
3. Increasing exposure each day.
4. Gradually reducing water amount and frequency.
5. Avoiding severe wilting.
6. Eventually allowing seedlings to tolerate full-day sunlight.
Hardening prepares seedlings for field conditions.
16. LAND PREPARATION
Prepare the land as early as possible to allow:
* weeds to decompose;
* crop residues/stubbles to decompose.
Recommended operations include:
* plowing;
* harrowing;
* repeated alternation of plowing and harrowing.
One guide recommends:
* plowing and harrowing 2–3 times alternately;
* approximately one-week intervals;
* plowing approximately 15–20 cm deep;
* harrowing twice to break soil clods;
* leveling the field.
The Studocu-based guide gives a shorter procedure of:
* plowing once;
* harrowing twice.
17. PLANTING BEDS AND FURROWS
Plastic mulch system
Construct beds:
* approximately 1 m wide;
* approximately 20 cm high;
* beds approximately 0.5 m apart.
The space between beds serves as:
* a pathway;
* an area for manual watering;
* a furrow-irrigation area.
Conventional system
Use:
* furrows during dry months;
* ridges during rainy months.
Spacing:
approximately 1 m apart.
18. MULCHING
Mulching is an important management practice because it:
* controls weeds;
* conserves soil moisture;
* reduces soil erosion;
* reduces fertilizer leaching;
* reflects sunlight;
* helps repel insect pests hiding beneath leaves.
Possible materials include:
* coconut coir dust;
* rice straw;
* rice hull;
* plastic mulch.
Organic mulch materials may be applied approximately:
3–5 cm thick
after hilling-up.
19. PLASTIC MULCH
Plastic mulch should be placed:
before transplanting.
Procedure:
1. Stretch the plastic over the planting bed.
2. Place the silver side upward.
3. Fasten the edges using bamboo slats.
4. Bamboo slats may be placed approximately 30 cm apart.
5. If bamboo slats are unavailable, cover the edges with soil.
20. PREPARING PLANTING HOLES IN PLASTIC MULCH
Planting distance:
0.5 m
Mark planting locations approximately:
10 cm from the edge on both sides of the plastic mulch.
Holes can be created using:
* serrated-lipped tin can;
* heated tin can;
* cutter.
The cutter method involves cutting intersecting lines approximately 7–10 cm long into a “+” or “X” shape. The resulting flaps can be folded during transplanting.
21. TRANSPLANTING
Eggplant seedlings are ready for transplanting approximately:
4–6 weeks after sowing
or approximately four weeks under the other production guide.
When pulling seedlings:
* handle carefully;
* avoid removing soil adhering to the roots;
* minimize root damage.
Minimal root damage allows seedlings to recover more easily.
22. PLANT SPACING
The ATI organic production guide recommends:
* 75 cm between rows
* 50 cm between hills
Another guide gives a planting distance of approximately:
0.5–1 m between plants, depending on variety.
For conventional production:
* single rows: approximately 100 cm between rows;
* double rows:
   * 50 cm between adjacent rows
   * 150 cm between double-row sets.
Double-row planting can provide developing fruits with greater protection from sun scald and can facilitate farm operations.
23. IMMEDIATE POST-TRANSPLANT CARE
After transplanting:
* water newly transplanted seedlings immediately;
* replace dead seedlings within approximately one week;
* maintain adequate soil moisture.
24. FERTILIZATION
The amount and type of fertilizer should ideally be determined by:
soil analysis
and consideration of:
* soil fertility;
* previous cropping patterns.
When soil analysis is unavailable, the production guide provides recommended rates.
25. ORGANIC FERTILIZATION
The ATI organic production guide recommends:
5 tons/ha vermicompost
during planting.
It also recommends:
* vermi-tea;
* probiotic liquid fertilizer.
These may be applied:
once a week
either:
* to foliage; or
* as a soil drench.
26. PREPARATION OF VERMI-TEA
The ATI organic guide describes:
1. Place approximately ¾ sack of vermicompost in a plastic drum.
2. Fill the drum with water.
3. Allow the mixture to ferment for approximately 7 days.
For commercial probiotic fertilizer:
* mix approximately 2–3 tablespoons per liter of water.
27. FERTILIZATION WITH PLASTIC MULCH
Basal application
For every 10 linear meters of planting row, apply:
* 0.5 kg processed chicken manure or commercial organic fertilizer;
* 0.4 kg ammonium phosphate (16-20-0);
* 0.4 kg ammonium sulfate (21-0-0);
* 0.1 kg muriate of potash (0-0-60).
Cover the fertilizer with soil or mix thoroughly with the soil.
Sidedress
Apply approximately:
170 ml fertilizer solution per hill
at:
* 3 WAT
* 5 WAT
* 9 WAT
* 13 WAT
* 17 WAT
using:
3 tbsp urea (46-0-0) per 15 L water.
During fruiting:
* 7 WAT
* 11 WAT
* 15 WAT
* 19 WAT
use:
3 tbsp muriate of potash (0-0-60) per 15 L water.
28. FERTILIZATION UNDER CONVENTIONAL PRODUCTION
Basal
For every 10 linear meters of planting furrow/ridge:
* 0.5 kg fully decomposed chicken manure or commercial organic fertilizer;
* 0.4 kg ammonium phosphate (16-20-0).
Sidedress
Fertilizer can be:
* dibbled approximately 25 cm from the plant;
* or distributed in a shallow furrow approximately 20 cm from the plant base.
The schedule includes:
Timing
	Fertilizer
	3 WAT
	6 g urea/hill or 120 g/10 linear m
	6 WAT
	3 g muriate of potash/hill or 60 g/10 linear m
	9 WAT
	6 g urea/hill or 120 g/10 linear m
	12 WAT
	3 g muriate of potash/hill or 60 g/10 linear m
	15 WAT
	8 g complete fertilizer (14-14-14)/hill or 160 g/10 linear m
	Foliar fertilizer may also be used according to its product label.
29. WATER MANAGEMENT
Eggplant requires approximately:
35–40 mm of water per week.
The crop is moderately sensitive to flooding, while insufficient or poorly managed moisture can be particularly problematic during:
* flowering;
* fruit enlargement.
The organic production guide recommends:
* watering immediately after transplanting;
* providing approximately 35–40 mm during dry-season conditions.
Another production guide recommends furrow irrigation approximately:
every 7–14 days
depending on:
* season;
* soil type.
30. WEED MANAGEMENT
Weeds should be controlled regularly.
Practices include:
* frequent cultivation/tillage;
* manual weed removal;
* mulching.
The conventional production guide recommends weeding approximately:
2–3 times during the growing season
or as needed.
31. PRUNING
Pruning is used to manage plant structure.
The organic production guide recommends removing:
* selected lateral shoots;
* excess branches;
* sickly branches.
In controlled-environment production, plants may be trained into:
* two main stems/leaders;
* side shoots are removed throughout production to maintain leader dominance.
Stem clips may be used to support stems to vine twine.
32. STAKING AND SUPPORT
The Philippine production guide recommends providing approximately:
1-meter-long stakes
to prevent:
lodging.
This is particularly important as plants develop and carry fruit.
33. POLLINATION
The greenhouse production reference states that eggplant is:
self-compatible.
Pollination assistance by:
* bumblebees;
* mechanical methods;
is not required for adequate fruit set, although assistance may improve yield under controlled-environment production.
34. MAJOR INSECT PESTS
The provided sources identify several important pests.
Fruit and shoot borer
The references identify fruit and shoot borer as an important eggplant pest.
Management includes:
* monitoring plants;
* removing damaged shoots;
* destroying affected material;
* avoiding conditions that encourage continuous pest buildup.
The production guide specifically recommends removing and burning damaged shoots together with larvae in cases of tip borer infestation.
Aphids
Aphids may cause:
* direct feeding damage;
* fruit deformation/crooking or curling in greenhouse production.
The greenhouse guide identifies aphid infestations as a significant concern.
Green leafhopper
The production guide identifies green leafhopper as an important insect pest.
Where incidence becomes severe, insecticides may be applied according to recommended rates.
35. PEST MANAGEMENT STRATEGIES
The Philippine guide recommends avoiding continuous monocropping because year-round monocropping contributes to increasing insect-pest problems.
Recommended strategies include:
* avoid monocropping;
* plant different varieties in an area;
* intercrop with other vegetables;
* use repellant plants such as:
   * garlic;
   * marigold;
   * ginger;
   * lemongrass;
* remove and destroy infested shoots;
* use insecticides when pest incidence is severe;
* maintain mulch.
The source indicates that with intercropping and mulching, pest incidence may be reduced and that eggplant can be grown economically without pesticides under appropriate management.
36. MAJOR DISEASES
The ATI organic eggplant guide identifies:
* bacterial wilt
* Phytophthora
* Phomopsis
* anthracnose
as major diseases of eggplant.
37. DISEASE MANAGEMENT
The organic guide identifies several approaches:
* mulching;
* solar sterilization of the seedbed/soil;
* maintaining appropriate crop management;
* botanical or organic solutions as described by the guide.
Solar sterilization may be performed for approximately:
one week before transplanting
to help minimize certain diseases.
38. BOTANICAL PEST MANAGEMENT
The ATI organic guide describes an OHN (Oriental Herbal Nutrient) solution as one management option, with application according to the guide's stated dilution.
It also gives an alternative botanical spray:
* 100 g pounded hot pepper
* 16 L water
* 1 tablespoon powdered soap
The mixture is used as a botanical spray.
39. GREENHOUSE / CONTROLLED-ENVIRONMENT PRODUCTION
The Produce Grower reference adds information specifically relevant to controlled-environment production.
Common greenhouse production systems include:
* Dutch buckets;
* gutter systems;
* substrate slabs;
* substrate bags.
Substrates identified include:
* perlite;
* lightweight expanded clay aggregate (LECA);
* rockwool;
* coconut coir.
The appropriate substrate depends on the production system.
40. GREENHOUSE SEEDLING PRODUCTION
Greenhouse eggplant is propagated by seed.
Seeds are initially sown in small propagation cubes, with approximately:
50–200 seedlings per flat.
The greenhouse reference reports germination at approximately:
74–79°F (23.3–26.1°C).
Seedlings can then be transplanted into:
* 4-inch rockwool blocks;
* coconut-coir blocks;
before being transferred into final production systems.
41. GREENHOUSE NUTRIENT MANAGEMENT
The greenhouse reference describes two nutrient phases:
1. Early phase
2. Full-production phase
After young plants are transplanted into the final production system, stronger nutrient solutions of approximately:
2.0–2.5 mS/cm
are used.
The source also describes:
* higher N:K ratios during seedling/young-plant development;
* a more balanced N:K ratio once fruit production begins.
42. GREENHOUSE TEMPERATURE
For controlled environments:
Day
Approximately:
75–80°F (23.9–26.7°C)
The source warns that temperatures should not exceed approximately:
85–90°F (29.4–32.2°C)
Night
Temperature should not fall below approximately:
65°F (18.3°C).
43. LIGHT
Eggplant is a fruiting vine crop that benefits from a high:
Daily Light Integral (DLI).
However, the greenhouse source states that supplemental lighting may not always be economically justified for full production because of capital and operating costs.
Supplemental lighting may be more economically viable during:
* seedling production;
* young-plant production;
where plant density is high.
44. CARBON DIOXIDE
The greenhouse production reference states that supplemental:
CO₂
can improve yield when:
* ventilation is minimal;
* the crop is grown in a controlled environment.
45. GREENHOUSE TRAINING
Eggplant can be trained similarly to pepper:
1. Allow the plant to bifurcate.
2. Establish two stems/leaders.
3. Train the two leaders using vine twine.
4. Remove side shoots throughout production.
5. Maintain the dominance of the main leaders.
6. Use stem clips for support.
The source specifically says not to use:
* tomato lean-and-lower training;
* cucumber drape/umbrella methods.
46. FLOWERING AND FRUIT DEVELOPMENT
The production cycle can be represented as:
Seed → Seedling → Hardening → Transplanting → Vegetative Growth → Flowering → Fruit Development → Harvest
Important management points during fruit development include:
* adequate moisture;
* appropriate fertilization;
* staking;
* pruning;
* pest monitoring;
* disease monitoring.
Moisture management becomes particularly important during:
* flowering;
* fruit enlargement.
47. HARVESTING
Eggplant should generally be harvested while fruits are:
* mature;
* shiny;
* still soft.
Harvesting before full ripeness is important for maintaining quality.
The greenhouse reference explains that allowing fruit to become fully mature can result in:
* tougher skin;
* harder seeds;
* pithy texture;
* bitter flavor.
48. HARVEST FREQUENCY
The ATI organic production guide specifies:
* first harvest approximately 60–80 days after transplanting;
* harvest approximately twice per week;
* harvesting can continue for approximately 3–5 months.
Another Philippine production guide gives:
3–6 months
as a possible harvesting period.
Frequent harvesting can also help reduce damage from fruit borers.
49. HARVESTING DAMAGED FRUITS
The production guide recommends harvesting:
* marketable fruits;
* deformed fruits;
* damaged fruits.
Removing all fruits helps reduce the opportunity for:
* pest development;
* disease spread.
50. POSTHARVEST HANDLING
The ATI organic production guide states that eggplant may be stored for up to approximately:
10 days
under:
* 85–90% relative humidity
* 10–13°C
The greenhouse production source gives a similar short-term-storage concept, stating that storage of less than two weeks is acceptable. It also warns that eggplant is sensitive to chilling injury and gives approximately:
50–54°F (10–12.2°C)
as the preferred storage range.
51. IMPORTANT POSTHARVEST PRINCIPLE
Eggplant should not be allowed to become fully ripe before harvest because excessive maturity can reduce market quality through:
* tougher skin;
* increased seed development;
* pithy texture;
* bitter flavor.
Therefore, harvest timing is a critical production decision.

	55. SYNTHESIS FOR DOCUMENTATION / CAPSTONE PAPER
Eggplant production requires suitable soil, climate, quality planting materials, proper land preparation, and continuous crop management. The production references identify sandy-loam and clay-loam soils as suitable, with a reported soil pH range of approximately 5.5–6.8. Eggplant can be cultivated from low to mid elevations, while suitable temperature conditions vary according to the growth stage. Proper seedling production involves preparing a suitable seedbed or seedling tray, maintaining adequate moisture, protecting seedlings from excessive environmental conditions, applying appropriate starter nutrients, and hardening seedlings before field transplanting. Field establishment includes land preparation, bed and furrow construction, appropriate planting distance, fertilization, irrigation, weed management, mulching, staking, and pruning.
Continuous crop protection is also an important component of eggplant production. The provided references identify fruit and shoot borers, aphids, and green leafhoppers as important insect pests, while bacterial wilt, Phytophthora, Phomopsis, and anthracnose are identified as major diseases in the organic production guide. Recommended management practices include avoiding continuous monocropping, intercropping, mulching, removal and destruction of infested plant parts, and appropriate pest-control measures when necessary. Water management is particularly important because eggplant requires approximately 35–40 mm of water per week and is sensitive to moisture conditions during flowering and fruit enlargement.
Harvesting should be conducted while fruits are mature, shiny, and still soft rather than fully ripe. Frequent harvesting can reduce fruit-borer damage and allows the crop to continue producing over an extended harvest period. The ATI organic guide reports harvesting approximately 60–80 days after transplanting and twice weekly for approximately three to five months. Proper postharvest handling is necessary because eggplant is perishable and sensitive to chilling injury; the references identify approximately 10–13°C and high relative humidity as suitable short-term storage conditions.
For AgriDabaw-3D, these production requirements can be translated into a localized eggplant farming simulation involving soil suitability, temperature, water availability, seedling preparation, transplanting, fertilizer application, mulching, weed management, staking, pruning, pest and disease events, fruit development, harvesting, and postharvest storage. The production information provides measurable farming conditions that can be represented as gameplay variables and decision points while maintaining the documented agricultural practices from the selected references.

MANGOSTEEN
MANGOSTEEN PRODUCTION GUIDE 
1. MANGOSTEEN OVERVIEW
* Scientific name: Garcinia mangostana L.
* Mangosteen is a tropical fruit belonging to the family Guttiferae in the Department of Agriculture production guide; the research article identifies it under Clusiaceae.
* It is described as one of the most highly flavored tropical fruits and is commonly known as the “queen of fruits.”
* The crop originated in the Malay Archipelago/Moluccas region and has long been cultivated in Southeast Asia, including the Philippines.
* Philippine production is particularly associated with parts of Mindanao, including:
   * Zamboanga del Norte
   * Davao del Norte
   * Misamis Occidental
   * Davao City
   * Agusan del Sur.
2. FRUIT CHARACTERISTICS
* The mangosteen tree is a small, evergreen, slow-growing tree with an erect form and pyramidal crown.
* Homegrown describes mature trees as approximately 6–20 m high.
* The bark is dark brown to nearly black and may flake.
* The inner bark contains yellow, gummy, bitter latex.
* Flowers are approximately 4–5 cm wide.
* Flowers may be male or hermaphrodite on the same tree.
* Fruits are approximately the size of a small orange.
* The fruit is round or somewhat flattened at the ends.
* The rind is thick and hard, becoming deep red as the fruit matures.
* The edible flesh is white and divided into segments resembling a mandarin orange.
3. FOOD VALUE AND USES
The DA production guide reports:
* edible portion: approximately 26%;
* moisture: 79.7 g;
* energy: 71 calories;
* protein: 0.7 g;
* fat: 0.8 g;
* carbohydrates: 17.3 g;
* fiber: 1.3 g;
* ash: 0.2 g;
* calcium: 18 mg;
* phosphorus: 11 mg;
* iron: 0.30 mg;
* thiamin: 0.06 mg;
* riboflavin: 0.01 mg;
* niacin: 0.40 mg;
* ascorbic acid: 2.0 mg.
The aril is reported at approximately 25–30% of the fruit and contains approximately:
* 19.8% soluble solids;
* 4.3% reducing sugar;
* 17.5% total sugar.
The fruit may be:
* eaten fresh;
* chilled before eating;
* processed into jam;
* processed into sweetened syrup;
* made into candy;
* used in preserves or as a topping for ice cream or sherbet.
The seeds are described as having a nutty flavor, while the rind contains pectin and tannin. The DA guide also reports that the seed contains approximately 30% oil.
4. SOIL REQUIREMENTS
Mangosteen requires fertile soil with good moisture availability and drainage.
The DA production guide describes suitable soil as:
* rich;
* porous;
* deep;
* moist;
* well drained.
It also describes heavy clay containing a generous mixture of sand and silt as suitable, with a water table at approximately 2 m.
The Caraga guide specifies:
* fertile soil;
* well-drained conditions;
* somewhat acidic clay loam;
* high organic matter.
It specifically states that mangosteen is not suitable for:
* limestone soils;
* sandy alluvial soils;
* very sandy soils with low humus.
Soil pH
The references provide:
* pH 5.5–6.8 in the DA Caraga guide;
* pH 5.5–6.5 in the Homegrown guide.
For capstone documentation, it is safest to state:
Mangosteen performs well in acidic soils, with the supplied references indicating approximately pH 5.5–6.8.
5. CLIMATE REQUIREMENTS
The DA production guide states that mangosteen thrives best under a:
* warm;
* humid environment.
The guide identifies an ideal temperature of approximately:
20–30°C.
The Caraga guide identifies:
* approximately 25–35°C;
* relative humidity above 80%;
* generally well-distributed rainfall;
* no prolonged dry period under normal growing conditions.
The Homegrown guide provides:
* temperature: 22–35°C;
* rainfall: approximately 2,000 mm;
* relative humidity: 70% RH.
Because these values come directly from different supplied references, they should not be presented as one artificially combined exact range.
6. DRY PERIOD AND FLOWER INDUCTION
The Caraga guide provides an important production condition:
* approximately 15–30 days of dry weather can contribute to flower induction;
* this is followed by rainfall;
* the guide associates flowering conditions with approximately 25–35°C and RH above 80%.
The guide also notes that:
* a prolonged dry period of approximately one month;
* followed by heavy rainfall;
can induce flowering.
This is an important variable for a climate-based farming simulation because water availability can affect flowering rather than simply functioning as a general growth requirement.
7. ELEVATION
The DA guide states that mangosteen grows well from:
0–500 meters above sea level.
The Caraga guide similarly associates mangosteen with lower elevations around 500 masl, while noting that it can be planted at higher elevations but growth becomes slower.
Homegrown provides a broader range of:
0–2,500 ft MSL,
which is approximately 762 meters above sea level.
8. SUNLIGHT AND SHADE
Young mangosteen plants require protection from excessive sunlight.
The Homegrown guide states that the crop:
* requires semi-shade initially;
* can gradually receive partial sunlight;
* can eventually receive full sunlight.
The Caraga guide recommends providing shade to newly transplanted seedlings and retaining the cover for approximately:
1–2 years,
with gradual removal as the plant becomes able to tolerate greater sunlight.
The DA guide also states that planting young mangosteen in areas receiving light shade/shimmers helps establishment.
9. PROPAGATION AND PLANTING MATERIAL
Mangosteen is commonly established using seeds.
The Caraga guide recommends:
1. Collect seeds from ripe fruits.
2. Select seeds weighing more than 1 g.
3. Sow freshly extracted seeds immediately.
4. Use fresh seed to obtain better germination.
The research study by Haba et al. (2021) provides experimental evidence that:
* fresh seeds germinate better and faster than dried or frozen seeds;
* seed mass significantly affects germination;
* seeds greater than 2 g produced the best germination performance;
* seed germination is influenced by seed condition, seed mass, and substrate.
10. SEED GERMINATION
DA Caraga procedure
The recommended seedbed may be made from:
* wood; or
* cement.
The sowing medium consists of:
3 parts sand : 1 part soil.
The medium should:
* retain moisture;
* not dry out quickly;
* remain well drained;
* not become waterlogged.
Sowing depth and spacing
Seeds should be:
* planted approximately 5–10 mm deep;
* spaced approximately 2–3 cm apart;
* covered with fine sand.
The Caraga guide reports germination approximately 30 days after sowing.
11. FINDINGS FROM THE HABA ET AL. (2021) GERMINATION STUDY
Haba et al. examined:
* seed condition;
* seed mass;
* germination substrate;
* seedling growth in nursery conditions.
The study compared:
* fresh seeds;
* dried seeds;
* frozen seeds;
and seeds of different masses, including:
* less than 1 g;
* more than 2 g.
It also evaluated substrates including:
* Petri dishes;
* forest soil;
* lowland soil;
* construction sand.
Seed condition
Fresh seeds performed best.
Reported germination timing:
* fresh seeds: 16 days;
* frozen seeds: 18 days;
* shade-dried seeds: 20 days.
After 30 days, fresh seeds recorded approximately 80% germination.
Seed mass
Seeds greater than 2 g showed substantially better seedling growth.
After six months:
* large-seed seedlings: approximately 12.07 ± 1.42 cm average height;
* small-seed seedlings: approximately 6.19 ± 0.95 cm.
The study therefore supports selecting heavier seeds for improved nursery performance.
Germination substrate
The highest germination rate in the laboratory experiment was reported for seeds germinated in Petri dishes, approximately 99%.
For nursery seedling growth, lowland soil produced better growth than forest soil and construction sand.
After six months:
* lowland soil: approximately 10.26 ± 1.4 cm;
* forest soil: approximately 8.72 ± 1.23 cm;
* sand: approximately 5.39 ± 0.55 cm.
The corresponding reported monthly growth rates were approximately:
* lowland soil: 1.71 cm/month;
* forest soil: 1.45 cm/month;
* sand: 0.88 cm/month.
12. NURSERY MANAGEMENT
After germination:
1. Transfer germinated seeds from the seedbed.
2. Place them into polyethylene bags.
3. Use a 3:1 sand-to-soil mixture.
4. Water seedlings regularly.
5. Maintain appropriate moisture without waterlogging.
6. Provide shade during early growth.
The Caraga guide recommends transferring seedlings to the field at approximately:
24–36 months old.
The older DA production guide describes seedlings as suitable for field transplanting at approximately:
2 years old and approximately 25–30 cm tall.
13. LAND PREPARATION
Before establishing the orchard:
1. Clear the land.
2. Remove large plants.
3. Remove as many roots and stumps as possible.
4. Plow the land.
5. Harrow the soil until appropriate tilth is obtained.
6. Incorporate organic matter when available.
The Caraga guide recommends incorporating organic materials approximately:
1–2 months before planting.
The DA production guide similarly recommends:
* removing stumps and roots;
* deep plowing once or twice;
* several harrowings;
* achieving suitable soil tilth before planting.
14. PLANTING DISTANCE
The DA production guide recommends:
8 × 8 m to 10 × 10 m.
At 8 × 8 m, approximately:
156 seedlings/ha
are required.
The Homegrown guide provides:
20 × 20 ft
as its planting spacing.
15. PLANTING HOLE
The DA production guide recommends:
0.6 × 0.6 × 0.6 m
planting holes.
The holes should be filled with farm manure before planting.
The Caraga guide gives a minimum hole size of:
12 × 12 × 12 inches
and recommends incorporating:
1–2 kg organic material
approximately 1–2 months before planting.
Homegrown provides a larger pit specification of:
3 × 3 × 3 ft.
16. FIELD TRANSPLANTING
When transplanting:
1. Carefully remove the seedling from its container.
2. Avoid disturbing or damaging the root system.
3. Place the seedling in the center of the planting hole.
4. Gradually fill the hole with loose topsoil.
5. Gently press the soil around the plant.
6. Provide support using a wooden stake if needed.
7. Provide shade after transplanting.
8. Maintain adequate soil moisture.
17. BEST PLANTING PERIOD
The references indicate that planting is best associated with the rainy season.
The DA guide states that transplanting is most suitable:
just after the rainy season has started.
The Caraga guide states that planting may be done in any month provided water is available, but the preferred period is:
the beginning of the rainy season.
18. SUPPORT AND INITIAL SHADE
Newly planted seedlings may be supported using:
* wooden sticks;
* stakes.
Shade should be provided to newly transplanted plants.
The Caraga guide recommends maintaining shade for approximately:
1–2 years,
then gradually reducing it to expose the plant to increasing sunlight.
19. WEED MANAGEMENT
Mangosteen is slow-growing during its early development, making young seedlings vulnerable to competition from weeds.
Recommended practices include:
* regular weeding;
* ring weeding;
* loosening the soil;
* controlling weeds around young plants;
* maintaining the area between rows.
The DA guide specifically recommends:
ring-weeding at a 1-meter radius.
Areas between rows may also be plowed for weed control and cultivation.
20. MULCHING
Mulching helps conserve soil moisture during dry periods.
The Caraga guide recommends materials such as:
* coconut husk;
* grasses.
These can be placed around young plants to conserve moisture during the dry season.
21. IRRIGATION
Mangosteen requires adequate soil moisture.
The DA guide recommends artificial irrigation during dry months and states that:
* plants should be watered immediately after transplanting;
* irrigation should continue when rainfall is inadequate;
* high soil moisture should be maintained.
However:
Standing water around seedling roots can kill young plants.
Therefore, irrigation must maintain moisture without creating prolonged waterlogging.
The Caraga guide recommends installing an irrigation system to ensure water availability during dry months.
It also notes that mangosteen has:
* few root hairs;
* a requirement for abundant soil moisture.
22. WATER REQUIREMENT FROM HOMEGROWN
Homegrown provides the following daily water quantities:
Tree age
	Water/tree/day
	1 year
	10 L
	2 years
	15 L
	3 years
	20 L
	These values are specifically from the Homegrown guide and should be identified as such rather than treated as a universal irrigation standard.
23. PRUNING
Mangosteen generally does not require extensive pruning during its early non-bearing stage.
For young trees:
* remove dead branches;
* avoid unnecessary pruning.
For mangosteen trees approximately 8 years old or older:
* remove small branches growing inward;
* remove branches from old trees that are no longer productive;
* pruning may be used to stimulate fruit production.
Homegrown also identifies:
* pruning: yes;
* training: yes.
24. FERTILIZATION
Mangosteen responds well to organic manuring.
The DA production guide recommends:
* diluted organic fertilizer;
* slowly available nutrients;
* nitrogenous fertilizer to stimulate vegetative growth.
Fertilizer should generally be applied:
* around the canopy;
* in several holes around the tree;
* followed by covering with soil.
This helps reduce:
* volatilization;
* nutrient runoff during heavy rainfall.
25. DA FERTILIZER PROGRAM
At planting
Apply:
200–250 g complete fertilizer/tree
The guide specifies placement approximately:
* 3 inches below the roots;
* 5 inches to the side of the seedling.
Young trees
Apply:
300–500 g of 14-14-14 or 12-24-12
in two equal doses.
The guide additionally describes:
200–300 g urea (45-0-0)
and recommends applying the first dose:
* at the beginning of the rainy season;
and the second:
* at the end of the rainy season.
Fruit-bearing trees
Apply in two equal doses:
1.5–3.0 kg of 14-14-14 or 12-24-12
plus:
200–300 g muriate of potash (0-0-60).
Fertilizer quantities should increase gradually as:
* trees become larger;
* fruit production increases.
26. SOIL-BASED FERTILIZER ADJUSTMENT
The Caraga guide emphasizes that fertilizer amounts should increase as the tree grows.
It also recommends using:
soil analysis
as a basis for determining whether nutrient management needs to be adjusted.
This is particularly useful for a farming simulation because fertilizer decisions can be linked to the player's soil condition rather than simply applying a fixed amount every season.
27. SULFUR, CALCIUM AND FOLIAR FERTILIZER
The Caraga guide states that sulfur and calcium fertilization can help support:
* vegetative growth;
* flowering;
* fruit development.
It also describes SQL (sulfur-quick lime) foliar fertilizer applications together with NPK fertilizer for increasing off-season fruiting.
The guide provides SQL concentrations ranging from approximately:
* 1:55 / 19 ppm
* to 1:35 / 30 ppm.
These concentrations should be retained as source-specific technical information rather than generalized to all production systems.
28. INTERCROPPING
Intercropping is particularly useful during the early years when mangosteen trees are still small.
The Caraga guide recommends short-duration crops such as:
banana
to provide shade to young mangosteen seedlings.
The DA guide lists examples of intercrops and companion plants:
* peanut;
* other leguminous field crops;
* abaca;
* banana;
* marang;
* lanzones;
* dapdap;
* durian.
Some companion trees can provide partial shade.
As mangosteen trees become larger, the available unshaded space decreases, making extensive intercropping less practical.
29. GROWTH AND JUVENILE PERIOD
Mangosteen is a slow-growing crop.
The Caraga guide describes a juvenile phase that may last:
12–20 years,
but also states that good management may result in fruiting as early as:
5–7 years after planting.
The DA production guide states that ordinary mangosteen trees may flower in:
10–15 years,
while asexually propagated trees may bear fruit in:
8–9 years.
The Caraga guide's later production section reports that under very good conditions, trees may bear around:
6–8 years after planting.
These values should be presented as source-reported production timelines, because actual bearing age depends on planting material and management.
30. FLOWERING
The DA guide reports that mangosteen requires approximately:
5–6 months
from flowering to fruit ripening.
The Caraga guide identifies a dry period followed by rainfall as an important flowering trigger.
It also notes that mangosteen may:
* flower twice in one year under some conditions.
31. POLLINATION
Homegrown describes mangosteen as:
self-pollinated.
It also describes the crop as producing fruit through parthenocarpy, meaning fruit development can occur without pollination.
Therefore, for documentation, it is appropriate to state:
Mangosteen has a distinctive reproductive biology, with the supplied Homegrown source describing it as self-pollinated and parthenocarpic.
32. PESTS
The DA production guide identifies several common mangosteen pests:
* mites;
* aphids;
* fructifier ants;
* mealybugs;
* tussock caterpillars;
* coconut scales.
Coconut scales may form colonies underneath leaves and cause:
* patchy leaf yellowing;
* impaired plant growth.
33. DISEASES
The DA guide reports:
* sooty molds;
* anthracnose;
* bacterial leaf sheath disease.
As a preventive measure, the guide states that plants may be sprayed two to four times per year with common fungicides at recommended dosages and emphasizes following the product label.
34. GUMMOSIS
The Caraga guide identifies gummosis as a production problem.
A visible symptom is:
* yellow gum or latex emerging from the fruit skin.
The guide associates the problem with:
* insects that pierce the fruit and damage latex vessels;
* excessive direct heat striking the fruit.
Therefore, environmental stress and insect damage can both be represented as factors affecting fruit quality.
35. HARVEST MATURITY
The Caraga guide identifies the following maturity indicators:
* fruit skin becomes reddish-purple;
* no remaining gum is present on the skin;
* flesh segments separate easily from the rind;
* soluble solids reach approximately 17–20%.
The guide gives a maturity period of approximately:
113–119 days from flower opening.
The fruit also changes from:
green → pinkish-purple
during maturation.
36. HARVESTING TIME
The DA production guide states that harvesting normally occurs:
August–October.
However, the exact harvest period may vary with production location, flowering time, and season.
37. HARVESTING METHODS
Possible harvesting methods include:
Handpicking
* Pick mature fruit directly.
* Preserve the fruit carefully.
* Avoid dropping fruit because the pericarp can be damaged.
Pole harvesting
A long pole can be equipped with:
* a hook;
* a catching basket.
This allows fruit to be collected without dropping directly onto the ground.
The Caraga guide recommends harvesting:
* early in the morning; or
* late in the afternoon.
It also states that fruit should be harvested every:
2–3 days
because not all fruits mature at the same time.
38. CAREFUL HARVESTING
Mangosteen should be handled carefully because:
* the pericarp can be injured;
* mechanical damage can reduce quality;
* damaged fruit may deteriorate more quickly.
The DA guide specifically recommends handpicking because the slightly soft pericarp is easily injured if the fruit falls.
In Sulu, the guide reports that fruits are handpicked with the:
peduncle intact
and bound in groups of approximately:
15 fruits per cluster.
39. POSTHARVEST HANDLING
After harvesting:
1. Transport fruit carefully from the farm.
2. Minimize physical injuries.
3. Bring fruit to a packing house.
4. Remove immature and damaged fruit.
5. Remove foreign materials.
6. Grade according to quality and size.
7. Pack in suitable containers.
8. Store under appropriate temperature conditions.
9. Transport carefully to market.
A packing house may be a simple shed, according to the Caraga guide.
40. SORTING
During selection, remove:
* immature fruit;
* damaged fruit;
* unsuitable fruit;
* stones or other foreign materials.
The objective is to ensure that only acceptable fruit proceeds to grading and packaging.
41. GRADING
The Caraga guide classifies fruit according to size:
Grade
	Number of fruits per kilogram
	Small
	16–18 fruits/kg
	Medium
	12–14 fruits/kg
	Large
	8–10 fruits/kg
	42. PACKAGING
Selected fruits may be packed using:
* wooden crates;
* cartons.
The objective is to protect the fruit from physical damage during:
* handling;
* storage;
* transportation.
43. STORAGE
The Caraga guide states that mangosteen may be stored at approximately:
8–10°C
for up to:
8 weeks.
This should be presented as the storage condition reported by the supplied production guide.
44. PROCESSING
Mangosteen may be processed into:
* jam;
* sweetened syrup;
* candy;
* preserves.
The DA guide provides a mangosteen preserve procedure using:
* 200 pieces mangosteen
* 1 kg white sugar
Procedure:
1. Remove the pericarp.
2. Remove the seeds using pressure with the thumb.
3. Add 1 kg white sugar.
4. Boil for approximately 35–50 minutes.
5. Stir continuously until the mixture becomes light brown.
6. Pack into sterilized bottles.
45. PRODUCTION YIELD
The Caraga guide states that yield varies:
* from tree to tree;
* from season to season.
It also notes that fruiting may sometimes occur with a year interval.
Reported production levels include:
* approximately 100 fruits/tree during light fruiting;
* approximately 500–600 or more fruits/tree during heavy fruiting.

	49. SYNTHESIS FOR DOCUMENTATION / CAPSTONE PAPER
Mangosteen (Garcinia mangostana L.) production requires suitable soil, warm and humid environmental conditions, adequate moisture, quality planting materials, and careful orchard establishment. The supplied Department of Agriculture sources describe mangosteen as performing well in fertile, acidic, organic-matter-rich, well-drained soils, while unsuitable conditions include prolonged waterlogging, limestone soils, and low-humus sandy soils. The crop is generally associated with low elevations and requires protection from excessive sunlight during its early growth. Proper land preparation, appropriate planting distance, organic matter incorporation, irrigation, mulching, weed control, fertilization, and gradual exposure to sunlight are important for successful establishment.
Seed quality is particularly important during nursery establishment. The Caraga guide recommends freshly extracted seeds weighing more than 1 g, while Haba et al. (2021) found that fresh seeds and seeds weighing more than 2 g produced better germination and subsequent seedling growth. The study reported approximately 80% germination for fresh seeds after 30 days and found that lowland soil supported stronger nursery seedling growth than forest soil and construction sand. These findings emphasize the importance of selecting appropriate planting material and nursery substrates when establishing mangosteen seedlings.
After field establishment, mangosteen requires continuous maintenance because of its slow early growth. Management includes weed control, irrigation during dry periods, mulching, appropriate fertilization, limited pruning, and protection from pests and diseases. The crop requires adequate soil moisture, although excessive standing water can damage young roots. Flower induction is associated in the Caraga guide with a dry period followed by rainfall, while regular irrigation is important during fruit development. Major production problems identified in the supplied references include mites, aphids, mealybugs, scales, caterpillars, sooty molds, anthracnose, bacterial leaf sheath disease, and gummosis.
Harvesting requires careful monitoring because mangosteen fruits do not all mature simultaneously. The Caraga guide identifies approximately 113–119 days from flower opening as a maturity period and describes reddish-purple skin, absence of gum on the rind, easy separation of the flesh segments, and soluble solids of approximately 17–20% as maturity indicators. Harvesting every two to three days allows mature fruits to be collected while minimizing the presence of immature fruit. After harvest, fruits should be carefully sorted, graded, packaged, stored, and transported to minimize mechanical injury and preserve quality. The guide classifies fruits into small, medium, and large categories and reports storage at 8–10°C for up to eight weeks.
50. COMBINED PARAGRAPH FOR YOUR CAPSTONE
Mangosteen production requires suitable acidic, fertile, organic-matter-rich, and well-drained soil, a warm and humid environment, quality planting materials, and careful orchard establishment and maintenance (Department of Agriculture–High Value Crops Development Program [DA-HVCDP], 2022). Proper production includes fresh and sufficiently heavy seed selection, nursery management, appropriate planting distance, shade protection, irrigation, mulching, fertilization, weed management, and limited pruning. Research by Haba et al. (2021) further demonstrated that seed freshness and seed mass significantly influence germination and seedling growth, with fresh seeds and seeds greater than 2 g producing better germination and nursery performance. During production, maintaining adequate moisture, managing the dry period associated with flower induction, monitoring pests and diseases, and protecting fruits from environmental and mechanical damage are important for crop development and quality (Department of Agriculture–Caraga Region, 2018). Harvesting should be based on appropriate maturity indicators, including reddish-purple fruit coloration and approximately 113–119 days from flower opening, followed by careful sorting, grading, packaging, storage, and transportation.

COCONUT
COCONUT PRODUCTION GUIDE 
1. COCONUT PRODUCTION OVERVIEW
* Coconut is identified in the Department of Education module as Cocos nucifera.
* It is a perennial crop that can be grown in many types of well-drained soil.
* Coconut may be harvested as:
   * tender nuts, approximately 7–8 months old; or
   * mature nuts, approximately 11½–12 months old.
* Under favorable environmental conditions, the module reports an average annual yield of approximately:
   * 80–150 nuts per tree, or
   * 2–4 tons/ha of copra.
* Flowering under favorable conditions may begin within approximately 4–5 years for dwarf and tall hybrids, while the module's variety discussion identifies reproductive maturity of tall varieties at approximately 5–7 years and dwarf varieties at approximately 3–4 years.
* Coconut palms require substantial amounts of potassium and magnesium, and the module also identifies the importance of phosphorus/rock phosphate and lime where soils are very acidic. (Department of Education [DepEd], n.d.).
2. SITE SELECTION REQUIREMENTS
A suitable coconut site should have:
* adequate water supply;
* good drainage;
* accessibility to roads;
* accessibility to transportation;
* accessibility to markets;
* suitable soil depth;
* suitable soil fertility;
* appropriate sunlight exposure.
The Department of Education module emphasizes that coconut can grow in almost all types of well-drained soil and can also be planted in hilly areas when adequate water, road access, market access, and sufficient soil for root development are available.
TNAU further identifies suitable soils such as:
* red sandy loam;
* laterite;
* alluvial soils.
TNAU recommends deep, well-drained soils and states that shallow soils over hard rock, waterlogged lowlands, and heavy clayey soils should be avoided.
3. SOIL REQUIREMENTS
Suitable soil
Coconut performs well in:
* well-drained soils;
* red sandy loam;
* lateritic soils;
* alluvial soils;
* fertile soils with adequate moisture-holding capacity.
The Department of Education module states that coconut can grow in almost all soil types if drainage is adequate.
TNAU specifies:
* minimum desirable soil depth of approximately 1.2 m;
* deep soils of approximately 1.5 m or more are preferred;
* adequate water-holding capacity;
* good drainage.
TNAU reports that coconut can tolerate approximately pH 5.2–8.6.
The Department of Education module provides a narrower optimum range:
* pH 5.5–6.5 for optimum growth;
* approximately pH 5.0–8.0 as a tolerance range.
Soil conditions to avoid
Avoid:
* shallow soils over hard rock;
* poorly drained heavy soils;
* waterlogged areas;
* prolonged stagnant water;
* areas with inadequate water supply.
Waterlogged coconut areas may show:
* yellowing leaves;
* stunted growth;
* poor yield. (DepEd, n.d.).
4. SOIL SAMPLING AND ANALYSIS
Soil sampling is important because it determines:
* soil pH;
* nutrient status;
* fertilizer requirements.
The Department of Education module provides the following sampling procedure:
1. Map the farm.
2. Divide the farm into relatively uniform sampling areas.
3. Consider:
   * cropping history;
   * previous lime application;
   * previous fertilizer treatments;
   * slope;
   * erosion;
   * soil texture;
   * soil color.
4. Collect spot samples from each sampling area.
5. Take approximately 5–10 pits in each sampling area.
6. Clear litter and vegetation from the soil surface.
7. Collect the soil samples.
8. Pulverize and thoroughly mix the samples from the same sampling area.
9. Remove stones and fresh leaves.
10. Place approximately ½ kg of composite soil sample in a clean plastic bag.
11. Label the sample properly.
12. Submit the sample for chemical analysis.
The results should be used as the basis for fertilizer application. (DepEd, n.d.).
5. CLIMATE REQUIREMENTS
TNAU identifies coconut as a tropical crop that grows well under hot conditions.
The source reports:
* preferred latitude: approximately 20°N to 20°S for Tamil Nadu and Kerala;
* commercial cultivation generally up to approximately 600 m above mean sea level, although coconut may occur at higher elevations;
* temperature approximately 20–32°C;
* mean annual temperature around 27°C is identified as favorable for vigorous growth and good yield;
* yield decreases when mean temperature falls below approximately 21°C;
* approximately 1,000 mm rainfall may be sufficient if evenly distributed;
* up to approximately 3,000 mm rainfall may be suitable when drainage is good;
* prolonged dry periods require irrigation.
TNAU also notes that irrigation during summer has a positive relationship with nut yield.
The Department of Education module states that coconut can also be grown in hilly areas up to approximately 5,000 feet above sea level, provided adequate water, road access, market access, and well-drained soil are available.
6. WATER REQUIREMENTS
Coconut is commonly rainfed.
The Department of Education module states that irrigation is generally unnecessary under adequate rainfall, but irrigation is desirable during dry periods when monthly rainfall is below approximately 150 mm, particularly to stabilize production.
For young palms, TNAU recommends:
* approximately 45 liters of water per seedling;
* once every 4 days;
* during dry summer months;
* particularly during the first two years after planting.
Proper drainage is equally important where waterlogging occurs.
7. COCONUT TYPES
The Department of Education module identifies two principal coconut types:
Tall
Characteristics include:
* wider geographic distribution;
* more commercially distributed;
* greater height increase;
* highly cross-pollinated;
* later reproductive maturity;
* approximately 5–7 years to reproductive maturity;
* expected lifespan of more than 50 years;
* high genetic variability;
* generally less sensitive to adverse conditions;
* average cultural requirements;
* strong leaf and bunch attachment.
Dwarf
Characteristics include:
* less widely distributed;
* more self-pollinated;
* lower annual height increase;
* earlier reproductive maturity;
* approximately 3–4 years to reproductive maturity;
* expected lifespan of less than 50 years;
* lower genetic variability within cultivar;
* greater sensitivity to adverse conditions;
* higher cultural requirements;
* more fragile leaf and bunch attachment.
(DepEd, n.d.).
8. EXAMPLES OF COCONUT VARIETIES
The module describes Philippine coconut materials including:
* Bago-Oshiro Tall;
* Baybay Tall;
* Laguna Tall;
* Makapuno;
* other tall varieties;
* dwarf varieties;
* F1 hybrids.
Bago-Oshiro Tall
* approximately 32% average variability;
* approximately 210 g copra per nut;
* may produce approximately 100 nuts/palm/year when properly maintained.
Baybay Tall
* approximately 288 g copra/nut;
* relatively thin husk;
* good uniform stand;
* fast germination;
* early flowering;
* robust growth;
* approximately 88 nuts/palm/year;
* capable of producing nearly 3.5 tons copra/ha under average rainfall and soil fertility.
Laguna Tall
* approximately 156 medium-sized nuts/palm/year;
* approximately 195 g copra/nut;
* good fruit quality.
Makapuno
* characterized by soft endosperm and viscous liquid;
* requires special propagation methods because of abnormal endosperm development;
* the module discusses embryo culture as a propagation method.
(DepEd, n.d.).
9. SELECTION OF PLANTING MATERIALS
Selection should consider:
* yield;
* resistance;
* adaptability;
* variety characteristics;
* quality of planting material.
The Department of Education module emphasizes that improved genetic materials combined with appropriate cultural management and fertilizer application can help maintain high production over time.
10. SELECTION OF SEEDNUTS
The module recommends preparing planting materials carefully.
Important practices include:
* select mature seednuts;
* avoid immature seednuts;
* use appropriate mother palms;
* choose uniform and fast-germinating materials;
* remove seednuts that fail to germinate within the specified nursery period.
TNAU's coconut recommendations likewise emphasize selecting a good mother palm and avoiding mother palms that habitually produce barren nuts.
11. SEEDNUT STORAGE AND PREPARATION
The Department of Education module states that:
* partially brown seednuts may be stored in a ventilated or open shed for approximately 3–4 weeks to hasten germination;
* completely brown seednuts do not need the same storage treatment;
* soaking seednuts in water for approximately 2 weeks, especially during dry periods, can enhance germination.
(DepEd, n.d.).
12. GERMINATION BED
A coconut germination bed should be:
* level;
* well-drained;
* near a water source;
* free from natural hazards;
* composed of friable soil;
* cleared of weeds and stones;
* thoroughly cultivated and pulverized.
The module states that approximately:
* 240 nuts may be sown for every 160 seedlings required;
* ungerminated nuts may be rejected after approximately 4 months.
A hectare of germination nursery may accommodate approximately 150,000–180,000 seednuts.
13. NURSERY MANAGEMENT
Germinated nuts begin appearing approximately:
6–8 weeks after sowing.
Germinated nuts can then be transferred to:
* polyethylene bags; or
* a field nursery.
Transfer can be conducted once or twice per week until approximately four months from sowing.
Polybag nursery
Use:
* black polyethylene bags;
* drainage holes at the lower portion;
* loamy soil.
The module provides examples of bag sizes depending on seednut size and orientation:
* 41 × 41 cm for small seednuts;
* 46 × 46 cm when a larger bag is required for tilted seednuts;
* 41 × 41 cm when seednuts are positioned upright.
A hectare of polybag nursery may accommodate approximately 18,000 seedlings.
Field nursery
Field nursery is suitable:
* when seedlings are transplanted before approximately seven months;
* when polybags are unavailable;
* for rolling to hilly plantations.
A good field nursery should be:
* well-drained;
* friable;
* near the planting area;
* cleared of weeds and stones;
* cultivated until fine.
Approximately 40,000 seedlings/ha may be raised in a field nursery.
14. NURSERY WATERING
In the absence of rain:
* water seedlings at least every other day;
* approximately 1 liter of water per seedling per day is described as beneficial on average.
The actual water requirement depends on local conditions.
15. NURSERY FERTILIZATION
The module recommends nitrogen- and chloride-containing fertilizers for good seedling growth.
Sources include:
* ammonium sulfate (21-0-0) for nitrogen and sulfur;
* muriate of potash (0-0-60) or common salt for potassium and chloride.
Application:
* first application at 2 months;
* second application at 5 months.
The module gives the following guide:
At 2 months
* 1 tablespoon ammonium sulfate;
* 1 tablespoon muriate of potash.
At 5 months
* 2 tablespoons ammonium sulfate;
* 2 tablespoons muriate of potash;
or the stated equivalent of:
* 4 tablespoons common salt.
To help avoid boron deficiency:
* 2 g borax/seedling may be applied directly to soil; or
* 0.2% borax spray may be applied 1–2 times.
(DepEd, n.d.).
16. NURSERY SHADE
Seedlings may require partial shade, especially:
* in relatively dry areas;
* where water supply is limited.
Coconut leaves can be used as shading material.
Before field planting:
gradually remove the shade
to harden/acclimatize the seedlings.
17. LAND PREPARATION
Farm establishment includes:
* thorough land preparation;
* correct spacing;
* proper planting;
* early care of young palms.
Land preparation depends on:
* topography;
* soil type;
* site conditions.
On slopes or erosion-prone areas:
* use cover crops;
* establish terraces following contour lines;
* use bunding where appropriate.
In waterlogged areas:
* use raised mounds;
* TNAU specifies mounds at least 1 m above the water level in low-lying areas.
18. LAND CLEARING
Where the area is forested:
* chop trees and bushes;
* remove grasses;
* clear undergrowth;
* fell trees;
* cut and pile wood.
Where cogon is dominant, the module discusses mechanical and chemical approaches to land preparation.
The source specifically mentions historical herbicide examples; for a modern capstone simulation, these should be treated as source-documented historical practices, not automatically as current chemical recommendations.
19. FIELD LAYOUT SYSTEMS
TNAU identifies:
* square system;
* rectangular system;
* triangular system;
* hedge system;
* contour system.
The appropriate layout depends on:
* soil;
* climate;
* plant type;
* site conditions.
Improper layout may result in:
* overlapping plant parts;
* competition for water;
* competition for light;
* competition for nutrients;
* unequal water distribution;
* poor plant performance.
20. PLANTING SPACING
The Department of Education module's assessment identifies 10 × 10 meters among the recommended spacing options.
TNAU provides variety-specific spacing for Tamil Nadu:
Coconut type
	Spacing
	Approx. plants/ha
	Tall
	7.5 × 7.5 m
	175
	Dwarf
	6.5 × 6.5 m
	—
	Hybrids
	8.5 × 8.5 m
	—
	Single border row
	20 ft between plants
	—
	TNAU also gives regional systems for Kerala and Karnataka.
21. PLANTING HOLES
Tamil Nadu system
TNAU recommends:
* 3 × 3 × 3 ft pit;
* approximately 60 cm of the pit filled with:
   * farmyard manure;
   * red earth;
   * sand;
* mixed in equal proportions.
The seedling is planted at the center after removing the appropriate soil mixture and pressing soil firmly around the plant.
Kerala
Pit size varies with soil:
* loamy soil: 1 × 1 × 1 m;
* laterite soil with underlying rock: 1.2 × 1.2 × 1.2 m;
* sandy soil: 0.75 × 0.75 × 0.75 m.
Pits may be filled with:
* topsoil;
* powdered cow dung/compost.
Husk layers can be placed at the bottom for moisture conservation.
Karnataka
* approximately 1 m³ pits;
* pits should be prepared well in advance;
* expose pits for weathering;
* fill with green leaves, compost/FYM, and topsoil;
* plant at the center;
* press soil firmly around the seedling;
* provide support.
22. PLANTING TIME
TNAU provides regional planting periods:
Tamil Nadu
* June–July;
* December–January;
* other seasons may be possible where irrigation and drainage are available.
Kerala
* May with onset of pre-monsoon rains;
* April under assured irrigation;
* September for low-lying areas after heavy rains.
Karnataka
* May–June.
23. PLANTING PROCEDURE
For polybagged seedlings:
1. Cut the bottom of the bag.
2. Place the seedling in the prepared hole.
3. Cut the remaining side of the bag longitudinally.
4. Pull the bag away after positioning the seedling.
5. Partially cover the hole with topsoil.
6. Press soil firmly around the seedling.
7. Provide temporary shade.
8. Maintain drainage.
9. Keep the planting area free from weeds.
TNAU recommends transplanting 9–12-month-old seedlings, while the Department of Education module discusses nursery rearing before permanent field planting.
24. CARE OF YOUNG PALMS
The first 3–4 years are described as the most critical period for growing palms.
Proper care during this period helps promote:
* healthy growth;
* early bearing;
* better production.
Important practices include:
* protecting seedlings from animals;
* maintaining drainage;
* watering when needed;
* mulching;
* weed management;
* replacing dead or unhealthy seedlings.
25. PROTECTION FROM ANIMALS
Young coconut seedlings can be damaged by:
* grazing;
* trampling;
* goats and other farm animals.
Protection may be provided through:
* individual plant fencing; or
* fencing the entire plantation.
Animal activity can harden and expose soil around young palms.
26. DRAINAGE
During heavy rains:
* prevent prolonged standing water in planting holes;
* maintain ring bunds;
* repair damaged bunds;
* remove soil washed into the planting hole;
* prevent soil from covering the collar or leaf axils;
* gradually fill planting holes as the palm grows.
Drainage is important because waterlogging is associated with poor coconut growth and yield.
27. MULCHING
Mulching:
* suppresses weeds;
* conserves soil moisture;
* regulates soil temperature.
Materials may include:
* dried weeds;
* organic mulch;
* coconut husks;
* coconut leaves.
The module identifies coconut husk as an abundant farm waste suitable for mulching.
28. REPLANTING
Dead plants and seedlings showing persistent:
* unhealthy growth;
* stunted growth;
should be replaced promptly.
The module recommends keeping approximately:
15 replacement seedlings per hectare.
Seedlings should be lifted carefully:
* avoid root damage;
* plant immediately after lifting;
* keep roots moist during transportation;
* do not lift seedlings by the leaves;
* lift them by the nut.
29. FERTILIZATION — IMPORTANCE
The Philippine Coconut Authority fertilization guide reports that Philippine coconut areas may experience deficiencies involving:
* nitrogen (N);
* phosphorus (P);
* potassium (K);
* chlorine (Cl);
* sulfur (S);
* magnesium (Mg).
The source states that many provinces are particularly affected by N–Cl deficiency.
Proper fertilizer application is reported to increase:
* nut yield;
* copra yield;
by as much as 230% in the cited source.
A Davao City study cited in the guide reported approximately 180% net income compared with no fertilization. These are source-reported study results, not guaranteed outcomes for every farm.
30. ORGANIC FERTILIZERS
The fertilization guide identifies organic materials that may replace part of commercial fertilizer requirements:
* ipil-ipil green manure;
* cattle manure;
* carabao manure;
* pig manure;
* goat manure;
* chicken manure;
* compost;
* night soil;
* coconut crown residues;
* nitrogen-fixing legumes;
* Flemingia;
* Desmodium rensonii;
* cocopeat;
* coconut husk.
The source identifies coconut crown residues and nitrogen-fixing legumes as potential nitrogen sources, while cocopeat and husk may contribute chlorine.
31. FERTILIZER APPLICATION METHODS
Flat areas
Fertilizer may be:
1. broadcast within the ring-weeded area;
2. applied approximately 1–1.5 m radius around the palm;
3. incorporated into the soil using a fork or digging tool.
Hilly areas
Use hole placement:
1. Make 8–10 equidistant holes around the palm.
2. Place fertilizer into the holes.
3. Cover the fertilizer with soil.
The Philippine Coconut Authority guide specifies approximately 5 cm hole depth, while the Department of Education module describes approximately 14 cm depth within a 1–2 m radius for sloping/hilly areas. These are the procedures stated in the respective supplied sources.
32. WHEN TO APPLY FERTILIZER
Areas with distinct wet and dry seasons
Apply fertilizer:
* every 6 months;
* split between rainy-season periods;
* approximately half at the beginning of the rainy season;
* the other half approximately six months before the end of the rainy season.
Areas with relatively uniform rainfall
Where there are approximately 1.5–3 dry months:
* use split application during the first year;
* apply once annually afterward.
33. FERTILIZER RATES FOR SEEDLINGS
The Philippine Coconut Authority guide provides:
