36. WEED MANAGEMENT
The Department of Education module emphasizes:
* regular weeding;
* cultivation;
* keeping the area around young palms clean.
TNAU recommends keeping planting pits free of weeds and using intercultivation practices appropriate to the local conditions.
For coconut gardens, TNAU describes:
* plowing;
* digging;
* raking;
* mound formation;
as possible intercultural methods.
37. WEED-FREE BASIN
The Department of Education module identifies approximately:
1–1.5 m around the base
as an area that should be maintained free of competing crops/weeds for fertilizer and cultural operations.
TNAU similarly identifies a wider basin around palms for regular management in intercropping systems.
38. INTERCROPPING
TNAU explains that coconut palms are commonly spaced widely enough to provide interspaces for additional crops.
Potential intercrops include:
* groundnut;
* sesame;
* sunflower;
* tapioca;
* turmeric;
* banana;
* pineapple;
* ginger;
* sweet potato;
* yams;
* vegetables;
* legumes;
* fodder crops.
Crop selection depends on:
* palm age;
* canopy coverage;
* soil type;
* climate;
* irrigation;
* market suitability.
39. INTERCROPPING BY PALM AGE
TNAU describes age-based systems.
Below 7 years
Suitable annual crops may be grown, depending on local conditions, including:
* groundnut;
* sesame;
* sunflower;
* tapioca;
* turmeric;
* banana.
7–20 years
The source recommends:
* green manure crops;
* fodder crops such as Napier grass and Guinea grass.
Above 20 years
Where sufficient sunlight reaches the ground, crops may include:
* groundnut;
* okra;
* turmeric;
* tapioca;
* sweet potato;
* ginger;
* pineapple;
* banana;
* cocoa;
* pepper;
* nutmeg;
* vanilla.
Separate fertilizer and manure application is required for intercrops.
40. MULTI-STOREY CROPPING
TNAU describes multi-storey systems designed to use:
* sunlight;
* soil moisture;
* nutrients;
* vertical space.
One example is:
Coconut + pepper + cocoa + pineapple
where:
* coconut forms the top level;
* pepper forms the second level;
* cocoa forms the lower tree level;
* pineapple occupies the ground level.
The source describes these crops as having relatively complementary root distribution under the described system.
41. BANANA AS A MIXED CROP
TNAU identifies banana as an important mixed crop for coconut gardens.
The source states:
* banana responds to similar irrigation and manuring systems as coconut;
* it can be grown under rainfed conditions;
* approximately 1,000 banana plants/ha may be grown in a coconut garden with approximately 125 palms/ha.
Intercrops should still receive their own appropriate management and fertilizer.
42. GREEN MANURE AND COVER CROPS
TNAU identifies green manure crops such as:
* Crotalaria juncea;
* Tephrosia purpurea;
* Gliricidia maculata;
* Indigofera hirsuta;
* Pueraria phaseoloides.
Cover crops include:
* Calapagonium muconoides;
* Mimosa invisa;
* Stylosanthes gracilis.
Seeds may be sown around April–May with pre-monsoon rainfall, while green manure crops may be incorporated into the soil around August–September.
43. MAJOR INSECT PESTS
The Department of Education module identifies numerous coconut pests.
Palm weevil
* Particularly harmful to palms approximately 3–15 years old.
* Larvae tunnel into:
   * trunk;
   * cabbage/growing tissues.
* Severe infestation can destroy the growing point.
* Symptoms include:
   * withered buds;
   * drooping leaves;
   * eventual crown loss.
Control measures described include sanitation and destruction/burning of heavily infested materials or palms.
Slug caterpillar
* Larvae feed on coconut leaves.
* Severe infestation can result in complete defoliation.
* Young palms are particularly vulnerable.
* Severe infestations are associated with dry weather.
* Reduced photosynthetic activity may result in yield losses.
Coconut lice
The module groups several sucking pests under coconut lice, including:
* scale insects;
* aphids;
* mealybugs;
* spider mites.
They feed on plant sap and may produce honeydew, which can support sooty mold. Natural enemies such as coccinellid beetles are identified as important predators.
Scale insects
* Attack nursery seedlings and older palms.
* Damage is particularly serious in young palms.
* Heavy populations may form a scurfy layer on the underside of leaves.
* Leaves may wither and die.
* Heavy attacks can reduce yield.
Aphids
* Usually not severe;
* can be important in nurseries;
* feeding may cause twisting and distortion;
* heavy infestation may contribute to sooty mold.
Mealybugs
* Common in coconut;
* generally not considered major pests in the module;
* may coexist with coconut scale in nursery foliage.
Whitefly
The module reports that high populations may cover the underside of leaves with a thick white waxy layer and can cause serious damage.
Termites
The module emphasizes plantation sanitation as preventive management:
* collect fallen leaves;
* remove useless nuts;
* remove broken/fallen palms;
* destroy breeding materials.
44. MAJOR COCONUT DISEASES
Bud rot
The module identifies bud rot as an important and potentially fatal coconut disease.
When advanced symptoms are visible, particularly when the youngest leaves have dried and collapsed, the palm may already be severely affected.
The source recommends removing severely infected palms and destroying the affected crown material.
Shoot rot
Shoot rot can be fatal to seedlings.
It may occur:
* during nursery stage;
* in young field-planted palms up to approximately 2 years old.
The module identifies possible causes including:
* mechanical injury;
* cracked shells;
* poor handling;
* water stress;
* insect-related damage.
Control measures described include:
* fungicide treatment;
* treating wounds;
* removing and destroying rotten tissue;
* disinfecting tools;
* temporary shade after treatment;
* protecting treated seedlings from rain.
Leaf spot
Leaf spot is described as a common coconut disease occurring in:
* nurseries;
* field-planted palms.
It reduces:
* photosynthetic activity;
* palm vitality;
* early vegetative performance;
* earliness of bearing;
* yield.
The module describes fungicide spraying when symptoms appear and removal/burning of infected leaves as part of control.
Cadang-cadang
The module identifies cadang-cadang as a serious/fatal communicable disease capable of affecting coconut palms at different developmental stages.
45. INTEGRATED PEST AND DISEASE MANAGEMENT PRINCIPLES FROM THE SOURCES
The supplied sources emphasize:
* sanitation;
* removal of diseased plant material;
* destruction of pest breeding sites;
* proper drainage;
* proper irrigation;
* maintaining healthy plants;
* balanced fertilization;
* regular field inspection;
* use of appropriate control measures;
* personal protective equipment when applying pesticides/fungicides.
TNAU additionally recommends:
* plant products;
* biological control agents;
* proper drainage;
* drip irrigation where possible;
* fertilizer application based on soil testing.
46. HARVESTING
The Department of Education module states that:
* the female flower develops into a mature nut in approximately 12 months;
* a regularly bearing palm may produce an inflorescence approximately every month;
* therefore, a bunch may potentially be ready every month;
* actual harvesting is commonly done only 3–6 times per year because of operating costs.
47. COCONUT MATURITY
Tender coconut
Marketable tender nuts are approximately:
7–8 months old.
Mature coconut
Marketable mature nuts are approximately:
11½–12 months old.
The module states that nuts harvested at approximately 12 months have high oil quality and quantity.
Harvesting at approximately 11 months is reported to result in around 6% loss in oil quality and quantity.
48. HARVESTING METHODS
Three harvesting methods are identified:
1. Collecting fully mature fallen nuts.
2. Climbing the tree.
3. Cutting clusters with a sharp curved knife attached to a long slender pole.
The common harvesting tool identified is the:
short, scythe-shaped knife.
In Luzon, the knife may be attached to a bamboo pole approximately 7–8 feet long.
In Visayas and Mindanao, climbing and cutting with a sharp tool is described as common.
49. HARVESTING PROBLEMS
When ripe and immature nuts are harvested together:
* immature nuts may crack when they hit the ground;
* the resulting mixture of ripe and unripe nuts can produce poor-quality copra.
The source therefore emphasizes appropriate maturity when harvesting.
50. MARKETING
Marketing varies by region.
The module reports:
Luzon and Southern Tagalog
Young/tender coconuts may be sold in:
* parks;
* resorts;
* terminals;
* waiting stations.
Laguna and Quezon
Mature nuts may be sold for:
* desiccated coconut;
* copra;
* oil.
Visayas and Mindanao
The source identifies:
copra
as the preferred coconut product.
51. FARM RECORD KEEPING
Record keeping should begin when the coconut enterprise is planned.
Good records help farmers:
* make decisions;
* identify what happened and when;
* identify weak points;
* understand the farm's business position;
* calculate taxes and insurance;
* identify opportunities for greater profit;
* support loan applications and expansion.
52. IMPORTANT FARM RECORDS
The module recommends maintaining:
1. Short-range program of work.
2. Long-range program of work.
3. Project prospectus/economic study.
4. Farm cash accounts.
5. Expense and receipt records.
6. Income records.
7. Labor records.
8. Investment records.
9. Other necessary records.
53. FARM WORK PROGRAM
A coconut farm work program should contain:
* project aims and goals;
* ways to achieve the goals;
* equipment required;
* tools;
* supplies;
* materials;
* labor requirements;
* funds required;
* activity calendar;
* expected problems;
* possible solutions.
54. FARM INVESTMENT AND OPERATING EXPENSES
Investment expenses
The module identifies:
* land purchased or leased;
* farm equipment;
* tools and implements;
* buildings;
* storage facilities;
* dryers.
The module states a depreciation rate of approximately 9–12% per year for applicable long-term assets in its example.
Operating expenses
May include:
* fertilizers;
* planting materials;
* weeding;
* cultivation;
* supplies;
* small tools;
* nursery supplies;
* worker food;
* harvesting labor;
* replanting labor;
* land lease/amortization;
* pest and disease control chemicals;
* building repairs;
* nursery-shed repairs;
* depreciation;
* miscellaneous expenses.
55. GOOD RECORD-KEEPING PRACTICES
Records should be:
* complete;
* readable;
* neat;
* protected from rain;
* protected from dust;
* protected from damage;
* compiled year after year;
* systematically filed;
* indexed for easier retrieval.
Because coconut is a long-term crop, the module emphasizes maintaining records over many years.

	Farm records
	Record expenses, yield, sales and labor
	59. SYNTHESIS FOR DOCUMENTATION / CAPSTONE PAPER
Coconut production requires appropriate site selection, suitable soil and climate conditions, quality planting materials, proper land preparation, correct spacing, and continuous field management. Coconut can grow in many well-drained soils, although deep and fertile soils with adequate water-holding capacity are preferred. Soil sampling and analysis are important for determining soil pH and nutrient status and for guiding fertilizer application. The sources identify approximately 5.5–6.5 as an optimum soil pH range, while TNAU reports that coconut can tolerate a broader pH range. Suitable tropical conditions, adequate rainfall or irrigation, and effective drainage are also important for maintaining healthy palm growth and production (Department of Education [DepEd], n.d.; Tamil Nadu Agricultural University [TNAU], n.d.).
Proper coconut establishment begins with the selection and preparation of quality seednuts, germination in a suitable nursery, seedling care, land preparation, appropriate planting layout, and careful transplanting. Young palms require protection from animals, weeds, excessive water, and drought. Fertilization is particularly important because coconut areas may experience deficiencies of nitrogen, phosphorus, potassium, chlorine, sulfur, and magnesium. The Philippine Coconut Authority fertilization guide provides fertilizer recommendations according to seedling or palm age, location relative to the coastline, fertilizer source, and rainfall pattern, while also recognizing the use of organic materials such as manure, compost, coconut residues, and green manure (Department of Education [DepEd], n.d.; Philippine Coconut Authority [PCA], 2019).
Coconut production also requires regular weed management, mulching, irrigation when necessary, drainage maintenance, pest and disease monitoring, and appropriate crop protection. Major problems identified in the production module include palm weevil, slug caterpillar, scale insects, aphids, mealybugs, whiteflies, termites, bud rot, shoot rot, leaf spot, and cadang-cadang. Intercropping and multi-storey cropping can also be used where site, palm age, canopy, soil, water, and market conditions are appropriate. TNAU identifies crops such as banana, pineapple, groundnut, turmeric, ginger, cocoa, pepper, and other crops for particular coconut-based cropping systems (DepEd, n.d.; TNAU, n.d.).
Harvesting is based on the intended product. Tender coconuts may be harvested at approximately 7–8 months, whereas mature nuts are approximately 11½–12 months old. The production module states that fully mature nuts have high oil quality and quantity and that harvesting at approximately 11 months is associated with a reported 6% loss in oil quality and quantity. Harvesting may involve collecting fallen mature nuts, climbing the palm, or cutting clusters using a sharp curved knife attached to a pole. After harvest, proper marketing and record keeping are important components of coconut farm management. Farm records should cover work programs, expenses, receipts, income, labor, investments, and other operational information (DepEd, n.d.).
60. COMBINED PARAGRAPH FOR CAPSTONE
Coconut production requires suitable site conditions, well-drained and sufficiently deep soil, adequate water supply, quality planting materials, proper land preparation, appropriate spacing, and continuous crop management (Department of Education [DepEd], n.d.; Tamil Nadu Agricultural University [TNAU], n.d.). Soil sampling and analysis are important for determining soil pH and nutrient status, while fertilization should consider the growth stage, soil condition, rainfall pattern, and location of the coconut palm (Philippine Coconut Authority [PCA], 2019). Young palms require careful nursery and field management, including watering, shading, weed control, mulching, drainage, and protection from animals. As palms mature, farmers must continue monitoring nutrient deficiencies, insect pests, diseases, and water conditions. Intercropping may also be practiced when appropriate based on palm age, canopy, soil, climate, irrigation, and market conditions. Harvesting is performed according to the intended coconut product, with tender nuts harvested at approximately 7–8 months and mature nuts at approximately 11½–12 months. Proper harvesting, marketing, and systematic farm record keeping are important for maintaining productivity and managing the long-term coconut enterprise (DepEd, n.d.; TNAU, n.d.).

Philippine Coconut Authority. (2019). Fertilization guide for coconuts. Department of Agriculture, Agricultural Training Institute. Fertilization Guide on Coconuts (ATI)
Philippine Coconut Authority. (2019). Fertilization guide for coconuts. Fertilization Guide for Coconuts
Tamil Nadu Agricultural University. (n.d.). Coconut: Main field (cultivation practices). TNAU Agritech Portal, Expert System on Coconut. Coconut: Main Field (Cultivation Practices)


MANGO
MANGO PRODUCTION GUIDE 
1. MANGO OVERVIEW
* Mango is identified as Mangifera indica.
* Mango production is an important agricultural activity in the Philippines.
* The Philippine National Standard for Good Agricultural Practices for Mango is identified in the ATI material as PNS/BAFPS 45:2009.
* The PhilGAP approach follows the principle of producing safe and quality food from farm to fork.
* The ATI material explains that the Department of Agriculture approved Administrative Order No. 25, Series of 2005, concerning certification of Good Agricultural Practices for fruits and vegetables.
* The material also identifies Republic Act No. 10611, the Food Safety Act of 2013, as part of the legal framework for food safety.
* DA Administrative Circular No. 1, Series of 2018 established rules and regulations concerning Philippine Good Agricultural Crop Practices (PhilGAP).
* The GAP framework is therefore not limited to increasing yield. It incorporates:
   * food safety;
   * environmental management;
   * worker health and safety;
   * farm sanitation;
   * proper input management;
   * traceability;
   * postharvest handling;
   * responsible pesticide use.
2. GENERAL MANGO PRODUCTION REQUIREMENTS
Successful mango production requires the integration of:
* suitable farm location;
* appropriate climate;
* suitable soil;
* adequate drainage;
* reliable water source;
* quality planting materials;
* proper farm structures;
* proper land preparation;
* correct planting;
* irrigation management;
* fertilization;
* pruning;
* weed management;
* flowering and fruit-set management;
* integrated pest management;
* pesticide management;
* sanitation;
* worker protection;
* harvesting;
* postharvest handling;
* record keeping;
* traceability.
The Farmers Advisory source similarly identifies climate, soil, grafted planting material, land preparation, irrigation, fertilization, pruning, flowering, pest and disease management, harvesting, and postharvest handling as major production stages.
3. FARM LOCATION
Farm location is an important part of PhilGAP because the location can affect:
* crop growth;
* drainage;
* accessibility;
* contamination risk;
* water availability;
* worker safety;
* transportation;
* farm management;
* postharvest operations.
The site should be evaluated before establishing the orchard.
Important considerations include:
* accessibility of the farm;
* availability and quality of water;
* soil condition;
* drainage;
* surrounding land uses;
* possible contamination sources;
* flood risk;
* chemical spray drift from neighboring farms;
* accessibility for farm workers and vehicles.
The Farmers Advisory source additionally emphasizes that mango should be planted in a site with good drainage and adequate sunlight, and that low-lying areas prone to standing water should be avoided.
4. CLIMATE REQUIREMENTS
Mango is described in the Farmers Advisory guide as a tropical to subtropical crop.
Important climate considerations include:
* warm conditions;
* frost-free conditions;
* adequate sunlight;
* a distinct dry period;
* relatively dry conditions during flowering.
The source emphasizes that:
* mango is sensitive to frost;
* young trees are particularly vulnerable;
* a dry period around flowering supports flowering;
* rainfall and high humidity during flowering may interfere with pollination and increase fungal disease pressure;
* full sunlight is important for productive trees.
For the Philippine setting, climate conditions should therefore be evaluated according to the intended production area rather than applying one universal climate value.
5. SOIL REQUIREMENTS
Mango can tolerate different soil types, but good drainage is particularly important.
The source emphasizes:
* deep soil;
* good drainage;
* avoidance of waterlogged conditions;
* adequate root-zone development;
* soil testing before planting.
A soil test can help determine:
* soil texture;
* drainage characteristics;
* pH;
* nutrient condition;
* amendments that may be needed before planting.
Waterlogged roots can severely affect establishment and long-term tree performance.
6. SUNLIGHT REQUIREMENTS
Mango trees require adequate sunlight.
Full sun is preferred because insufficient sunlight can result in:
* weaker tree development;
* reduced productivity;
* poorer canopy performance.
Therefore, the orchard should avoid excessive shading from:
* buildings;
* large trees;
* structures;
* neighboring vegetation.
7. WATER REQUIREMENTS
Water management is important throughout mango production.
Young trees
Young mango trees require:
* regular watering;
* sufficient moisture for root establishment;
* protection against water stress.
Mature trees
As trees mature:
* irrigation requirements change;
* roots become more extensive;
* excessive irrigation should be avoided;
* water management around flowering becomes especially important.
The Farmers Advisory guide notes that reducing water before the expected flowering period can help promote flowering in many mango-growing areas, but the timing depends on local climate and should not be treated as a universal fixed schedule.
8. WATER QUALITY
Water quality is part of PhilGAP management.
A GAP-compliant farm should consider:
* source of irrigation water;
* possible contamination;
* protection of water sources;
* cleanliness of water used in production;
* appropriate water management practices.
The GAP cooperator example in the supporting Philippine GAP literature maintained a deep well and water pump as part of water-quality management.
9. CHOOSING PLANTING MATERIAL
Quality planting material is essential for orchard establishment.
The Farmers Advisory guide strongly recommends grafted trees for commercial production because:
* grafted trees reproduce the characteristics of the selected parent variety;
* they reach bearing age earlier than seed-grown trees;
* the rootstock can be selected according to local soil and disease conditions.
Seed-grown trees may:
* take considerably longer to bear;
* produce unpredictable fruit quality;
* fail to reproduce the desired characteristics of the parent tree.
10. VARIETY SELECTION
Variety selection should consider:
* local climate;
* local production conditions;
* disease resistance;
* fruit characteristics;
* market preference;
* target buyers;
* intended use of the fruit.
Different varieties can vary in:
* flavor;
* fiber content;
* disease resistance;
* climate tolerance;
* fruit quality.
Therefore, the source recommends selecting varieties proven to perform well in the intended production area.
11. FARM STRUCTURES
Farm structures are an important component of PhilGAP compliance.
Depending on the farm operation, appropriate structures may include:
* farm roads;
* drainage facilities;
* water facilities;
* storage areas;
* packing house;
* toilet facilities;
* worker facilities;
* pesticide storage;
* input storage;
* waste-management areas.
Structures should support:
* safe production;
* sanitation;
* worker welfare;
* efficient movement;
* proper handling of inputs;
* proper handling of harvested fruit.
The Philippine GAP example of a certified mango farm specifically included structures such as roads, packing house, and toilet facilities as part of its GAP-aligned farm system.
12. FARM ROAD AND ACCESS
Farm access is important for:
* bringing planting materials into the farm;
* transporting fertilizers and other inputs;
* carrying harvested mangoes;
* allowing workers and equipment to move efficiently;
* emergency access.
Farm roads should be maintained so that farm operations can continue safely during production and harvest.
13. DRAINAGE
Adequate drainage is essential.
The production site should prevent:
* prolonged waterlogging;
* standing water;
* excessive moisture around roots;
* conditions that encourage disease.
Drainage facilities should be inspected and maintained regularly.
This is especially important because the Farmers Advisory guide identifies waterlogged roots as a major risk to mango establishment.
14. LAND PREPARATION
Before planting:
1. Assess the site.
2. Conduct soil evaluation/testing.
3. Clear unwanted vegetation.
4. Correct major soil or drainage problems.
5. Prepare planting locations.
6. Prepare planting holes.
7. Establish appropriate spacing.
8. Ensure that water and access facilities are ready.
The objective is to ensure that the orchard is established under suitable physical conditions rather than attempting to correct major site problems after planting.
15. PLANTING HOLES
Planting holes should be:
* sufficiently wide for the root ball;
* sufficiently deep for proper root placement;
* prepared without forcing or crowding the roots.
The tree should be planted at approximately the same depth at which it was growing in its nursery container.
Planting too deeply can contribute to poor establishment.
16. PLANTING SPACING
Spacing should not be treated as one fixed value for all mango orchards.
It depends on:
* variety;
* tree vigor;
* expected canopy size;
* training system;
* orchard management system;
* available land.
More vigorous varieties require more space, while compact varieties can be planted more closely.
The spacing should therefore be planned before planting.
17. PLANTING
The general planting procedure includes:
1. Select healthy planting material.
2. Prepare the planting location.
3. Dig a hole appropriate for the root ball.
4. Avoid damaging the roots.
5. Place the tree at the appropriate depth.
6. Fill the hole around the root system.
7. Ensure the plant is stable.
8. Water after planting.
9. Provide support where needed.
Young trees in windy locations may require staking.
18. PROTECTION OF YOUNG TREES
Young mango trees should be protected from:
* strong wind;
* animals;
* physical damage;
* excessive environmental stress.
The Farmers Advisory guide recommends staking young trees in windy sites and protecting trunks while the bark remains thin.
19. IRRIGATION MANAGEMENT
Irrigation should be adjusted according to:
* tree age;
* weather;
* soil moisture;
* rainfall;
* growth stage;
* flowering stage;
* fruit-development stage.
Avoid both:
* insufficient water;
* excessive water.
Young trees generally require more regular irrigation during establishment, while mature trees require more carefully managed water depending on production stage.
20. FERTILIZATION
Fertilization should be based on:
* tree age;
* growth stage;
* soil condition;
* nutrient requirements;
* soil-test results;
* leaf/tissue-test results where appropriate.
Young trees require nutrients to support:
* root development;
* branches;
* leaves;
* canopy establishment.
Bearing trees have different nutrient requirements associated with:
* flowering;
* fruit set;
* fruit development;
* recovery after harvest.
The source specifically warns against indiscriminate nutrient application and recommends using soil or leaf analysis to guide nutrient management.
21. MICRONUTRIENTS
The Farmers Advisory guide identifies zinc and boron as micronutrients commonly associated with:
* fruit set;
* fruit quality.
However, it emphasizes that nutrient requirements vary according to soil conditions.
Therefore:
soil or leaf testing is preferred over automatically applying a generic micronutrient schedule.
22. NITROGEN MANAGEMENT
Excessive nitrogen application should be avoided, especially around flowering.
Too much nitrogen can encourage:
* excessive vegetative growth;
* reduced emphasis on flowering;
* poorer fruit-setting performance.
Therefore, fertilizer application should be based on actual crop requirements rather than simply increasing fertilizer quantities to obtain more growth.
23. WEED MANAGEMENT
Weeds compete with mango trees for:
* water;
* nutrients;
* growing space.
This competition is particularly important for young trees.
The orchard should therefore be regularly inspected and maintained to prevent excessive weed growth around the trees.
24. PRUNING AND CANOPY MANAGEMENT
Pruning has different objectives depending on tree age.
Young trees
Pruning is used to:
* establish a strong framework;
* develop an open canopy;
* improve light penetration;
* shape the tree.
Mature trees
Pruning is performed after harvest to:
* maintain tree size;
* remove dead branches;
* remove crossing branches;
* improve canopy condition;
* encourage new growth for the following production cycle.
25. FARM SANITATION
Farm sanitation is a major component of GAP.
The farm should be maintained through:
* regular clearing;
* pruning;
* weed management;
* removal of damaged plant materials;
* proper disposal of waste;
* cleaning of production and packing areas;
* prevention of animal contamination.
The Philippine GAP example specifically included:
* regular land clearing;
* clean farm and packing house;
* keeping animals away from production areas.
26. WASTE MANAGEMENT
Waste should not simply be left in the production area.
The GAP approach includes:
* waste segregation;
* proper disposal;
* removal of rotten fruit;
* proper handling of pesticide containers;
* maintaining cleanliness in the farm and packing areas.
Improper disposal can create:
* pest problems;
* contamination;
* worker hazards;
* environmental risks.
27. FLOWERING
Flowering is one of the most critical stages of mango production.
The Farmers Advisory source identifies flowering as sensitive to:
* rainfall;
* humidity;
* temperature;
* pollination conditions;
* nutrient status.
Wet and humid conditions during flowering can:
* interfere with pollination;
* increase fungal disease pressure;
* reduce fruit set.
28. FLOWER INDUCTION
Mango production in the Philippines commonly involves flower-induction operations, and the Philippine GAP material identifies flower induction as one of the services performed by mango sprayers.
However, the key GAP principle is that any chemical or input used for flower induction must be:
* appropriate for the crop;
* legally permitted;
* used at the prescribed rate;
* properly documented;
* applied safely;
* handled by appropriately trained workers.
29. POLLINATION AND FRUIT SET
Fruit set is affected by:
* flowering conditions;
* pollination;
* weather;
* nutrient status;
* pest and disease pressure.
The Farmers Advisory guide emphasizes that cool, wet, or overcast weather can interfere with pollination and consequently reduce fruit set.
Some natural fruit drop is normal.
However, unusually heavy fruit drop may indicate:
* water stress;
* nutrient imbalance;
* pest pressure;
* disease problems.
30. BAGGING OF MANGO FRUITS
Individual fruit bagging is identified in the Philippine GAP material as an important practice.
Mango fruits may be individually wrapped in paper bags to:
* reduce blemishes;
* protect fruits from insect infestation;
* reduce exposure to pesticide residues;
* improve fruit appearance;
* support production of quality fruit.
The Philippine GAP example describes the use of:
* bags;
* staple wires;
* staplers
for bagging operations.
31. INTEGRATED PEST MANAGEMENT
PhilGAP Series 3 specifically focuses on:
Integrated Pest Management (IPM).
IPM involves managing pests through an integrated approach rather than relying exclusively on pesticides.
Important activities include:
1. Regular monitoring.
2. Identification of pests.
3. Identification of damage.
4. Monitoring pest population.
5. Maintaining farm sanitation.
6. Applying appropriate cultural practices.
7. Protecting beneficial organisms.
8. Using chemical control only when appropriate.
9. Following prescribed pesticide rates.
10. Observing required safety and harvest intervals.
The Farmers Advisory guide similarly emphasizes monitoring, sanitation, and targeted treatment when necessary rather than routine preventive spraying.
32. PEST MONITORING
Regular inspection is the foundation of pest management.
Farmers should inspect:
* leaves;
* shoots;
* flowers;
* young fruits;
* mature fruits;
* branches;
* trunks.
The objective is to identify problems early before they become severe.
The Farmers Advisory guide specifically mentions monitoring problems such as:
* fruit flies;
* scale insects;
* fungal diseases such as anthracnose.
33. COMMON PEST AND DISEASE CONCERNS
The supplied Farmers Advisory source identifies examples such as:
Fruit flies
Can cause damage to mango fruits and should be detected early.
Scale insects
May attack plant tissues and should be monitored regularly.
Anthracnose
A fungal disease that can become particularly problematic under conditions favorable to disease development.
The source emphasizes early detection and integrated management rather than waiting until fruit damage becomes severe.
34. PESTICIDE MANAGEMENT
Pesticide management is a major component of PhilGAP Series 3.
Pesticides should be:
* legally registered/approved for their intended use;
* used according to the label;
* applied at the prescribed dosage;
* applied at the appropriate time;
* handled by trained personnel;
* stored safely;
* recorded;
* transported safely;
* disposed of properly.
The Philippine GAP literature shows the importance of following Fertilizer and Pesticide Authority (FPA) recommendations and avoiding banned chemicals.
35. CORRECT PESTICIDE DOSAGE
Farmers should not increase or decrease pesticide concentration arbitrarily.
Incorrect pesticide use can result in:
* ineffective pest control;
* crop damage;
* unnecessary cost;
* pesticide residues;
* environmental contamination;
* worker exposure;
* market rejection.
The Philippine mango GAP example specifically notes that GAP-aligned farmers used only the recommended pesticide dosage and avoided banned chemicals.
36. MAXIMUM RESIDUE LIMITS
Maximum Residue Limits (MRLs) are important for mangoes intended for markets with strict food-safety requirements.
The Philippine GAP literature documents that export markets such as Japan and South Korea have strict requirements regarding pesticide residues.
Therefore, farmers need to:
* use approved chemicals;
* follow prescribed rates;
* follow application schedules;
* observe required pre-harvest intervals;
* maintain records;
* avoid prohibited chemicals.
This is particularly important for export-oriented mango production.
37. PESTICIDE APPLICATION RECORDS
Farm operations should maintain records of pesticide applications.
Records should support traceability of:
* date of application;
* product used;
* application purpose;
* dosage;
* area treated;
* responsible worker;
* relevant production stage.
The GAP system uses records as part of demonstrating that production practices are controlled and traceable.
38. PESTICIDE STORAGE
Pesticides should be stored in a controlled and safe location.
Storage should prevent:
* unauthorized access;
* contamination;
* accidental exposure;
* contact with food;
* contact with workers not involved in pesticide operations;
* environmental contamination.
Pesticides should not be stored together with harvested mangoes or food products.
39. PESTICIDE HANDLING
Workers handling pesticides need appropriate:
* training;
* protective equipment;
* supervision;
* hygiene practices;
* knowledge of label instructions;
* knowledge of emergency procedures.
Pesticide handling should minimize exposure through:
* skin;
* eyes;
* inhalation;
* accidental ingestion.
40. WORKER HEALTH AND SAFETY
PhilGAP Series 3 specifically incorporates worker health.
Workers should be protected through:
* appropriate training;
* supervision;
* protective equipment;
* sanitation facilities;
* toilet facilities;
* safe working conditions;
* proper pesticide-handling procedures.
The GAP cooperator example specifically included:
* worker training;
* worker supervision;
* protective gear;
* toilets.
41. WORKER TRAINING
Workers should be trained in the activities they perform.
Training should cover relevant areas such as:
* farm sanitation;
* pesticide safety;
* proper pesticide application;
* use of protective equipment;
* harvesting;
* handling of produce;
* emergency procedures;
* personal hygiene.
Workers should understand that food safety and worker safety are interconnected.
42. PERSONAL HYGIENE
Good personal hygiene is important because workers can contaminate produce during:
* harvesting;
* sorting;
* packing;
* handling.
Facilities such as clean toilets and handwashing areas help reduce contamination risks.
43. ANIMAL CONTROL
Animals should be prevented from freely entering production and packing areas.
This reduces risks associated with:
* fecal contamination;
* physical damage;
* disease transmission;
* contamination of harvested produce.
The Philippine GAP farm example specifically included keeping animals away from the farm as part of field sanitation.
44. SPRAY DRIFT
A major risk identified in the Philippine mango industry is agrochemical trespass or spray drift from neighboring farms.
This occurs when pesticide applications from another farm reach the mango production area.
Potential consequences include:
* contamination;
* pesticide residues;
* food-safety concerns;
* problems with export compliance.
The Philippine GAP literature describes the selection of isolated farm locations, where possible, as one strategy for reducing this risk.
45. ENVIRONMENTAL MANAGEMENT
PhilGAP requires agricultural production to consider environmental impacts.
Important practices include:
* proper waste disposal;
* responsible pesticide use;
* protection of water sources;
* proper chemical storage;
* prevention of spray drift;
* sanitation;
* appropriate farm maintenance.
The objective is to produce mango safely while minimizing negative environmental impacts.
46. RECORD KEEPING
Record keeping is an important GAP component.
Records help document:
* farm activities;
* fertilizer application;
* pesticide application;
* worker activities;
* harvesting;
* production;
* input use;
* farm inspections;
* corrective actions.
Records also make it possible to trace problems back to the farm operation.
47. TRACEABILITY
Traceability allows harvested mangoes to be linked to:
* the farm;
* farmer;
* production area;
* production activity;
* harvest date;
* handling operation.
The Philippine GAP literature describes exporters using coding systems in which boxes or containers carry information such as the farmer's name and area where the fruit was harvested.
48. FARM INSPECTION
PhilGAP compliance involves farm inspection.
Inspection can evaluate whether the farm is following:
* required production practices;
* sanitation procedures;
* pesticide management;
* worker safety;
* farm structure requirements;
* record keeping;
* food-safety measures.
The ATI Series 1 material identifies PhilGAP certification as part of the Philippine agricultural food-safety system.
49. HARVESTING
Mangoes should generally be harvested:
mature but still firm.
Harvesting too early can result in:
* poor ripening;
* inadequate fruit quality.
Leaving fruit too long on the tree can increase:
* bruising;
* damage;
* reduced shelf life.
Maturity indicators vary among varieties, so the source recommends learning the appropriate maturity characteristics for the particular variety and production area.
50. HARVESTING PRACTICES
During harvesting:
* handle fruit carefully;
* avoid dropping fruit;
* prevent bruising;
* use clean harvesting equipment;
* keep harvested fruit away from contaminated areas;
* separate damaged fruit;
* transfer fruit carefully to the packing area.
The objective is to preserve quality from the tree through packing.
51. POSTHARVEST HANDLING
Mangoes are sensitive to physical damage.
Careful handling is necessary because bruised fruit can:
* ripen unevenly;
* deteriorate faster;
* develop quality problems;
* become more susceptible to spoilage.
The Farmers Advisory guide therefore emphasizes careful handling from picking through packing.
52. PACKING HOUSE
A packing house should be:
* clean;
* maintained properly;
* protected from contamination;
* organized to support safe handling.
The Philippine GAP example identifies the cleanliness of the packing house as part of field and packing sanitation.
53. QUALITY MANAGEMENT
Quality management begins before harvest.
Important factors include:
* variety;
* planting material;
* soil;
* climate;
* irrigation;
* fertilization;
* flowering;
* pest management;
* fruit bagging;
* maturity;
* harvesting;
* handling.
A high-quality mango is therefore the result of the entire production system rather than only the harvesting process.
54. FOOD SAFETY
The PhilGAP system places strong emphasis on food safety.
Food safety risks can arise from:
* pesticide residues;
* contaminated water;
* improper worker hygiene;
* animals;
* poor sanitation;
* improper chemical storage;
* contaminated equipment;
* inappropriate postharvest handling.
The goal is to prevent hazards before they reach the consumer.
55. IMPORTANT GAP PRINCIPLE: PREVENTION
The combined sources emphasize prevention rather than simply correcting problems after they occur.
Examples:
* choose a suitable site instead of correcting poor drainage later;
* use quality grafted planting materials;
* maintain sanitation before pest problems become severe;
* monitor pests regularly;
* use pesticides responsibly;
* protect workers before exposure occurs;
* maintain records before traceability becomes necessary;
* harvest at appropriate maturity;
* handle fruit carefully to prevent bruising.

	Planting material

	Training, PPE and sanitation
	Farm sanitation
	Clean farm and packing areas
	Animals
	Keep away from production areas
	Waste
	Segregate and dispose properly
	Records
	Document important farm operations
	Traceability
	Identify farm/source of harvested fruit
	Harvest
	Pick mature but firm fruit
	Postharvest
	Minimize bruising and contamination
	58. KEY PESTICIDE-SAFETY RULES FOR THE CAPSTONE
For a farming simulation, the source-supported pesticide-management logic can be represented as:
Identify pest → Monitor severity → Determine appropriate control → Select permitted pesticide if chemical control is necessary → Check label → Apply prescribed dosage → Use PPE → Record application → Observe required restrictions/pre-harvest interval → Harvest only when appropriate → Maintain traceability
This reflects the IPM and pesticide-management orientation of PhilGAP rather than treating pesticides as the automatic first response.
59. IMPORTANT MANGO PRODUCTION CONDITIONS

	60. SYNTHESIS FOR DOCUMENTATION / CAPSTONE PAPER
Mango production requires appropriate farm location, suitable climate and soil conditions, quality planting materials, adequate water and drainage, proper farm structures, and continuous cultural and farm management. The PhilGAP materials emphasize that mango production should be managed not only for productivity but also for food safety, environmental protection, worker health and safety, sanitation, and traceability. Suitable production begins with selecting a well-drained site with adequate sunlight and a reliable water source, followed by the use of quality planting materials, proper planting, irrigation, fertilization, weed management, pruning, and sanitation. Grafted planting materials are particularly appropriate for commercial production because they provide more predictable fruit characteristics and generally reach bearing age earlier than seed-grown trees (Agricultural Training Institute–Regional Training Center VII [ATI-RTC VII], n.d.; Farmers Advisory Editorial Team, 2026).
Mango flowering and fruit set are critical stages that require careful environmental and crop management. A dry period around flowering can support flowering, while wet and humid conditions may interfere with pollination and increase disease pressure. Fruit production should therefore be supported through appropriate water and nutrient management, pruning, pest monitoring, and fruit protection practices. Individual fruit bagging is also identified in Philippine GAP-aligned mango production as a method of protecting fruit from blemishes and insect infestation while reducing exposure to pesticides. Integrated pest management emphasizes regular monitoring, sanitation, appropriate cultural practices, and targeted treatment rather than unnecessary routine pesticide application (ATI-RTC VII, n.d.; Farmers Advisory Editorial Team, 2026).
Pesticide management is a major component of safe mango production because improper application can create risks to workers, consumers, the environment, and market access. PhilGAP emphasizes appropriate pesticide selection, prescribed dosage, worker protection, proper handling, record keeping, and consideration of maximum residue limits. Farm sanitation, water-quality management, proper waste disposal, animal exclusion, worker training, and clean packing facilities further reduce contamination risks. These practices are particularly relevant to export-oriented mango production, where residue and quality requirements can affect market acceptance (ATI-RTC VII, n.d.).
Harvesting should be performed when mangoes have reached appropriate maturity while remaining sufficiently firm for handling and transport. Harvesting too early can result in poor ripening, whereas leaving fruit too long on the tree can increase bruising and reduce shelf life. Careful handling from picking through packing is therefore necessary. Overall, mango production can be represented as an integrated farm-management cycle involving site selection, soil and water management, planting, orchard maintenance, flowering and fruit-set management, IPM, pesticide safety, worker protection, harvesting, postharvest handling, and traceability (Farmers Advisory Editorial Team, 2026; ATI-RTC VII, n.d.).
61. SHORTER SYNTHESIS FOR YOUR CAPSTONE
Proper mango production involves selecting a suitable, well-drained and fully sunlit farm site; using quality grafted planting materials; preparing the land and planting area; maintaining adequate irrigation and balanced fertilization; controlling weeds; pruning and training trees; and maintaining farm sanitation. During flowering and fruit development, farmers should carefully manage water, nutrients, pests, diseases, and fruit protection. Philippine Good Agricultural Practices further require responsible pesticide use, worker health and safety, waste management, water-quality protection, record keeping, and traceability to support safe and quality mango production (Agricultural Training Institute–Regional Training Center VII [ATI-RTC VII], n.d.; Farmers Advisory Editorial Team, 2026).

TOMATO
TOMATO PRODUCTION GUIDE 
1. TOMATO OVERVIEW
Tomato, locally known as kamatis, is an important vegetable crop cultivated in the Philippines. It is widely used as an ingredient in Filipino dishes and may be consumed fresh or processed into products such as paste and sauces. Tomatoes contain carotene and lycopene and are also a source of vitamin C. (Agricultural Training Institute–Regional Training Center VII [ATI-RTC VII], n.d.; Tababa, 2023).
Tomato production can be undertaken under different Philippine conditions, but the sources emphasize that crop performance is strongly affected by temperature, rainfall, drainage, soil condition, and variety selection. Cool and relatively dry conditions are generally favorable, while excessive rainfall increases the risk of fungal and bacterial diseases. (Tababa, 2023).
2. SOIL REQUIREMENTS
Tomato grows well in sandy-loam soil, although it can also grow in other soil types provided that drainage is adequate. The reported preferred soil pH is approximately 6.0–6.8. (Tababa, 2023).
The production guide also emphasizes fertile soil and the use of organic matter. Good drainage is particularly important because excessive water around the roots can contribute to plant problems. (ATI-RTC VII, n.d.; Tababa, 2023).
For organic tomato production, soil fertility is supported through the application of organic fertilizer, including the use of organic materials during plot preparation and crop maintenance. (ATI-RTC VII, n.d.).
3. CLIMATE REQUIREMENTS
Tomato generally performs best under a cool and dry climate. High nighttime temperatures can negatively affect fruiting. Heat-tolerant varieties may nevertheless be grown during hotter periods in low-elevation areas. (Tababa, 2023).
Planting during the wet season is discouraged where excessive rainfall creates favorable conditions for fungal and bacterial diseases. During wet periods, adequate drainage should therefore be maintained. (Tababa, 2023).
The source describes tomato planting schedules according to location:
* In low-elevation areas in Luzon, tomatoes may be planted from October to March when conditions are cooler and drier.
* Some slightly elevated areas such as Laguna and Quezon plant from approximately January to February.
* Some areas including Laguna, Quezon, Batangas, Nueva Ecija, Bukidnon, and Misamis Oriental also produce tomatoes during the wet season when market supply is lower. (Tababa, 2023).
4. SITE SELECTION
Select an area with:
* suitable soil;
* good drainage;
* adequate growing conditions;
* protection from excessive waterlogging.
For tomato production, sandy loam is preferred, but other soil types may be used if drainage is sufficient. Wet-season production requires particular attention to drainage because excessive moisture can encourage fungal and bacterial diseases. (Tababa, 2023).
5. VARIETY SELECTION
Variety selection should consider:
* yield potential;
* adaptability;
* marketability;
* resistance or tolerance to pests and diseases;
* planting season;
* intended market.
The source identifies different tomato types, including table tomatoes and processing tomatoes. Variety characteristics can differ in fruit size, color, shape, growth habit, maturity, and resistance to pests and diseases. (Tababa, 2023).
Heat-tolerant varieties are useful when tomato production is undertaken under hotter conditions. (Tababa, 2023).
6. PLOT PREPARATION FOR ORGANIC TOMATO
For the organic tomato production guide:
* Plow the garden or production plot.
* Prepare plots approximately 1 meter wide and 10–20 meters long.
* Dig planting holes approximately 15 cm deep.
* Maintain approximately 50 cm × 50 cm spacing between plants. (ATI-RTC VII, n.d.).
The plot should be prepared before transplanting so that seedlings can be transferred immediately after reaching the appropriate stage.
7. LAND PREPARATION
For the production procedure described by Tababa (2023):
1. Plow the soil manually or use a tractor.
2. Where tractors are used, plowing may be performed two to three times.
3. Plow to approximately 20–30 cm depth.
4. Level the soil after plowing.
5. Establish furrows approximately 1 meter apart.
6. Apply animal manure as basal fertilizer approximately two weeks before transplanting.
Good land preparation establishes a suitable growing environment for the seedlings and helps provide adequate drainage and root development.
8. SEEDBED AND SEED BOX PREPARATION
Seeds should first be grown in a seedbed or seed box to make germination, monitoring, and early seedling management easier. (Tababa, 2023).
The seedbed should be:
* raised approximately 20–30 cm;
* positioned so that excess water can drain properly.
A seed box can be used for smaller-scale production. (Tababa, 2023).
A suitable seedling medium consists of:
* compost;
* coir dust;
* carbonized rice hull.
A plastic roof may be installed above the seedbed or seed box to protect young seedlings from heavy rainfall. (Tababa, 2023).
9. SOWING OF TOMATO SEEDS
Before sowing:
1. Water the seedbed.
2. Create small furrows approximately 2–4 cm deep.
3. Maintain approximately 15 cm between furrows.
4. Sow seeds approximately 2.5 cm apart.
5. Cover the seeds lightly with compost.
The seedbed can then be covered with:
* paper;
* coconut coir;
* rice hull; or
* rice straw.
The covering helps conserve moisture. Once seedlings emerge, the cover should be removed, generally after approximately 4–5 days. (Tababa, 2023).
The organic tomato guide specifies growing tomato seeds in a seed box for approximately 14 days before pricking and transplanting. (ATI-RTC VII, n.d.).
10. SEEDLING CARE
After emergence, seedlings should be monitored closely.
Seedlings that are too close together should be carefully removed and transferred to provide sufficient space for uniform development. This process is called pricking. (Tababa, 2023).
For seedlings grown in trays or boxes:
* prick seedlings;
* transfer them into another seed box or appropriate growing container;
* provide sufficient space;
* avoid excessive moisture.
Watering should preferably be done in the morning or mid-afternoon. Watering late in the afternoon or evening can encourage disease development. (Tababa, 2023).
11. HARDENING OF SEEDLINGS
Before transplanting, seedlings should be gradually acclimatized to field conditions.
The hardening process involves:
* gradually exposing seedlings to sunlight;
* reducing the amount of watering;
* continuing this process for approximately one week before transplanting.
This helps seedlings adjust to the environmental conditions they will encounter after field establishment. (Tababa, 2023).
12. TRANSPLANTING
Tomato seedlings are ready for transplanting when they have approximately 3–5 true leaves. The source indicates that transplanting generally occurs approximately 3–4 weeks after seed emergence. (Tababa, 2023).
For field transplanting:
1. Select healthy seedlings.
2. Ensure seedlings have approximately 3–5 leaves.
3. Transplant one seedling per planting position.
4. Maintain approximately 50–70 cm between plants.
5. Maintain approximately 1 meter between rows.
6. Press soil gently around the seedling base.
7. Water immediately after transplanting.
8. Replace missing plants approximately 5–7 days after transplanting.
For the organic tomato production procedure, seedlings are grown for approximately 14 days, then pricked and transplanted into prepared plots. (ATI-RTC VII, n.d.).
13. MULCHING AFTER TRANSPLANTING
After transplanting, the soil can be covered using:
* rice straw; or
* plastic mulch.
Mulching helps:
* conserve soil moisture;
* suppress weed growth.
(Tababa, 2023).
For organic production, organic materials are particularly relevant as soil-covering and fertility-management materials.
14. ORGANIC FERTILIZER APPLICATION
The organic tomato production guide specifies applying organic fertilizer at approximately 1 kilogram per hill. (ATI-RTC VII, n.d.).
Organic fertilizer is therefore incorporated into the planting and crop-management system rather than relying solely on synthetic fertilizers.
The source also specifies that organic fertilizer should be applied approximately every 15 days during crop maintenance. (ATI-RTC VII, n.d.).
15. FERTILIZER MANAGEMENT
The general production guide recommends analyzing the soil first to determine the available nutrients before fertilizer application. (Tababa, 2023).
The source describes:
* complete basal fertilizer 14-14-14 at 20 g per hill, applied 3–5 days after transplanting;
* after two weeks, urea (46-0-0) and muriate of potash (0-0-60) are combined at a 2:1 ratio and applied approximately 6–8 cm from the plant base;
* the fertilizer combination is applied again after another two weeks.
For the organic production system, organic fertilizer is emphasized instead of relying exclusively on the synthetic fertilizer schedule. (ATI-RTC VII, n.d.).
16. ORGANIC FOLIAR AND BIOLOGICAL INPUTS
The production information identifies several organic inputs that may be used according to plant vigor, including:
* Vermitea
* Fermented Plant Juice (FPJ)
* Fermented Fruit Juice (FFJ)
* Fish Amino Acid (FAA)
* water-soluble calcium
The source states that these may be sprayed approximately every 2–3 weeks, depending on plant vigor. (Tababa, 2023).
17. TRELLISING
Tomato plants require support to maintain upright growth and prevent fruit from touching the soil.
The organic production guide recommends:
* providing support approximately one month after transplanting;
* using a one-foot bamboo stick;
* supporting the tomato stem to encourage straight growth. (ATI-RTC VII, n.d.).
The general production guide describes trellising as a structure for supporting vertical plant growth. Bamboo or ipil-ipil poles may be used as posts. Trellising helps prevent fruit from touching the soil. (Tababa, 2023).
Trellising in the general guide begins approximately one week after transplanting. Branches are tied to the support and trained using plastic straws. The guide identifies an “A” type trellis as the preferred trellis structure. Branch training is performed approximately six times during the growing season. (Tababa, 2023).
18. WATER MANAGEMENT
Water management should be adjusted according to:
* weather;
* soil type;
* crop growth stage.
Mulching with rice straw or plastic helps conserve soil moisture, particularly during dry conditions. (Tababa, 2023).
During the fruiting stage, irrigation is performed approximately once a week. At harvesting, irrigation is reduced to approximately once every 10 days. (Tababa, 2023).
During wet periods, drainage canals should be constructed to prevent waterlogging. (Tababa, 2023).
For the organic tomato guide, plants should be watered every day, preferably early in the morning. (ATI-RTC VII, n.d.).
19. WEED MANAGEMENT
Weeds around the tomato plant should be removed because they compete with the crop for:
* water;
* nutrients.
Mulching with:
* rice straw;
* black plastic;
* silver plastic;
can reduce weed growth. (Tababa, 2023).
For non-mulched plots, weeding is performed approximately 2–3 times during the growing season. The source also recommends trimming weeds along rows rather than completely eliminating them in order to encourage natural enemies of pests. (Tababa, 2023).
20. PRUNING
Pruning involves selectively removing side shoots to:
* improve fruit size;
* improve fruit uniformity;
* improve air circulation;
* reduce conditions favorable to disease development. (Tababa, 2023).
The recommended pruning activities include:
* removing old leaves below fruit clusters;
* removing newly sprouting branches;
* maintaining approximately 3–4 main branches.
(Tababa, 2023).
21. CROP MONITORING
Regular crop monitoring should include checking:
* plant growth;
* soil moisture;
* weed competition;
* fruit development;
* pest presence;
* disease symptoms;
* nutrient condition;
* support structures;
* drainage.
Early detection allows problems to be addressed before they become severe.
The organic guide recommends spraying probiotics or organic pesticide once a week as part of its care and management practices. (ATI-RTC VII, n.d.).
22. ORGANIC PEST MANAGEMENT
The organic production system emphasizes the use of organic pest-management practices, including probiotics/organic pesticide applications once a week. (ATI-RTC VII, n.d.).
The general tomato guide emphasizes appropriate management according to pest and disease occurrence and recognizes that production conditions influence pest pressure. (Tababa, 2023).
Maintaining proper spacing, drainage, pruning, sanitation, and crop vigor is important because these practices contribute to healthier crop conditions.
23. DISEASE MANAGEMENT THROUGH CULTURAL PRACTICES
The sources emphasize avoiding excessive moisture and improving air circulation.
Important practices include:
* selecting well-drained land;
* constructing drainage canals during wet periods;
* avoiding excessive irrigation;
* watering at appropriate times;
* pruning old and unnecessary leaves;
* maintaining proper plant spacing;
* using trellises to keep fruits away from soil;
* removing damaged plant material.
Wet-season production is particularly associated with greater fungal and bacterial disease concerns. (Tababa, 2023).
24. REPLANTING MISSING PLANTS
After transplanting, some seedlings may fail to establish.
The general guide recommends inspecting the field and replanting missing hills approximately 5–7 days after transplanting. (Tababa, 2023).
This maintains a more uniform plant population throughout the plot.
25. CONTAINER PRODUCTION
Tomatoes may also be grown in:
* plastic bags;
* containers.
This option can be used when available planting space is limited. (Tababa, 2023).
26. FLOWERING AND FRUIT DEVELOPMENT
During flowering and fruit development, management should focus on:
* adequate water;
* appropriate nutrition;
* plant support;
* pruning;
* pest and disease monitoring.
The source specifically identifies calcium nitrate or calcium phosphate application at the onset of flowering in its general fertilizer recommendations. (Tababa, 2023).
27. HARVESTING TIME
The organic tomato production guide indicates that harvesting begins approximately 80–90 days after planting. (ATI-RTC VII, n.d.).
The general production guide reports that the first harvest usually begins approximately 50–60 days after transplanting and that harvesting can occur approximately 2–3 times per week. (Tababa, 2023).
The difference in timing is associated with the different starting points used by the sources after planting versus after transplanting so the values should not be treated as interchangeable measurements.
28. FRUIT MATURITY FOR HARVEST
The appropriate maturity stage depends on:
* the destination market;
* transportation distance;
* intended use;
* desired ripening stage.
For long-distance markets, tomatoes may be harvested at the mature-green stage. For farms located closer to the market, fruits may be harvested at the breaker stage, when an orange or reddish color begins appearing on the peel. (Tababa, 2023).
The red-ripe stage is identified as the best stage for fruit maturity, but fruits at this stage are more susceptible to postharvest losses. (Tababa, 2023).
29. HARVESTING PROCEDURE
When harvesting:
1. Select fruits according to the intended market.
2. Harvest at the appropriate maturity stage.
3. Handle fruits carefully.
4. Avoid bruising.
5. Place fruits gently into the harvesting container.
6. Separate damaged fruits.
7. Group fruits of similar maturity together.
(Tababa, 2023).
30. POSTHARVEST HANDLING
After harvesting, tomatoes should be handled carefully because physical damage can reduce market quality.
Recommended practices include:
* use plastic or wooden crates;
* line crates with banana leaves or used newspaper;
* remove bruised and damaged fruits;
* pack fruits of similar maturity together.
(Tababa, 2023).
31. HARVESTING AND MARKET DISTANCE
Harvest maturity should be selected according to transportation requirements.
Long-distance market
Harvest at:
mature green stage
This provides more time for the fruit to reach the market while continuing to develop its color.
Nearby market
Harvest at:
breaker stage
This is when an orange or reddish color begins appearing.
Immediate/local consumption
Harvesting at:
red-ripe stage
provides a fully mature fruit but increases vulnerability to postharvest damage. (Tababa, 2023).

34. ORGANIC TOMATO PRODUCTION MANAGEMENT
The organic production sequence can be summarized as:
Organic plot preparation → Seed-box production → 14-day seedling growth → Pricking → Transplanting → Organic fertilizer → Daily watering → Trellising → Regular organic fertilizer → Weekly organic pesticide/probiotic → Weed management → Harvest at 80–90 days after planting.
(ATI-RTC VII, n.d.)
The guide specifically identifies organic fertilizer, daily watering, regular fertilizer application, organic pesticide/probiotic application, and trellising as core crop-management activities.

	36. SYNTHESIS FOR DOCUMENTATION / CAPSTONE PAPER
Tomato production requires suitable soil, favorable climatic conditions, quality seedlings, proper land preparation, and continuous crop management. Tomato grows well in sandy-loam soil with good drainage and is generally favored by cool and relatively dry conditions, while excessive rainfall can increase fungal and bacterial disease problems. Proper production begins with seedbed or seed-box preparation, appropriate sowing, seedling care, pricking, hardening, and transplanting of healthy seedlings. For organic production, the use of organic fertilizer and organic pest-management inputs forms an important part of crop management (Agricultural Training Institute–Regional Training Center VII [ATI-RTC VII], n.d.; Tababa, 2023).
After establishment, tomato plants require regular irrigation, fertilizer application, weed management, mulching, trellising, pruning, and monitoring of pests and diseases. Trellising supports upright growth and prevents fruits from touching the soil, while pruning improves air circulation and helps reduce disease development. Soil moisture should be managed according to weather and crop stage, with drainage provided during wet conditions to prevent waterlogging. Organic tomato production further emphasizes regular organic fertilizer application and weekly application of probiotics or organic pesticide (ATI-RTC VII, n.d.; Tababa, 2023).
Harvesting should be based on the maturity stage appropriate for the intended market. Tomatoes for long-distance markets may be harvested at the mature-green stage, while tomatoes intended for nearby markets may be harvested at the breaker stage. Red-ripe tomatoes provide greater maturity but are more susceptible to postharvest losses. Careful harvesting, sorting, and packaging are therefore necessary to minimize bruising and mechanical damage and to maintain fruit quality during transportation (Tababa, 2023).
37. COMBINED PARAGRAPH FOR CAPSTONE
Tomato production requires suitable soil and climatic conditions, quality planting materials, proper land preparation, and continuous crop management from seedling establishment to harvesting. Tomato performs well in well-drained soil, particularly sandy loam, and is generally favored by cool and relatively dry conditions, while excessive wetness can increase fungal and bacterial disease risks. Production involves seedbed preparation, sowing, seedling care, pricking, hardening, transplanting, organic fertilization, irrigation, trellising, pruning, weed management, and pest and disease monitoring. In organic production, organic fertilizer is applied regularly and probiotics or organic pesticides are incorporated into crop care. Harvesting is conducted according to fruit maturity and market distance, followed by careful sorting and packaging to minimize mechanical damage and maintain quality (Agricultural Training Institute–Regional Training Center VII [ATI-RTC VII], n.d.; Tababa, 2023).
