PINEAPPLE
PINEAPPLE PRODUCTION GUIDE 
1. PINEAPPLE OVERVIEW
* Scientific name: Ananas comosus.
* Pineapple belongs to the Bromeliaceae family.
* It is a perennial, monocotyledonous plant.
* The fruit is a composite fruit formed from the coalescence of individual berries.
* Pineapple is cultivated mainly in tropical and subtropical regions.
* It is commercially important for:
   * fresh consumption;
   * canned products;
   * juice;
   * processed food;
   * other food products.
* Pineapple also contains bromelain, an enzyme with commercial and food-related applications.
* The Micronesian guide emphasizes sustainable, climate-smart and organic commercial production, including quality planting material, nursery management, appropriate fertilization, and integrated pest and disease management.
2. CLIMATE REQUIREMENTS
Pineapple is adapted to tropical conditions and can tolerate relatively dry conditions because of its physiological characteristics.
The Micronesian guide reports that pineapple:
* is native to the American tropics;
* is generally cultivated at low elevations;
* can produce fruit across a broad range of annual precipitation depending on cultivar and location;
* has considerable drought tolerance.
The guide reports annual precipitation ranging from approximately 25–150 inches, depending on cultivar, location, and environmental conditions.
For production planning, climate should therefore be considered together with:
* rainfall amount;
* rainfall distribution;
* temperature;
* sunlight;
* humidity;
* water availability;
* drainage.
The COLEAD guide specifically treats temperature, rainfall, relative humidity, insolation, and water as separate crop requirements.
3. CLIMATE AND WATER MANAGEMENT
Pineapple can withstand periods of drought, but drought does not mean that irrigation is unnecessary.
Water management becomes particularly important when:
* rainfall is irregular;
* the crop is planted in upland areas;
* the soil has limited water-holding capacity;
* the dry season is prolonged;
* crop water demand exceeds available rainfall.
A 2023 Philippine study conducted in an upland and rolling production area in Bataan found that supplemental irrigation improved pineapple production conditions. The study used micro-sprinklers, soil-moisture measurements, and evapotranspiration monitoring to schedule irrigation.
4. SOIL REQUIREMENTS
Pineapple requires soil conditions that permit:
* good root development;
* adequate drainage;
* sufficient aeration;
* nutrient availability;
* effective water management.
The COLEAD guide treats soil as a major crop requirement and includes:
* soil selection;
* soil preparation;
* soil fertility;
* soil analysis;
* organic soil improvement;
* integrated soil fertility management.
Poor drainage should be avoided because excessive soil moisture can increase the risk of root and heart diseases.
5. SOIL PREPARATION
The COLEAD production system includes several soil-preparation operations:
1. Site selection
2. Assessment of the preceding crop
3. Topography assessment
4. Land development
5. Ploughing
6. Burial/incorporation of residues
7. Subsoiling when necessary
8. Harrowing
9. Ridging
10. Application of soil improvers
11. Bottom dressing
12. Organic soil improvement
The objective is to create a suitable rooting environment while maintaining soil structure, drainage, and fertility.
6. SITE SELECTION
A suitable pineapple site should be assessed for:
* topography;
* drainage;
* soil condition;
* previous crop;
* water availability;
* accessibility;
* potential pest and disease problems;
* environmental risks.
The COLEAD guide specifically includes preceding crop and topography as considerations in plot selection.
7. SLOPING AND UPLAND AREAS
Pineapple can be grown in upland and rolling areas when appropriate water and soil-management practices are used.
A 2023 study in Bataan used an upland/rolling site with approximately 17–30% slope. Pineapple was planted along contour lines using a double-row triangular arrangement.
This is important because sloping production areas can experience:
* runoff;
* soil erosion;
* uneven water distribution;
* dry-season water deficits.
Contour-oriented planting and appropriate water management can therefore be incorporated into production planning.
8. ORGANIC SOIL IMPROVEMENT
The COLEAD guide describes the use of cover crops and organic materials to improve soil condition.
Examples include:
* Mucuna pruriens
* Vigna unguiculata
* Crotalaria
These cover crops can contribute nitrogen and potassium after their biomass is incorporated into the soil.
Organic fertilizers such as compost can:
* improve soil condition;
* contribute potassium;
* improve micronutrient availability.
9. NUTRIENT REQUIREMENTS
The COLEAD guide treats pineapple nutrition as an integrated management activity.
The nutrients specifically discussed include:
* nitrogen;
* potassium;
* phosphorus;
* magnesium;
* calcium;
* boron;
* copper;
* iron;
* manganese;
* zinc;
* sulphur;
* molybdenum.
Nutrient management should therefore not be based simply on applying a fixed fertilizer amount. The guide includes:
* soil analysis;
* leaf analysis;
* identification of nutrient deficiency symptoms;
* fertilization planning;
* fertilizer selection;
* application rate;
* application method;
* application timing.
10. NITROGEN
Nitrogen is important for:
* vegetative growth;
* leaf development;
* crop development;
* eventual fruit production.
However, nitrogen application should be managed according to crop requirements and soil conditions rather than applied excessively.
The COLEAD guide includes nitrogen as one of the principal macronutrients in its fertilization program.
11. POTASSIUM
Potassium is another major nutrient requirement.
It is associated with:
* plant development;
* fruit development;
* fruit quality;
* crop productivity.
The COLEAD guide gives potassium its own nutrient-management section and includes it in fertilization planning.
12. PHOSPHORUS
Phosphorus is included among the major nutrients required by pineapple.
Its management should be based on:
* soil condition;
* crop requirement;
* soil analysis;
* appropriate fertilizer planning.
13. SECONDARY AND MICRONUTRIENTS
The COLEAD guide also identifies:
* magnesium;
* calcium;
* boron;
* copper;
* iron;
* manganese;
* zinc;
* sulphur;
* molybdenum.
Nutrient deficiencies should be diagnosed rather than assumed, because visual symptoms may have different causes. Soil and leaf analysis are therefore part of the recommended nutritional diagnosis process.
14. PLANTING MATERIAL
Pineapple is propagated vegetatively.
The production guides discuss different planting materials and production systems.
Planting materials may include:
* suckers/offshoots;
* slips;
* crowns;
* tissue-culture-derived plantlets.
The COLEAD guide places strong emphasis on plant-material quality before planting.
15. QUALITY OF PLANTING MATERIAL
Good planting material should be:
* healthy;
* vigorous;
* appropriately sized;
* free from visible pest and disease problems;
* suitable for the selected production system;
* properly prepared before planting.
The COLEAD guide specifically includes:
* offshoot quality;
* grading;
* trimming;
* treatment;
* biological treatment.
16. TISSUE-CULTURE PLANTING MATERIAL
The Micronesian guide gives particular emphasis to micropropagation.
The research program was designed to produce:
* uniform plants;
* disease-free plants;
* high-quality planting material;
* large quantities of planting material;
* year-round availability.
The system includes:
1. In-vitro multiplication
2. Production of elite plantlets
3. Greenhouse acclimatization
4. Nursery management
5. Field establishment
17. VARIETY SELECTION
The COLEAD guide discusses several commercially important pineapple cultivars, including:
* Smooth Cayenne
* Sugarloaf
* MD2
* Red Spanish
* Queen Victoria
* Perolera
Different cultivars have different characteristics relating to:
* fruit size;
* fruit appearance;
* flavor;
* market;
* maturity;
* production characteristics;
* processing or fresh-fruit suitability.
Therefore, cultivar selection should consider the intended market and production environment.
18. INTERCROPPING
The COLEAD guide includes:
* intercropping;
* crop associations;
* crop rotation
as part of pre-planting decisions.
These practices should be evaluated according to:
* available land;
* crop competition;
* nutrient requirements;
* water availability;
* pest and disease considerations;
* farm objectives.
19. CROP ROTATION
Crop rotation can be used as part of production planning.
The purpose is to avoid continuously maintaining the same crop under conditions that may encourage:
* pest buildup;
* disease carryover;
* soil degradation.
The COLEAD guide therefore treats preceding crop and crop rotation as part of plot management.
20. OFFSHOOT PRODUCTION
The COLEAD guide provides extensive procedures for producing pineapple offshoots.
Methods discussed include:
* offshoot production from harvested pineapple fields;
* tissue-culture or vitroplants;
* growth-regulator methods;
* trunk-section methods;
* floral ablation/castration;
* crown propagation;
* temporary immersion bioreactors;
* etiolation.
The guide also discusses:
* offshoot nutrition;
* harvesting;
* storage.
21. PREPARATION OF OFFSHOOTS
Before planting:
1. Inspect the offshoot.
2. Grade planting material.
3. Remove unsuitable material.
4. Trim when required.
5. Treat planting material according to the production system.
6. Use biological treatment where appropriate.
7. Plant only suitable material.
22. PLANTING PERIOD
Planting time should consider:
* rainfall;
* soil moisture;
* availability of planting materials;
* expected dry period;
* local climate;
* irrigation availability.
The COLEAD guide has a dedicated section on planting periods, emphasizing that planting decisions should be adapted to local production conditions.
23. PLANTING DENSITY
The COLEAD guide discusses several planting arrangements:
* single-row planting;
* double or paired-row planting;
* multiple-row planting.
Plant density affects:
* fruit size;
* yield per hectare;
* competition;
* field management;
* weed growth;
* air circulation;
* harvesting operations.
24. DOUBLE-ROW TRIANGULAR PLANTING
A 2023 Philippine study used:
* double-row triangular planting;
* approximately 0.5 m between plants;
* approximately 1.0 m between double rows;
* contour-line planting in an upland/rolling site.
Rice hull was applied after planting at approximately 5 cm thickness as mulch and as an organic material that decomposes over time.
25. PLANTING PROCEDURE
A practical sequence from the guides is:
1. Select the prepared field.
2. Establish planting rows.
3. Determine planting spacing.
4. Prepare and grade planting materials.
5. Trim or treat planting materials as necessary.
6. Place the offshoot/planting material at the appropriate depth.
7. Firm the soil around the plant.
8. Maintain appropriate soil moisture.
9. Begin regular crop monitoring.
26. WATER MANAGEMENT
Water management is a major part of pineapple production.
The COLEAD guide includes:
* irrigation;
* sprinkler irrigation;
* drip irrigation;
* water-use efficiency;
* water productivity;
* water-saving techniques.
27. SPRINKLER IRRIGATION
Sprinkler irrigation can provide supplemental water when rainfall is insufficient.
The 2023 Philippine study used a micro-sprinkler system with:
* 90 L/hour sprinkler capacity;
* approximately 5.5–12.5 m wetted diameter depending on the experimental setup;
* soil-moisture monitoring;
* evapotranspiration monitoring.
28. SOIL-MOISTURE-BASED IRRIGATION
The 2023 Philippine study evaluated four water-management treatments:
* T1: no supplemental irrigation;
* T2: irrigation up to 30% soil moisture content;
* T3: irrigation up to 50% soil moisture content;
* T4: irrigation up to 70% soil moisture content.
Soil moisture was measured at approximately 10 cm depth using a digital soil-moisture meter.
29. EVAPOTRANSPIRATION MONITORING
The 2023 study used an atmometer to estimate daily evapotranspiration.
The reported daily evapotranspiration range was:
1.0–6.0 mm/day.
The researchers used evapotranspiration readings together with soil moisture to support irrigation scheduling.
30. WATER-SAVING MANAGEMENT
Water-saving practices should focus on:
* monitoring soil moisture;
* avoiding unnecessary irrigation;
* matching irrigation with crop demand;
* considering rainfall;
* using efficient irrigation systems;
* conserving soil moisture;
* managing soil organic matter.
The COLEAD guide specifically includes water efficiency and water-saving techniques as part of sustainable production.
31. FERTILIZATION PROGRAM
The COLEAD guide recommends developing a fertilization program based on:
1. Soil analysis
2. Leaf analysis
3. Nutritional diagnosis
4. Crop requirements
5. Fertilizer type
6. Application rate
7. Application method
8. Application timing
This prevents fertilizer management from becoming a simple one-size-fits-all procedure.
32. FERTILIZER APPLICATION METHODS
The guide discusses fertilizer application methods and timing as separate management decisions.
Depending on the production system, fertilizer may be managed through:
* soil application;
* side dressing;
* other appropriate field application methods;
* foliar nutrition when appropriate.
The Micronesian system specifically describes top and side dressings for uniform fertilizer application.
33. ORGANIC FERTILIZATION
Organic production can use:
* compost;
* organic fertilizers;
* cover-crop residues;
* other appropriate organic soil amendments.
The objective is to:
* improve soil condition;
* provide nutrients;
* maintain soil biological activity;
* improve nutrient availability.
34. WEED MANAGEMENT
Weed management is important because weeds compete with pineapple for:
* water;
* nutrients;
* sunlight;
* growing space.
The COLEAD guide organizes weed management into:
* chemical control;
* biological and cultural control;
* mechanical control.
35. CULTURAL WEED MANAGEMENT
Cultural practices can include:
* maintaining appropriate plant density;
* mulching;
* crop rotation;
* cover crops;
* maintaining healthy crop growth;
* preventing excessive weed establishment.
The 2023 Bataan study used rice-hull mulch after planting to suppress unwanted plants and provide organic material as it decomposed.
36. MULCHING
Mulching can:
* reduce weed emergence;
* conserve soil moisture;
* reduce soil temperature fluctuations;
* contribute organic matter;
* reduce erosion in appropriate situations.
The 2023 Philippine study applied approximately 5 cm of rice hull after planting.
37. FLOWER INDUCTION
Pineapple does not always flower uniformly under field conditions.
The COLEAD guide therefore provides an extensive section on flower induction.
Methods discussed include:
* calcium carbide/acetylene treatment;
* gaseous ethylene;
* ethephon;
* aviglycin;
* ethylene-enriched activated carbon;
* Ethrel;
* Zeothene;
* combinations involving Ethrel, urea and calcium carbonate;
* naphthaleneacetic acid;
* paclobutrazol;
* approaches for organic production.
For a production system, flower induction should be performed only using locally permitted products and according to applicable labels and regulations.
38. BEST PERIOD FOR FLOWER INDUCTION
The COLEAD guide treats the timing of flower induction as a separate management decision.
The appropriate timing depends on:
* plant development;
* cultivar;
* production objective;
* climate;
* intended harvest period;
* market requirements.
39. INTEGRATED PEST MANAGEMENT
The COLEAD guide recommends an Integrated Pest Management (IPM) approach.
The IPM sequence includes:
1. Identify the pest.
2. Define an action threshold.
3. Monitor the crop.
4. Prevent pest establishment.
5. Apply control measures when necessary.
40. MAIN INSECT AND OTHER PESTS
The COLEAD guide specifically covers:
* nematodes;
* symphylids;
* mealybugs;
* scale insects;
* fruit borers;
* mites.
41. NEMATODES
Nematodes can affect the root system.
Damage to roots may reduce:
* water uptake;
* nutrient uptake;
* plant vigor;
* crop development.
Therefore, pest monitoring should include underground/root-related problems, particularly when plants show unexplained poor growth.
42. SYMPHYLIDS
Symphylids are included among the major pineapple pests in the COLEAD guide.
Because they affect the root environment, management should be integrated with:
* soil preparation;
* field sanitation;
* monitoring;
* appropriate pest-control measures.
43. MEALYBUGS
Mealybugs are among the major pests covered by the guide.
Their management should consider:
* monitoring;
* prevention;
* control;
* relationships with other organisms in the production system.
The Micronesian guide also lists ants associated with mealybugs.
44. SCALE INSECTS
Scale insects are another pest group identified in the COLEAD production guide.
Regular crop inspection is important for early detection and management.
45. FRUIT BORERS
Fruit borers are included among the major pineapple pests.
Management should involve:
* monitoring;
* prevention;
* appropriate intervention when action thresholds are reached;
* protection of developing fruit.
46. MITES
Mites are also included in the major pest section.
Regular monitoring is necessary because pest problems can affect:
* plant growth;
* leaves;
* fruit development;
* final market quality.
47. MAJOR FUNGAL DISEASES
The COLEAD guide discusses:
* root rot;
* green fruit rot;
* heart rot/top rot;
* butt rot/base rot;
* black rot/water blister;
* white leaf spot;
* Fusarium;
* fruitlet core rot/eye rot/black spot;
* fruit rot caused by yeast.
48. HEART AND ROOT ROT
The Micronesian guide identifies:
* Phytophthora cinnamomi — heart and root rot;
* Phytophthora parasitica — heart rot;
* Pythium spp. — root rot.
Good drainage, healthy planting material and appropriate disease-management practices are important components of prevention.
49. BLACK ROT AND BUTT ROT
The Micronesian guide identifies:
* Ceratocystis paradoxa — black rot;
* Thielaviopsis paradoxa — butt rot.
These diseases reinforce the importance of:
* healthy planting material;
* sanitation;
* proper handling;
* avoiding unnecessary injury;
* appropriate field and postharvest management.
50. BACTERIAL DISEASES
The COLEAD guide includes:
* pink disease;
* bacterial fruit heart rot;
* fruit collapse;
* pineapple marbling disease.
51. VIRAL DISEASES
The COLEAD guide includes:
* yellow spot;
* wilt disease.
Disease management should begin with prevention and monitoring rather than relying only on chemical intervention.
52. FIELD SANITATION
Field sanitation should include:
* removal of diseased material when appropriate;
* proper management of crop residues;
* use of healthy planting materials;
* prevention of pest and disease carryover;
* appropriate crop rotation;
* removal of severely affected plants when required.
The Micronesian guide ultimately recommends clearing the field after the ratoon cycle to minimize carryover of pests and diseases.
53. RATOON CROPPING
Pineapple can be maintained as a ratoon crop after the original plant crop.
The Micronesian guide describes:
* retaining the plant crop after harvest;
* maintaining the first ratoon;
* potentially maintaining a second ratoon;
* selecting only suitable suckers;
* removing excess suckers;
* continuing crop nutrition and pest management.
The guide reports:
* first crop: approximately 15–22 months after planting;
* second crop: approximately 32–36 months after initial planting;
* third crop: approximately 40–44 months after initial planting.
After the ratoon cycle, field clearing is recommended to reduce pest and disease carryover.
54. RATOON MANAGEMENT
After harvesting the plant crop:
1. Chop side leaves where necessary.
2. Retain approximately 1–2 suitable suckers per mother plant.
3. Remove excess suckers.
4. Maintain nutrient management.
5. Continue pest and disease monitoring.
6. Harvest the ratoon crop.
7. Repeat appropriate management if a second ratoon is retained.
8. Clear the field after the production cycle.
55. PRE-HARVEST FRUIT CARE
The COLEAD guide identifies pre-harvest fruit care as an important stage.
It specifically includes:
* protection against sunburn;
* control of insect pests affecting fruit;
* preparation for harvest.
56. SUNBURN PROTECTION
Developing fruit can be damaged by excessive exposure to sunlight.
Therefore, pre-harvest management should consider:
* canopy/plant protection;
* crop density;
* field conditions;
* other appropriate methods for reducing excessive fruit exposure.
The COLEAD guide identifies fruit protection against sunburn as a dedicated pre-harvest activity.
57. FRUIT MATURITY
The Micronesian guide identifies visible maturity indicators.
For Hawaiian pineapple, fruit is considered ripe when:
* individual eyes become flattened;
* eyes become glossy;
* shell color changes from dark greenish-purple toward yellow-red or yellow-orange.
For Kosraean pineapple:
* eyes become flattened;
* eyes become glossy;
* shell color changes from dark green toward yellow or yellow-orange.
Color development begins at the base of the fruit and progresses toward the top.
58. HARVEST TIMING
The Micronesian guide reports that in its production environment:
* pineapple fruits are harvested approximately 4–7 months after flower initiation;
* or approximately 15–22 months after planting.
Harvest timing should depend on:
* cultivar;
* intended market;
* required maturity;
* transport distance;
* storage requirements.
59. HARVESTING FOR FRESH MARKET
Fruit intended for immediate fresh consumption can be harvested at a more advanced maturity stage when appropriate.
The guide states that:
* harvesting before full ripening increases postharvest storage life;
* harvesting ripe fruit is preferable when the objective is the best fresh-fruit quality for immediate consumption.
60. HARVESTING PROCEDURE
The Micronesian guide describes harvesting by:
1. Bending the fruit over by hand.
2. Twisting the fruit to remove it from the stalk.
3. Handling the fruit carefully to prevent damage.
Harvesting should avoid unnecessary mechanical injury because damaged fruit is more vulnerable to postharvest deterioration.
61. DEGREE OF RIPENESS AND MARKET
The COLEAD guide includes:
* harvest period;
* harvesting;
* minimum quality characteristics;
* ripeness criteria;
* product classification.
Therefore, harvest decisions should not rely only on fruit color. Market destination and required maturity should also be considered.
62. COMMERCIAL QUALITY
The COLEAD guide specifically provides a commercial-quality framework covering:
* minimum quality characteristics;
* product ripeness criteria;
* product classification.
These characteristics are important for ensuring that harvested fruit meets the requirements of the intended market.
63. YIELD
Yield is affected by:
* cultivar;
* planting density;
* planting material;
* soil fertility;
* water availability;
* pest and disease management;
* flower induction;
* crop maintenance;
* climate;
* harvesting practices.
The 2021 pineapple production guide reports that yield can vary considerably according to variety, production conditions and management.
64. 2023 PHILIPPINE WATER-MANAGEMENT FINDINGS
The 2023 Bataan study provides particularly useful information for Philippine production.
The study reported that the treatment receiving irrigation up to 70% soil moisture content had:
* approximately 72.7 cm plant height;
* approximately 109.5 cm crown diameter;
* approximately 25.7 leaves.
It also had the highest number of large-size fruit in the experiment.
However, these values are experimental results from the Bataan study, not universal requirements for every pineapple farm.
65. 2023 FRUIT-SIZE OBSERVATIONS
The Bataan study classified harvested fruit as:
* Extra Small — approximately 6.3 cm height and 4.8 cm diameter
* Small — approximately 7.3 cm height and 5.5 cm diameter
* Medium — approximately 8.1 cm height and 5.8 cm diameter
* Large — approximately 8.8 cm height and 5.9 cm diameter
The proportion of fruit by size was reported as approximately:
* Extra Small: 13.2%
* Small: 24.0%
* Medium: 27.1%
* Large: 35.6%
66. 2023 ORGANIC PRODUCTION MANAGEMENT
The Bataan study used:
* pineapple suckers;
* foliar organic fertilizer;
* micro-sprinkler irrigation;
* soil-moisture monitoring;
* evapotranspiration monitoring;
* regular weeding;
* pest and disease monitoring.
Foliar organic fertilizer was applied approximately every two weeks in the study.
67. POSTHARVEST STORAGE
The Micronesian guide recommends storing harvested pineapples at approximately:
* 44–46°F;
* 80–90% relative humidity;
* with adequate air circulation.
It warns that:
* temperatures of 40°F and below can cause chilling injury and breakdown;
* storage may generally be limited to approximately 4–6 weeks under the stated conditions.
68. POSTHARVEST HANDLING
After harvesting:
1. Handle fruit carefully.
2. Avoid bruising.
3. Sort according to quality and maturity.
4. Separate damaged or defective fruit.
5. Maintain appropriate storage temperature.
6. Maintain adequate relative humidity.
7. Provide air circulation.
8. Transport according to market requirements.
The COLEAD guide includes packing, trimming, sorting, brushing, washing, draining, transport and market requirements as postharvest operations.
69. PACKING
The COLEAD guide identifies several postharvest operations:
* trimming;
* sorting;
* brushing;
* washing;
* draining;
* packing.
These operations are intended to maintain commercial quality and prepare fruit for transport and sale.
70. TRANSPORT
Transport conditions should be selected according to:
* local market;
* regional market;
* international market;
* sea transport;
* air transport;
* fruit maturity;
* required shelf life.
The COLEAD guide treats these as separate marketing and logistics considerations.

	74. SYNTHESIS FOR DOCUMENTATION / CAPSTONE PAPER
Pineapple production requires suitable tropical environmental conditions, well-managed soil, quality vegetative planting materials, appropriate field preparation, and continuous management of water, nutrients, weeds, pests, and diseases. The production guides identify climate, water, soil, nutrition, plot preparation, planting material, planting density, irrigation, fertilization, weed management, flower induction, integrated pest management, harvesting, and postharvest handling as major components of a sustainable pineapple production system. Proper soil preparation and drainage are important for root development and disease prevention, while soil and leaf analysis can support more appropriate nutrient management (COLEAD, 2025; College of Micronesia-FSM, 2018).
Water management is particularly important in areas where rainfall is irregular or where pineapple is cultivated in upland and rolling terrain. A 2023 Philippine study demonstrated the use of micro-sprinkler irrigation, soil-moisture monitoring, and evapotranspiration measurements to manage organically grown pineapple in an upland production area. The study found that supplemental irrigation and regular crop maintenance could support improved growth and yield conditions, emphasizing the importance of matching water application with soil moisture and crop demand (Ines et al., 2023).
Pineapple production continues from vegetative establishment through vegetative growth, flower induction, fruit development, harvesting, and postharvest handling. Major production risks include nematodes, symphylids, mealybugs, scale insects, fruit borers, mites, fungal diseases, bacterial diseases, and viral diseases. Integrated pest management emphasizes identification, monitoring, prevention, action thresholds, and appropriate control rather than relying solely on chemical treatment (COLEAD, 2025).
Harvesting should be based on fruit maturity and the intended market. The Micronesian guide identifies flattened and glossy fruit eyes and progressive shell-color change as maturity indicators. Harvesting before full ripeness can increase storage life, while harvesting at a more advanced stage can provide better fresh-fruit quality for immediate consumption. Appropriate sorting, trimming, cleaning, packing, storage, and transportation are therefore necessary to maintain fruit quality after harvest (College of Micronesia-FSM, 2018; COLEAD, 2025).
75. COMBINED PARAGRAPH
Pineapple production requires appropriate tropical environmental conditions, suitable soil and drainage, quality planting materials, proper field preparation, and continuous crop management, including irrigation, fertilization, weed control, flower induction, and integrated pest and disease management (College of Micronesia-FSM, 2018; COLEAD, 2025). Sustainable production also requires attention to water availability and soil moisture, particularly in upland and rolling areas where rainfall may be irregular. In a 2023 Philippine study, supplemental micro-sprinkler irrigation, soil-moisture monitoring, evapotranspiration measurements, organic fertilization, and regular crop maintenance were used to improve water and crop management for organically grown pineapple (Ines et al., 2023). Pineapple production continues through flowering, fruit development, maturity determination, harvesting, grading, packing, storage, and marketing, with proper postharvest handling needed to maintain fruit quality and reduce deterioration (College of Micronesia-FSM, 2018; COLEAD, 2025).
76. PROPER IN-TEXT CITATIONS
General production
Pineapple production requires appropriate climate, soil, planting material, field preparation, water management, nutrition, and pest and disease management (College of Micronesia-FSM, 2018; COLEAD, 2025).
Water management
Appropriate irrigation and soil-moisture management can support pineapple production, particularly in upland areas where rainfall availability is irregular (Ines et al., 2023).
Soil and fertilizer
Pineapple production should incorporate soil assessment, nutrient diagnosis, organic soil improvement, and appropriate fertilizer application (COLEAD, 2025).
Pest and disease management
Integrated pest management for pineapple includes pest identification, monitoring, prevention, action thresholds, and appropriate control measures (COLEAD, 2025).
Harvesting
Pineapple maturity can be assessed using characteristics such as eye development and progressive fruit color change, while harvest timing should consider the intended market and postharvest requirements (College of Micronesia-FSM, 2018).

POMELO
PUMMELO PRODUCTION GUIDE — VERY DETAILED NOTES
Source basis: This guide synthesizes the two references you provided—Department of Agriculture–Regional Field Office No. 02 (DA-RFO 02), Pummelo Production Guide and Chen (2026), Pomelo Farming: Commercial Cultivation Practices—and adds 2021–2023 sources only for recent Philippine production context. The production practices below are kept source-based and are not supplemented with unsupported general recommendations.
Important citation note: Although the Scribd page you provided identifies the document as a 2025 upload, the actual DA-RFO 02 document states “First Edition, Copyright March 2012.” Therefore, for academic citation of the guide itself, 2012 is the more defensible publication year rather than 2025.
1. PUMMELO OVERVIEW
* Scientific name: Citrus maxima
* Common Philippine names include:
   * Pummelo
   * Pomelo
   * Suha
   * Lukban
* Pummelo is one of the important citrus fruit crops.
* The DA-RFO 02 guide describes pummelo as particularly suitable for the Cagayan Valley Region because of favorable land and climate conditions.
* The crop has economic potential because:
   * the fruit can be eaten fresh;
   * it can be processed;
   * it has nutritional value;
   * it has reported medicinal uses;
   * its relatively limited production area creates market demand.
* The tree can grow approximately 5–15 meters high.
* It has low-spreading branches and a canopy approximately 5–9 meters wide.
* Leaves are ovate to oblong.
* Flowers occur in axillary or terminal racemes.
* Fruit is yellowish-green and may be nearly round to pear-shaped.
* Flesh may be white, light pink, or red, depending on variety.
* The fruit normally matures approximately 5–6 months from flowering according to the DA guide.
Chen (2026) describes commercial pomelo production as a long-term orchard enterprise requiring coordinated propagation, orchard management, irrigation, fertilization, pest and disease management, harvesting, postharvest handling, and market planning.
2. IMPORTANCE AND USES
Pummelo can be used in several ways.
Fresh consumption
* The juicy pulp or vesicles are eaten fresh.
* The segments can be used in fruit salads.
* Juice can be extracted for beverages.
Processing
The DA guide identifies possible products such as:
* juices;
* candies;
* marmalades;
* food flavoring;
* food coloring;
* perfume-related products.
The white inner portion of the peel can be processed into candy after removing the outer peel containing oil glands.
Flowers and other plant parts
* Aromatic flowers may be used in perfume production.
* The wood may be used for tool handles.
* The DA guide also records traditional medicinal uses of leaves, flowers, fruits, and seeds for conditions such as cough, fever, and gastric disorders. These should be treated as traditional uses reported by the source, not as medical recommendations.
3. PLANT CHARACTERISTICS
The DA guide describes the pummelo as:
* a large citrus tree;
* approximately 5–15 m tall;
* low branching;
* approximately 5–9 m canopy spread;
* having ovate to oblong leaves;
* producing raceme-type flowers;
* producing yellowish-green fruit;
* producing fruit ranging from nearly round to pear-shaped;
* producing white, light-pink, or red pulp depending on variety;
* having relatively few to no seeds in some varieties.
4. SOIL REQUIREMENTS
Pummelo is relatively adaptable to different soil types, but the DA guide identifies the following requirements:
* soil should be reasonably deep;
* soil should be well drained;
* soil should be well aerated;
* soil should have high moisture-retention capacity;
* optimum soil pH is approximately 5.5–6.5.
If soil pH is below 5, liming is recommended by the guide to increase soil pH.
The following soils should be avoided:
* waterlogged soils;
* sticky soils;
* excessively heavy wet soils;
* soils underlain by hardpan.
Chen (2026) similarly emphasizes that commercial pomelo production requires suitable soil and consistent water management, particularly during flowering and fruit development.
5. CLIMATE REQUIREMENTS
The DA-RFO 02 guide identifies pummelo as a lowland tropical crop.
Recommended conditions include:
* elevation: up to approximately 400 meters above sea level;
* optimum temperature: approximately 23–30°C;
* annual rainfall: approximately 1,500–1,800 mm.
The 2021 cultivation reference also identifies commercial production at elevations not exceeding approximately 400 masl, with an optimum temperature of approximately 25–30°C and annual rainfall of 1,500–1,800 mm.
Chen (2026) further describes commercial pomelo production as suited to frost-free tropical or subtropical environments with warm, humid growing conditions and moderate dry periods before harvest.
6. RECENT PHILIPPINE PRODUCTION CONTEXT, 2021–2023
The Philippine Statistics Authority's Davao Region data provide useful recent production context.
Pomelo production in Davao Region was:
* 2021: 15,131.17 metric tons
* 2022: 15,241.37 metric tons
* 2023: 14,995.11 metric tons
Production therefore increased slightly in 2022 but declined by 1.6% in 2023. Pomelo accounted for approximately 0.4% of the region's total fruit-crop production in 2023.
The 2022 situationer reported that pomelo production increased by 0.7% in 2022 after a 1.0% increase in 2021.
These figures are useful for a Davao-focused capstone because they provide a recent regional production baseline.
7. RECENT 2023 ECONOMIC CONTEXT
The Philippine Statistics Authority's 2023 Census of Philippine Business and Industry reported that the growing of pomelo (suha) recorded a revenue-to-expense ratio of 2.79 among the agricultural industry subclasses covered in the census.
This means that, within the establishments represented by the census data, every peso of expense corresponded to approximately ₱2.79 in revenue.
This figure should be used as an industry-level statistical indicator, not as a guaranteed return for an individual farmer.
8. RECOMMENDED VARIETIES
The DA-RFO 02 guide identifies recommended pummelo varieties including:
* Siamese Abulug
* Amoy Mantan
* Magallanes
The guide particularly promotes Siamese Abulug, developed by the DA-Abulug Seed Farm and registered under:
NSIC 2008 Pm 04
The guide states that Siamese Abulug has a relatively long shelf life and is commercially available.
The guide reports the following information for selected varieties:
Magallanes
* Origin: Davao City
* Yield: 227 fruits/tree
* Fruit weight: approximately 859.03 g
* Edible portion: 44.20%
* Total soluble solids: 9.00%
* Flesh color: pink
Amoy Mantan
* Origin: Canton, China
* Yield: approximately 203 fruits/tree
Siamese Abulug
The guide describes it as:
* high yielding;
* approximately 300 fruits at 10 years old;
* medium fruit size of approximately 600–800 g;
* soft pulp;
* colorless to creamy-white pulp;
* greenish to light-yellow rind;
* sweet flavor of approximately 10.5° Brix;
* annual bearing habit.
The 2021 source also identifies Philippine varieties such as:
* Amoy Mantan;
* Magallanes;
* Panacan;
* Mintal;
* Aroman;
* Sunwui Luk;
* Siamese selections.
9. PROPAGATION
Pummelo can be propagated through:
* seeds;
* air layering or marcotting;
* budding;
* grafting;
* stem cuttings.
For commercial production, the 2026 commercial cultivation source emphasizes grafted plants because seed-grown trees take much longer to bear and may show variable fruit characteristics.
For Philippine production, shield budding using calamandarin rootstock is identified as a standard budding method in the 2021 reference.
10. PRODUCTION OF ROOTSTOCK SEEDLINGS
Select:
* fully developed seeds;
* seeds from mature or ripe fruits;
* seeds from healthy trees;
* seeds from fruits without abnormalities.
Do not use seeds from fruits that have fallen on the ground.
The seeds may be disinfected when necessary.
The seedbed should be isolated from existing citrus orchards to reduce possible contamination through insect vectors.
11. CARE OF ROOTSTOCK SEEDLINGS
During seedling establishment:
1. Water the seedbed to facilitate germination.
2. Once seeds begin germinating, expose seedlings to sunlight.
3. Avoid excessive watering.
4. Prevent poorly drained conditions.
5. Protect young seedlings from pests and diseases.
6. Use the minimum recommended chemical dosage when chemicals are required to prevent plant injury.
The guide explains that excessive moisture in poorly drained beds can predispose seedlings to damping-off disease.
12. POTTING OF SEEDLINGS
Seedlings are ready for potting approximately:
21–28 days after germination.
The guide specifies polyethylene bags approximately:
7 × 12 × 0.003
containing garden soil.
Important practices:
* avoid seedlings with deformed or goose-neck root systems;
* transfer carefully;
* water immediately after potting;
* maintain proper nursery management;
* control weeds.
13. BUDDING AND BUDDED SEEDLING MANAGEMENT
Rootstocks are ready for budding approximately:
6–8 months after potting.
Budding should be performed:
* approximately 6–8 inches above ground level.
After budding:
* do not fertilize newly budded plants until the bud eye shows growth;
* remove the bud wrap approximately 3 weeks after budding;
* lop or clip the top of the seedling approximately 2–3 inches above the bud to encourage bud-eye growth;
* once the bud eye begins to grow, decapitate the rootstock approximately 1–3 inches above the bud-eye union;
* carefully manage weeds while the scion is still succulent and tender.
14. LAND PREPARATION
Plain areas
The guide recommends:
* thorough plowing;
* harrowing.
Hilly areas
The guide recommends:
* clearing the land;
* removing stumps;
* preparing the area before planting.
A soil sample should be collected for analysis before orchard establishment.
The 2021 reference similarly recommends thorough plowing and harrowing on suitable terrain, while sloping land may require slashing vegetation and clearing around planting hills.
15. PLANTING DISTANCE
The DA guide gives several possible spacings:
Spacing
	Approximate plants/ha
	8 m × 8 m
	156
	8 m × 10 m
	125
	6 m × 8 m
	208
	10 m × 10 m
	100
	The guide states that orchard layout is commonly based on the square system, although other systems may be used depending on the site.
Possible layouts include:
* square;
* quincunx;
* triangular;
* contour.
Chen (2026) identifies approximately 6–8 m spacing as a common commercial spacing range and notes that higher-density systems require more intensive pruning and management.
16. PLANTING HOLES
Planting holes should be sufficiently large to accommodate the root system.
The DA guide indicates a desired hole volume of approximately:
40 cubic centimeters
as written in the source.
The holes should be exposed to:
* rain;
* sunlight;
for approximately 1–2 weeks before planting.
This allows organic material to accumulate and promotes aeration during plant establishment.
The 2021 reference gives a practical pit dimension of approximately 0.5 m deep and wide.
17. PLANTING PROCEDURE
1. Prepare the field and planting layout.
2. Prepare the planting holes.
3. Expose the holes before planting.
4. Plant at the onset of the rainy season.
5. Fill the hole with a soil-manure mixture.
6. Position the plant carefully.
7. Press the soil gently around the plant.
8. Ensure the plant is approximately at the same level as it was in the nursery.
9. Replace missing plants immediately to complete the orchard.
The guide's establishment practice emphasizes planting during the beginning of the rainy season.
18. FERTILIZATION DURING ESTABLISHMENT
The DA guide emphasizes that fertilizer requirements should be determined through:
soil analysis.
For young trees:
* organic fertilizer may be used;
* nitrogen fertilizer is included in the establishment schedule;
* fertilizer applications are divided over several applications;
* ring weeding should be performed before fertilizer application.
The guide also identifies liquid fertilizer as an optional input depending on the farmer's choice.
19. FERTILIZATION OF BEARING TREES
For bearing trees:
* fertilization supports regular bearing;
* fertilizer requirements depend on:
   * soil condition;
   * tree age;
   * variety;
   * stage of growth;
   * production condition.
The DA guide recommends soil analysis as the basis for fertilizer requirements.
Two major applications are described:
1. Start of the rainy season — to improve tree vigor before flowering.
2. Toward the end of the rainy season — second application.
Ring weeding should be completed before fertilizer application.
20. FERTILIZER REFERENCE FROM 2021 SOURCE
The 2021 pummelo production reference reports a Philippine fertilizer range for bearing trees of approximately:
* 5–20 kg organic fertilizer/tree/year
* 4–15 kg inorganic fertilizer/tree/year
depending on tree age.
Fertilizer is applied in holes approximately:
1–2 meters from the trunk.
It also reports foliar fertilizer application at approximately 20-day intervals from 40 to 140 days after fruit set.
21. IRRIGATION
Water management is essential during establishment and fruit production.
For newly planted trees:
* water immediately after planting;
* during the first year, water approximately once a week during sunny periods, according to the DA guide.
For bearing trees, irrigation becomes particularly important during:
* new flush development;
* flower-bud formation;
* fruit setting;
* rapid fruit-size increase.
The guide states that irrigation during flower-bud formation helps prevent flower abortion.
22. WATER MANAGEMENT CHECK
The DA guide provides a simple soil-moisture assessment:
* if water comes out when soil is squeezed, the soil contains too much water;
* if soil forms a ball, moisture is considered adequate;
* if soil crumbles, the soil is too dry.
This can be used as a simple field indicator for irrigation decisions.
23. COMMERCIAL IRRIGATION METHODS
Chen (2026) identifies:
* drip irrigation;
* micro-sprinklers;
* furrow or flood irrigation.
Drip irrigation delivers water directly to the root zone, while micro-sprinklers are also used in Southeast Asian orchards.
The DA guide also identifies:
* furrow;
* hose;
* sprinkler;
* drip;
using either pumpset or gravity systems.
24. WEED MANAGEMENT
Weeds compete with pummelo trees for:
* soil moisture;
* nutrients.
The DA guide recommends:
* regular weeding;
* ring weeding around the tree;
* brushing or clearing spaces between rows.
For the first year, ring weeding around the base should extend approximately:
1 meter from the tree.
Ring weeding is performed approximately every three months in the guide's management schedule.
Weeding should be done before fertilizer application.
The 2021 source also recommends using uprooted weeds as mulch around trees where appropriate.
25. PRUNING AND TREE TRAINING
For young trees:
* prune approximately 4–6 months after planting;
* top-prune approximately 30–40 cm above ground level;
* retain approximately three branches;
* distribute the selected branches in different horizontal directions.
This establishes the tree's basic structure.
The DA guide additionally recommends removing:
* water sprouts below the bud union;
* diseased twigs;
* dead branches;
* poorly positioned branches;
* long, weak branches;
* branches that receive insufficient sunlight.
Pruning wounds on bark and branches should be protected using water-repellent paint as specified by the guide.
26. PRUNING OF BEARING TREES
The best time for major pruning is:
after harvest.
Remove:
* diseased twigs;
* dead twigs;
* diseased branches;
* dead branches;
* diseased leaves;
* unproductive branches;
* branches receiving inadequate sunlight.
27. INTERCROPPING
Intercropping can maximize land use while young pummelo trees are still developing.
The DA guide recommends:
* early-maturing crops during the first year;
* crops should not be planted too close to pummelo trees;
* intercrops should be approximately 1–2 meters away from rows or planting hills.
The 2021 reference notes that banana can be used between rows to:
* utilize vacant land;
* provide temporary shade;
* provide wind protection;
* generate income during the juvenile stage;
* function as a cover crop.
28. MULCHING
Mulching can support:
* tree growth;
* fruit yield;
* soil moisture conservation.
The DA guide notes that profitability depends on the price and availability of mulch materials.
Precautions should be taken against:
* fire hazards;
* rodents.
29. WINDBREAKS
Strong winds can:
* lodge trees;
* break branches;
* interfere with tree development.
The DA guide identifies neem as a suitable windbreak and also notes its insect-repellent function.
30. CARE DURING BEARING
Once trees begin producing fruit, management should focus on:
* maintaining adequate nutrition;
* maintaining water availability;
* weed control;
* pruning;
* pest monitoring;
* disease monitoring;
* maintaining tree health;
* protecting developing fruit;
* maintaining postharvest quality.
The DA guide states that fertilization during the bearing stage is intended to maintain normal growth and regular bearing.
31. PEST MANAGEMENT PRINCIPLES
The DA guide emphasizes integrated approaches rather than relying exclusively on spraying.
Possible non-spray methods include:
* drenching;
* painting;
* coating;
* baiting;
* trapping.
Trapping can reduce pesticide use by concentrating a small amount of pesticide in a trapping device containing an attractant.
Chen (2026) similarly recommends integrated pest management combining:
* monitoring;
* biological control;
* sanitation;
* canopy management;
* targeted chemical control when necessary.
32. MAJOR INSECT PESTS
32.1 Rind borer
Scientific name:
Prays endolemma
Damage:
* attacks newly opened flowers;
* attacks young fruits;
* larvae bore into the fruit;
* larvae feed on rind tissue;
* immature fruits may drop.
Management:
* sanitation;
* collect and destroy infested fruits;
* use attractants;
* apply appropriate pre- and post-bloom insecticide sprays according to recommended dosage.
32.2 Scale insects
Example:
Pinnaspis sp.
Symptoms:
* yellowing leaves;
* twig dieback;
* mold development.
Management:
* prune infested twigs;
* burn removed material;
* use clean planting materials;
* apply appropriate oil-based materials or insecticides.
32.3 Whiteflies
Scientific name identified in the guide:
Aleurodicus disperses
Symptoms:
* yellowing leaves;
* premature leaf fall;
* sooty mold development.
Management:
* suitable insecticides;
* soap/detergent solutions for small areas;
* pruning;
* mulching to help reduce moisture loss associated with infestation.
32.4 Mites
The guide identifies:
Panonychus citri
Symptoms:
* scratch-like marks on upper leaf surfaces;
* pale or grayish leaf appearance;
* leaf drop;
* petiole may remain attached to the green fruit.
Management:
* appropriate acaricides;
* sulfur-based fungicides at recommended dosage.
32.5 Other pests
The 2021 reference additionally identifies:
* citrus leafminer;
* leaf-eating caterpillars;
* fruit-boring caterpillars;
* scales;
* red mites;
* fruit flies;
* nematodes;
* rats.
33. FRUIT BAGGING
Fruit bagging is identified as a management technique that can reduce serious damage from:
* insect pests;
* diseases.
It can therefore be incorporated into fruit protection during development.
34. MAJOR DISEASES
34.1 Citrus canker
The 2021 source identifies bacterial canker caused by:
Xanthomonas citri
Symptoms include:
* oily spots on leaves;
* oily spots on fruit;
* lesions later becoming brown;
* corky lesions.
Management described includes:
* removal/defoliation of affected material;
* burning severely infected plants to reduce disease spread.
The DA guide also recommends copper-based fungicide use, field sanitation, and disease-free planting material as part of management.
34.2 Foot rot / gummosis
Associated with:
Phytophthora sp.
Symptoms include:
* sap oozing from bark cracks;
* lesions around the trunk;
* chlorotic leaves;
* yellowing;
* root decay;
* eventual branch or tree death.
Management includes:
* resistant rootstocks;
* avoiding excessively deep planting;
* water management;
* good aeration around the crown;
* sanitation;
* appropriate copper-based treatment.
34.3 Pink disease
The DA guide identifies:
Pink disease — Corticium salmonicolor
Symptoms:
* infection of twigs and branches;
* bark cracking;
* gum secretion;
* pinkish fungal growth during humid or rainy periods;
* yellowing and wilting;
* death of infected portions.
Management includes:
* removing infected bark;
* pruning diseased portions;
* burning infected material;
* copper fungicide or lime-sulfur treatment;
* field sanitation.
34.4 Scab
The guide identifies:
Sphaceloma fawcetti
Symptoms begin as:
* small;
* pale-orange;
* circular spots;
on young fruits.
As the disease develops, lesions increase as fruits mature.
The guide recommends copper fungicide applications during:
* new flush development;
* flowering, particularly when approximately two-thirds of petals have fallen.
34.5 Root rot and brown rot
The 2021 reference identifies:
* root rot;
* trunk gummosis;
* brown rot of fruit;
as diseases associated with Phytophthora fungi.
34.6 Sooty mold
Sooty mold may affect:
* leaves;
* fruits;
* sometimes branches.
The 2021 source identifies Capnodium citri and Miliola citricola.
The source indicates that controlling insect pests helps prevent sooty mold.
35. VIRAL DISEASES AND DISEASE-FREE PLANTING MATERIALS
The DA guide emphasizes the importance of selecting healthy parent trees for propagation.
Trees should not be selected if they show:
* yellowing;
* leaf mottling;
* abnormal growth;
* other symptoms of disease.
Candidate parent source trees should be inspected and indexed for virus diseases.
For Citrus tristeza virus (CTV):
* scion samples are submitted for laboratory ELISA testing;
* a negative result allows the tree to be tagged as a certified parent source tree;
* a positive tree should be discarded;
* certified virus-free trees become sources of scions for mass propagation.
36. INDEXING
Indexing is described as a procedure for detecting infections that may exist in symptomless citrus plants.
It involves inoculating susceptible indicator plants that can show specific disease symptoms.
The purpose is to:
* detect virus or virus-like diseases;
* evaluate candidate mother trees;
* identify pathogens;
* maintain healthy propagation material.
37. HUANGLONGBING / CITRUS GREENING
The DA guide identifies:
Huanglongbing / Yellow Mottling or Greening
and associates it with:
Diaphorina citri
The guide recommends:
* disease-free planting material;
* removal/rouging of diseased plants;
* management of the insect vector;
* health indexing;
* biological control of the psyllid vector;
* insecticide management where appropriate.
Chen (2026) identifies Asian citrus psyllid and citrus greening as among the most important biological risks to commercial pomelo production.
38. HARVEST MATURITY
The DA guide gives specific minimum maturity indicators.
Harvest maturity includes:
* rind color changes from green to yellow;
* approximately 50% color change;
* juice content of approximately 50% of fruit weight;
* soluble solids of approximately 9%;
* titratable acidity of approximately 0.6%;
* soluble-solids-to-acid ratio of approximately 10:1.
The 2021 reference states that maturity occurs approximately:
140–160 days after fruit set.
The fruit's dull skin becomes brighter as the oil glands become more prominent and shiny, with the change beginning near the fruit tip and progressing toward the stalk.
The DA guide separately describes general fruit maturity as approximately 5–6 months from flowering.
39. HARVESTING
The DA guide states that fruits can be harvested by:
* pulling;
* clipping from the stem or branches.
The general harvesting technique described is:
twist → jerk → pull
The recommended harvesting period is approximately:
8:00 AM–3:00 PM
when sunlight is available, because this can reduce fruit injury and improve fruit quality.
For commercial production, Chen (2026) recommends clip-cut harvesting rather than pulling fruit, emphasizing protection of the stem end and reduction of mechanical damage.
40. POSTHARVEST HANDLING
After harvesting:
1. Bring fruits intended for distant transport to a shaded area.
2. Wash fruits thoroughly to remove dirt.
3. Dry the fruits using a clean cloth.
4. Handle fruit carefully.
5. Sort fruits according to quality.
6. Prepare suitable packaging.
7. Transport immediately after harvesting and packaging.
41. WAXING
Waxing is used to:
* minimize fruit shriveling;
* maintain rind gloss;
* preserve appearance for several days.
This is included in the DA guide's postharvest handling recommendations.
42. PACKAGING
The DA guide recommends:
* kaing;
* wooden containers;
* carton;
* plastic crates.
Containers should be lined with:
* newspaper;
* or another suitable protective material.
This helps prevent:
* abrasion;
* punctures.
Large and deep containers should be avoided because compression injuries can increase losses. Container size should depend on the transportation system.
43. TRANSPORT
Transportation should:
* occur immediately after harvest and packaging;
* minimize rough handling;
* protect fruit from compression and physical injury;
* use containers appropriate to the transportation method.
Chen (2026) further identifies cold-chain logistics as an important consideration for commercial pomelo production and reports 7–10°C and 85–90% relative humidity as optimal storage conditions in its commercial production discussion.
44. COMMERCIAL POSTHARVEST MANAGEMENT
Chen (2026) identifies the following commercial practices:
* field sorting;
* washing;
* waxing;
* individual wrapping for premium markets;
* size grading;
* cold storage;
* cold-chain transport.
The source gives typical commercial fruit grades based on size:
* large: 1–1.5 kg
* extra-large: 1.5–2 kg
* jumbo: 2 kg or more.
45. STORAGE
Chen (2026) reports:
* temperature: 7–10°C
* relative humidity: 85–90%
* potential storage life: approximately 4–8 weeks under suitable conditions;
* retail storage: approximately 2–4 weeks.
The source cautions that lower temperatures can cause chilling injury, including rind pitting and off-flavors.
46. YIELD AND PRODUCTION
The DA guide's ten-year financial analysis uses a one-hectare orchard with approximately 156 plants at 8 × 8 m.
The financial model assumes:
* fruiting generally begins around the 4th or 5th year;
* production increases from the fifth year;
* production increases by approximately 30% starting from the sixth year in the model.
The guide's production model gives:
Tree age
	Approx. production used in model
	Year 4
	1,560 kg/ha
	Year 5
	7,800 kg/ha
	Year 6
	15,600 kg/ha
	Year 7
	23,400 kg/ha
	Year 8
	31,200 kg/ha
	Year 9
	43,680 kg/ha
	Year 10
	46,800 kg/ha
	These are financial-model assumptions in the DA guide, not guaranteed yields.
Chen (2026) reports commercial yields varying widely by variety, tree age, and management, with mature orchards potentially reaching approximately 20–40 metric tons/ha under the conditions described by the source.
47. TEN-YEAR FINANCIAL MODEL
The DA guide uses a sample one-hectare farm with:
* approximately 156 plants;
* 8 × 8 m spacing;
* budded plant cost of ₱100 per plant in the original model;
* skilled labor cost of ₱200/day in the original model;
* fruiting beginning approximately in the fourth or fifth year.
The original guide's example revenue assumes a fruit price of approximately:
* ₱25/kg in some early production years;
* ₱30/kg in later years.
These are historical assumptions in the source and should not be used as current 2026 farmgate prices without verification.

The guide's orchard costs include:

* complete fertilizer;
* muriate of potash;

* fungicides.

* harvesting;
* hauling;
* sorting;
* packaging;
* orchard safeguarding.
The guide's financial tables provide historical unit prices and labor rates, which are useful for understanding the production-cost structure but should not be presented as current market prices.
49. MARKETING
The DA guide specifically identifies Siamese Abulug as a variety promoted through the DA's:
Agribusiness Marketing Assistance Section (AMAS) – One-Stop Agribusiness Center (OSAC).
The guide encourages prospective buyers and agricultural stakeholders to coordinate with the DA marketing office.
Chen (2026) adds that commercial pomelo profitability depends on:
* production scale;
* market access;
* fruit quality;
* variety;
* postharvest handling;
* packaging;
* transport;
* market segment.

* pummelo and avocado salad;

These demonstrate that pummelo can support value-added processing beyond fresh-fruit sales.

	56. SYNTHESIS FOR DOCUMENTATION / CAPSTONE PAPER
Pummelo (Citrus maxima) production requires suitable tropical environmental conditions, appropriate soil, healthy planting materials, proper orchard establishment, and continuous cultural management. The Department of Agriculture–Regional Field Office No. 02 identifies reasonably deep, well-drained, well-aerated soils with high moisture retention and an optimum pH of approximately 5.5–6.5 as suitable for pummelo. The crop is adapted to lowland tropical environments up to approximately 400 meters above sea level, with suitable temperatures of approximately 23–30°C and annual rainfall of 1,500–1,800 mm. Proper propagation, land preparation, planting distance, irrigation, fertilization, weed control, pruning, mulching, wind protection, and pest and disease management are therefore important throughout orchard establishment and maintenance (Department of Agriculture–Regional Field Office No. 02 [DA-RFO 02], 2012).
Pummelo production continues through vegetative growth, flowering, fruit development, maturity, harvesting, and postharvest handling. Young trees require adequate irrigation, fertilization, weed control, and pruning to establish productive structures. Bearing trees require continued nutrient and water management, while pests and diseases such as rind borers, scales, whiteflies, mites, citrus canker, Phytophthora diseases, and Huanglongbing must be monitored and managed. The use of healthy and disease-free planting materials is particularly important because citrus diseases may be transmitted through propagation materials and insect vectors (DA-RFO 02, 2012).
Harvesting should be based on maturity indicators rather than fruit size alone. The DA guide identifies approximately 50% yellow color change, approximately 50% juice content, 9% soluble solids, 0.6% titratable acidity, and a 10:1 soluble-solids-to-acid ratio as maturity indicators. The 2021 pummelo production reference further identifies approximately 140–160 days after fruit set as a maturity period and notes the change in rind appearance as the fruit ripens (Bareja, 2021; DA-RFO 02, 2012).
Recent Philippine data further demonstrate the continuing relevance of pummelo production. In the Davao Region, pomelo production was 15,131.17 metric tons in 2021, increased to 15,241.37 metric tons in 2022, and declined to 14,995.11 metric tons in 2023. In 2023, pomelo represented approximately 0.4% of the region's fruit-crop production. The Philippine Statistics Authority also reported a 2.79 revenue-to-expense ratio for establishments engaged in growing pomelo (suha) in its 2023 agricultural industry statistics (Philippine Statistics Authority [PSA], 2022, 2025).
For commercial production, pomelo cultivation also requires coordinated propagation, irrigation, fertilization, integrated pest management, harvesting, postharvest handling, and market planning. Chen (2026) identifies grafting as a preferred commercial propagation method, emphasizes consistent moisture during flowering and fruit development, and highlights the importance of citrus pest and disease management, particularly Asian citrus psyllid and Huanglongbing. Proper harvesting, grading, packaging, cold storage, and transport are also important for maintaining fruit quality and market value.
57. SHORT CAPSTONE VERSION
Pummelo (Citrus maxima) production requires suitable lowland tropical conditions, well-drained and well-aerated soil with an optimum pH of approximately 5.5–6.5, adequate water, quality planting materials, and proper orchard management. Production involves propagation, nursery management, land preparation, appropriate planting distance, irrigation, fertilization, weed control, pruning, intercropping, wind protection, and integrated pest and disease management. During fruit production, farmers must maintain adequate water and nutrients, protect fruits from pests and diseases, and determine maturity using fruit color, juice content, soluble solids, acidity, and the soluble-solids-to-acid ratio. Harvested fruits should be carefully washed, dried, graded, packaged, and transported to minimize physical damage and maintain quality (DA-RFO 02, 2012; Bareja, 2021; Chen, 2026).
