SQUASH
SQUASH PRODUCTION GUIDE 
1. SQUASH OVERVIEW
* Squash is a member of the cucurbit family.
* It is locally known as “Kalabasa” or “Calabaza.”
* The ATI-CAR guide describes squash as a monoecious annual crop, meaning male and female flowers occur on the same plant.
* Squash may have:
   * long-running stems; or
   * short, bushy stems.
* Stems may range from soft to hard and round to angular.
* Adventitious roots commonly develop at the nodes.
* Flowers are solitary and may range from lemon yellow to deep orange. (Agricultural Training Institute–Cordillera Administrative Region [ATI-CAR], 2022).
* The Bayer guide focuses specifically on summer squash, including straight-neck, crook-neck, and scalloped types. It identifies summer squash as a warm-season crop susceptible to frost damage. (Vegetables by Bayer, 2023).
2. TYPES AND GENERAL GROWING CHARACTERISTICS
The Bayer reference identifies several types of summer squash:
* Straight-neck squash, such as zucchini
* Crook-neck squash
* Scalloped squash, such as patty pan
Summer squash belongs to Cucurbita pepo. This species also includes some pumpkin types and certain winter squash types. (Vegetables by Bayer, 2023).
Summer squash differs from winter squash in harvesting:
* summer squash is normally harvested immature;
* the skin remains relatively thin and tender;
* seeds remain immature;
* winter squash is generally allowed to reach physiological maturity before harvest.
The summer squash production cycle can be relatively short, with the Bayer guide reporting approximately 55–60 days from planting seed to first harvest, depending on variety and production conditions. (Vegetables by Bayer, 2023).
3. CLIMATE REQUIREMENTS
Squash is a warm-season crop.
The Bayer guide indicates that:
* squash is susceptible to frost damage;
* planting should take place after the danger of early-season frost has passed;
* germination is affected strongly by temperature;
* optimal germination is generally around 21–35°C;
* the maximum germination temperature is approximately 38°C;
* germination is usually inhibited below approximately 15°C. (Vegetables by Bayer, 2023).
For seedling production:
* approximately 32°C is identified as an optimal germination temperature;
* seedlings can emerge approximately three days after planting under appropriate conditions. (Vegetables by Bayer, 2023).
4. SOIL REQUIREMENTS
The Bayer guide identifies an optimal soil pH of approximately:
6.0–6.8
for summer squash growth.
A soil pH below approximately 5.5 may cause manganese phytotoxicity. (Vegetables by Bayer, 2023).
For transplant production, the Bayer guide specifies a soilless planting medium with a pH of approximately:
5.5–6.8. (Vegetables by Bayer, 2023).
Important soil-management considerations
Fertilizer requirements should not be treated as identical for every farm because they vary according to:
* soil type;
* existing nutrient availability;
* production location;
* crop requirements.
The guide therefore emphasizes that fertilizer programs should be adapted to the actual growing conditions. (Vegetables by Bayer, 2023).
5. SEED PREPARATION
The ATI-CAR guide provides a specific seed-preparation process for squash:
1. Soak seeds in clean water for 24 hours.
2. Remove the seeds from the soaking water.
3. Wrap the seeds in a moist cloth.
4. Place the wrapped seeds in a dark place.
5. Allow them to incubate until the seed coat breaks.
6. The pre-germinated seeds can then be planted. (ATI-CAR, 2022).
This procedure is particularly useful for small-scale and urban/home-garden production.
6. DIRECT SEEDING
Squash can be established through direct seeding.
The ATI-CAR guide recommends:
* sowing 2–3 seeds per pot;
* planting approximately 2 cm deep;
* using a pot approximately 10 inches in diameter;
* using a pot approximately 15 inches high.
The guide notes that a larger pot is better for container production. (ATI-CAR, 2022).
The Bayer guide also recognizes direct seeding as an important commercial establishment method.
For bush-type squash:
* recommended planting depth is approximately 2.5–4 cm;
* direct seeding is commonly used because squash plants are sensitive to transplant shock. (Vegetables by Bayer, 2023).
7. TRANSPLANTING
Squash may also be established through nursery-grown seedlings.
The ATI-CAR guide recommends:
* sowing seeds in seedling trays or boxes;
* using a growing medium composed of:
   * coir dust or rice hull;
   * compost;
   * garden soil;
* mixing these components at a 1:1:1 ratio;
* watering the medium before sowing;
* watering regularly after sowing;
* transplanting approximately 2 weeks after emergence. (ATI-CAR, 2022).
The Bayer guide provides a commercial seedling system:
* use trays containing approximately 72–128 cells;
* cell diameters of approximately 1–1.5 inches;
* sow seed approximately ½ inch deep;
* use a soilless medium with pH 5.5–6.8;
* approximately 3.25 lb of seed can produce about 10,000 seedlings under the cited production system;
* transplant seedlings approximately 3–4 weeks old. (Vegetables by Bayer, 2023).
8. DIRECT SEEDING VS. TRANSPLANTING IN PRODUCTION
The Bayer source explains that both methods are used.
Direct seeding
Advantages within the source's discussion include:
* less transplant shock;
* commonly used in commercial production;
* can be performed directly into raised beds;
* can be combined with plastic mulch.
Transplanting
Transplanting is becoming more common where:
* growing seasons are short;
* plasticulture systems are used;
* growers want to establish nursery-grown plants.
However, squash remains sensitive to transplant shock. (Vegetables by Bayer, 2023).
9. PLANTING DEPTH
For bush-type summer squash:
2.5–4 cm
is the recommended planting depth identified by Bayer.
Shallower planting generally allows earlier emergence than deeper planting. (Vegetables by Bayer, 2023).
The ATI-CAR urban/home-garden guide specifies:
2 cm
for direct seeding into pots. (ATI-CAR, 2022).
10. PLANT SPACING
The Bayer guide states that spacing recommendations vary according to the production system and region.
For direct-seeded bush-type squash:
* row spacing: approximately 90–150 cm;
* plant-to-plant spacing: approximately 30–60 cm.
Higher plant densities may:
* increase fruit production per unit area;
* reduce individual fruit size;
* potentially increase the time required to reach harvest. (Vegetables by Bayer, 2023).
For transplants on raised beds with plastic mulch:
* row spacing: approximately 153–183 cm;
* within-row spacing: approximately 61–91 cm. (Vegetables by Bayer, 2023).
11. LAND AND BED PREPARATION
The Bayer source identifies raised beds as one of the production systems used for squash, particularly with plastic mulch.
Direct seeding into:
plastic-mulch-covered raised beds
is practiced by some growers. (Vegetables by Bayer, 2023).
The exact land-preparation sequence and dimensions are not specified in the two selected 2021–2023 sources, so no additional unsupported plowing or harrowing requirements are added here.
12. FERTILIZATION
The ATI-CAR guide provides a specific fertilizer program for non-organic urban/home-garden production.
At planting
Add:
1 tablespoon complete fertilizer (14-14-14)
to the planting hole.
After adding fertilizer:
* cover it with a handful of compost;
* then proceed with planting. (ATI-CAR, 2022).
Approximately one month after planting
When the vines are approximately 1 meter long:
* apply 1 tablespoon urea;
* bury the fertilizer in a hole approximately 1 inch deep;
* place the fertilizer approximately 3–4 inches away from the stem. (ATI-CAR, 2022).
Two weeks later
Apply:
1 tablespoon potash (0-0-60).
Depending on the crop stand, the urea and potash applications may be repeated after approximately 15 days. (ATI-CAR, 2022).
13. ORGANIC NUTRIENT MANAGEMENT
The ATI-CAR guide also describes organic nutrient management.
Before transplanting:
* place a handful of:
   * compost;
   * vermicast; or
   * manure
in the planting hole as basal fertilizer. (ATI-CAR, 2022).
Vegetative stage
To support plant vigor, weekly organic preparations may be applied through:
* spraying; or
* drenching.
The guide identifies:
* IMO;
* Vermitea;
* FAA;
* OHN;
* FPJ.
(ATI-CAR, 2022).
Reproductive stage
During flowering and fruit development, the guide identifies:
* FFJ;
* CalPhos;
* OHN
as preparations that may be used to support flower and fruit development. (ATI-CAR, 2022).
14. COMMERCIAL FERTILIZER MANAGEMENT
The Bayer guide emphasizes that commercial fertilizer recommendations vary according to:
* soil type;
* nutrient availability;
* production region;
* production system.
Nitrogen
Nitrogen should be split into applications.
The guide specifies:
* 30–60 lb/acre pre-plant;
* remaining nitrogen after planting;
* sidedressing may be used in non-mulched systems;
* drip irrigation/fertigation may be used in suitable systems. (Vegetables by Bayer, 2023).
Phosphorus
Phosphorus in the form of P₂O₅ may also be:
* applied pre-plant;
* split between pre-plant and sidedress;
* applied through fertigation after planting. (Vegetables by Bayer, 2023).
Potassium
Potassium applications are typically:
applied pre-plant. (Vegetables by Bayer, 2023).
15. WATER MANAGEMENT
The ATI-CAR guide recommends watering:
every other day
and watering should also be performed:
every time fertilizer is applied. (ATI-CAR, 2022).
The ATI-CAR guide specifically recommends stopping irrigation once fruits reach the:
mature green stage
to improve fruit quality. (ATI-CAR, 2022).
16. IRRIGATION MANAGEMENT
The Bayer guide provides additional information about the squash root zone.
A fully developed squash plant generally has a root zone occupying approximately:
18 inches / 45 cm of soil depth.
Irrigation should wet most of this zone while avoiding both:
* excessive soil moisture;
* water stress. (Vegetables by Bayer, 2023).
Irrigation amount and timing depend on:
* plant age;
* soil type;
* light;
* temperature;
* humidity;
* wind;
* rainfall.
Sandy soils require more frequent irrigation because they lose available moisture more rapidly between applications. (Vegetables by Bayer, 2023).
17. POLLINATION
Squash plants are monoecious:
* male and female flowers occur on the same plant.
Male flower
The male flower:
* does not have an ovary;
* usually occurs on a longer stem;
* contains the pollen-producing anther. (Vegetables by Bayer, 2023).
Female flower
The female flower:
* has an ovary at the base;
* usually occurs on a shorter stem;
* has an ovary that develops into the fruit. (Vegetables by Bayer, 2023).
18. NATURAL POLLINATION
Squash is generally self-fertile, but its pollen is sticky and is not effectively carried by air currents.
Therefore, insects are important for transferring pollen from male to female flowers.
The Bayer guide identifies:
* insects;
* especially honey bees
as important pollinators. (Vegetables by Bayer, 2023).
Poor pollination can result in:
* deformed fruit;
* fruit abortion;
* fruit shriveling;
* fruit falling from the plant. (Vegetables by Bayer, 2023).
19. HAND POLLINATION
The ATI-CAR guide recommends hand pollination for better fruit development.
Procedure:
1. Identify an open male flower.
2. Obtain pollen from the anther.
3. Identify an open female flower.
4. Transfer pollen to the stigma.
5. Perform pollination between approximately 6:00–8:00 AM when flowers are fully open.
6. One male flower can pollinate approximately 2–3 female flowers. (ATI-CAR, 2022).
This provides a useful management option when natural pollination is inadequate.
20. POLLINATOR MANAGEMENT
The Bayer guide notes that some commercial growers supplement naturally occurring bees by placing honey-bee hives around the production field.
The cited recommendation is approximately:
1–2 honey-bee hives per acre. (Vegetables by Bayer, 2023).
Most pollination occurs:
in the morning after flowers open.
Therefore, pesticide applications require special caution during the pollination period because some insecticides and fungicides can harm bees and other pollinators. The guide recommends avoiding the most harmful products during active pollination and applying products later in the day when flowers are closed and fewer bees are present. (Vegetables by Bayer, 2023).
21. THINNING
The ATI-CAR guide recommends removing:
* side vines before and near fully developed fruits;
* deformed fruits;
* damaged fruits.
These should be removed while the fruits are still young to reduce unnecessary nutrient competition. (ATI-CAR, 2022).
The guide recommends maintaining approximately:
1–2 well-developed fruits per main vine. (ATI-CAR, 2022).
22. TRELLISING
Squash normally can be allowed to:
creep along the field.
However, trellising can be used to:
* maximize available space;
* improve fruit development;
* support urban or backyard production.
Possible trellis materials include:
* sticks;
* bamboo;
* galvanized wire;
* plastic twine;
* fences.
(ATI-CAR, 2022).
23. PRUNING AND VINE MANAGEMENT
The ATI-CAR guide recommends pruning/training at approximately the:
5-leaf stage.
At this stage:
* cut the plant shoot;
* encourage branching;
* retain only 2 main vines where fruits will develop. (ATI-CAR, 2022).
Vine management is therefore intended to:
* control plant growth;
* concentrate fruit development;
* improve use of available space;
* facilitate crop management.
24. PEST MANAGEMENT
The ATI-CAR guide identifies the following prevalent insect pests:
* aphids
* squash beetle
It identifies the following common diseases:
* powdery mildew
* mosaic virus. (ATI-CAR, 2022).
25. ORGANIC/BOTANICAL PEST MANAGEMENT
For insect pests, the ATI-CAR guide identifies:
* OHN;
* hot pepper extract;
* lemongrass extract;
* ginger extract;
* tomato extract;
* other botanical pesticide preparations.
For powdery mildew, it identifies:
* OHN;
* onion extract;
* ipil-ipil extract;
* kamantigi extract;
* takip kuhol extract;
* other botanical fungicides.
(ATI-CAR, 2022).
For non-organic production, the guide states that appropriate chemical pesticides available in the market may be used. (ATI-CAR, 2022).
26. PYTHIUM WILT
The Bayer guide provides additional disease information and describes Pythium wilt.
Symptoms include:
* stunted plants;
* yellowing of older leaves;
* wilting of outer leaves;
* leaves becoming brown;
* leaf death;
* eventual plant collapse.
Root symptoms include:
* dark-yellow to brown discoloration of the taproot;
* reduced root system;
* necrosis of root tips and feeder roots.
An important characteristic described in the guide is that:
* the crown does not rot;
* affected plants do not break off at the soil line when pulled, unlike plants affected by Sclerotinia rot. (Vegetables by Bayer, 2023).
27. PEST AND DISEASE MANAGEMENT PRINCIPLE
The combined source information supports a management approach based on:
1. regular crop observation;
2. early identification of pests and disease symptoms;
3. maintaining appropriate soil moisture;
4. avoiding excessive irrigation;
5. maintaining plant vigor;
6. using appropriate organic/botanical methods where applicable;
7. using appropriate chemical pesticides where applicable;
8. protecting pollinators during flowering.
The Bayer guide emphasizes that production performance varies according to local soil, weather, and growing conditions, so production recommendations should be adapted to the specific farm or growing environment. (Vegetables by Bayer, 2023).
28. FRUIT DEVELOPMENT
Following successful pollination:
* the female flower's ovary develops into the fruit;
* inadequate pollination may cause fruit abortion;
* properly pollinated fruits continue developing toward harvest.
For summer squash, fruit can reach market maturity approximately:
3–8 days after pollination
depending on variety and environmental conditions. (Vegetables by Bayer, 2023).
29. HARVESTING — IMMATURE GREEN FRUIT
The ATI-CAR guide states that immature green fruits can be harvested approximately:
30–40 days from pollination.
The Bayer guide gives a shorter summer-squash market-maturity interval of approximately:
3–8 days after pollination.
These values apply to the different squash production contexts described by the respective sources, so they should not be treated as a single universal maturity period. (ATI-CAR, 2022; Vegetables by Bayer, 2023).
30. HARVESTING — MATURE FRUIT
The ATI-CAR guide identifies maturity indicators including:
* development of a powder-like coating;
* hardening of the rind;
* drying of the peduncle.
These indicate that the fruit has reached maturity for harvesting. (ATI-CAR, 2022).
31. HARVESTING PRACTICES
For summer squash, the Bayer guide recommends:
* harvesting as soon as fruit reaches the desired market size;
* ensuring seeds are immature;
* ensuring the rind remains tender;
* avoiding skin damage;
* harvesting frequently.
The source states that commercial plantings are most productive when harvested approximately:
every other day. (Vegetables by Bayer, 2023).
Squash is generally harvested by hand, with:
* sorting;
* grading;
* packing
often performed in the field. (Vegetables by Bayer, 2023).
32. IMPORTANCE OF HARVEST TIMING
Leaving fruit on the vine too long can negatively affect subsequent production.
The Bayer guide explains that oversized fruit can act as:
nutrient sinks
and consequently:
* draw nutrients away from developing fruit;
* suppress additional fruit set;
* reduce overall productivity.
Therefore, regular harvesting is important for maintaining continuous production. (Vegetables by Bayer, 2023).
33. FRUIT SIZE AND MARKET REQUIREMENTS
The Bayer guide explains that preferred harvest size depends on the target market.
For the markets discussed in the source:
* straight-neck/zucchini:
   * approximately 12–15 cm long and 32–50 mm diameter for western markets;
   * approximately 18–21 cm long for eastern markets;
* crook-neck:
   * approximately 32–50 mm diameter;
* scalloped squash:
   * approximately 7–10 cm diameter.
These measurements are market-specific examples from the Bayer guide and should not automatically be treated as Philippine market standards. (Vegetables by Bayer, 2023).
34. POSTHARVEST HANDLING
Squash should be handled carefully after harvesting.
Avoid:
* bruising;
* cuts;
* abrasions;
* unnecessary mechanical damage.
Wounds can become infection sites for pathogens and may:
* increase postharvest rotting;
* reduce shelf life;
* reduce marketability. (Vegetables by Bayer, 2023).
35. COOLING
The Bayer guide recommends cooling fruit promptly after harvest to approximately:
10°C (50°F).
Delays in cooling may reduce:
* shelf life;
* fruit quality.
However, squash is susceptible to chilling injury at temperatures below:
5°C (41°F). (Vegetables by Bayer, 2023).
36. STORAGE CONDITIONS
The Bayer guide specifies:
* storage temperature: 5–10°C;
* relative humidity: approximately 95%.
Storage below approximately 5°C may cause:
* surface pitting;
* discoloration;
* faster decay.
Some varieties are more genetically susceptible to:
* chilling injury;
* shriveling.
(Vegetables by Bayer, 2023).
37. STORAGE LIFE
Under the storage conditions described by Bayer, squash may have approximately:
7–14 days of shelf life
when stored at approximately 5°C. (Vegetables by Bayer, 2023).
38. ETHYLENE AND STORAGE
The Bayer guide notes that:
* low oxygen conditions can reduce shelf life;
* exposure to ethylene can reduce shelf life.
Therefore, squash should not be stored or transported together with crops that produce ethylene. (Vegetables by Bayer, 2023).
39. COMPLETE SQUASH PRODUCTION CYCLE

 ↓

 ↓

 ↓

 ↓

 ↓

 ↓

 ↓

 ↓

 ↓

 ↓

 ↓

 ↓

42. SYNTHESIS FOR DOCUMENTATION / CAPSTONE PAPER
Squash production requires suitable warm-season growing conditions, appropriate soil pH, quality seed preparation, proper crop establishment, adequate nutrient and water management, pollination, vine management, pest and disease monitoring, and timely harvesting. Squash is a monoecious annual crop in which male and female flowers occur on the same plant. For summer squash, the optimal soil pH is approximately 6.0–6.8, while germination generally performs well between 21–35°C. Squash can be established through direct seeding or transplanting, although direct seeding is commonly used because the crop is sensitive to transplant shock. Proper planting depth, spacing, basal fertilization, irrigation, and nutrient management are important for establishing healthy plants (Agricultural Training Institute–Cordillera Administrative Region [ATI-CAR], 2022; Vegetables by Bayer, 2023).
Crop management continues through vegetative and reproductive development. Regular irrigation should maintain adequate soil moisture without creating excessive wetness, while fertilizer application should be adjusted according to crop and soil conditions. The ATI-CAR guide provides specific applications of complete fertilizer, urea, and potash and also identifies organic inputs such as compost, vermicast, manure, and several organic preparations. Squash flowers depend strongly on insect pollination, particularly honey bees, because the sticky pollen is not effectively carried by air. Hand pollination between approximately 6:00 and 8:00 AM can be used to improve fruit development, while thinning deformed or damaged fruits and maintaining approximately one to two well-developed fruits per main vine can reduce nutrient competition (ATI-CAR, 2022; Vegetables by Bayer, 2023).
Pest and disease management is another important component of squash production. The ATI-CAR guide identifies aphids and squash beetles as prevalent insect pests and powdery mildew and mosaic virus as common diseases. It provides botanical and organic management options, while also recognizing appropriate chemical pesticides for non-organic production. The Bayer guide additionally describes Pythium wilt and emphasizes the importance of balancing irrigation to avoid both water stress and excessive soil moisture. Proper management during flowering is also necessary to protect pollinators from harmful pesticide exposure (ATI-CAR, 2022; Vegetables by Bayer, 2023).
Harvesting should be based on the intended market and the maturity stage appropriate to the squash type. The ATI-CAR guide identifies immature green fruit harvesting approximately 30–40 days from pollination and mature fruit indicators such as powder-like coating, hardened rind, and drying of the peduncle. For summer squash, the Bayer guide indicates that fruit may reach market maturity three to eight days after pollination and recommends frequent harvesting to prevent oversized fruit from becoming nutrient sinks that reduce the development of other fruits. Careful postharvest handling is necessary to prevent bruising and pathogen infection, while storage at approximately 5–10°C and 95% relative humidity can help maintain quality; temperatures below 5°C may cause chilling injury (ATI-CAR, 2022; Vegetables by Bayer, 2023).
43. GENERAL PRODUCTION SYNTHESIS
Overall, squash production can be understood as a continuous management process beginning with seed preparation and site suitability, followed by plant establishment, fertilization, irrigation, vine management, pollination, fruit thinning, pest and disease management, harvesting, and postharvest handling. The two references provide complementary information for documenting squash cultivation: the ATI-CAR guide provides practical procedures for small-scale and home/urban production, while the Bayer guide provides additional technical information on commercial establishment, soil pH, germination conditions, spacing, irrigation, pollination, harvesting, and storage. Together, these source-supported practices provide appropriate production parameters that can be incorporated into the agricultural simulation and documentation of AgriDabaw-3D (ATI-CAR, 2022; Vegetables by Bayer, 2023).
